ACA Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Tidal volume stays at the target while inspiratory pressure automatically falls as compliance improves): PRVC adjusts inspiratory pressure breath to breath to deliver the set tidal volume with a decelerating flow pattern. As compliance improves the pressure needed falls and volume stays at target. In plain pressure control the pressure is fixed, so tidal volume rises and may cause volutrauma. Pressure cannot remain constant while volume is guaranteed once compliance changes.
  2. The correct answer is A (Modified WHO class IV, with very high maternal mortality, so counseling should offer termination.): Pulmonary arterial hypertension, including Eisenmenger syndrome, is class IV, in which pregnancy is contraindicated and maternal mortality approaches 30 to 50 percent. Termination should be offered, and if she continues, care requires an expert multidisciplinary team. Classes I through III apply to lesions with minimal to moderate risk and do not describe this physiology.
  3. The correct answer is B (Repeat coagulation tests using tubes with citrate adjusted for the high hematocrit before treating prolonged times.): At hematocrit above about 55 percent the plasma volume in a standard citrate tube is reduced, so the fixed anticoagulant excess falsely prolongs PT and aPTT. Retesting with adjusted citrate avoids needless transfusion, which carries risk of volume load and alloimmunization. Platelet counts near 100 x10^9/L are expected in cyanotic disease and do not need prophylactic correction without bleeding.
  4. The correct answer is C (Use a standardized tool once stable, covering infusions, lines, bleeding and expected problems, inviting questions): Structured handoffs delivered at a calm point, with defined content and an opportunity for questions, reduce omissions in high-risk transfers. Infusion doses, lines, bleeding trends and anticipated issues are the details that the receiving team needs at once. A verbal summary during transport competes with monitor connection, and relying on the written record alone loses interactive verification.
  5. The correct answer is C (Diastolic pressure steps up from near 4 to about 10 mmHg and a dicrotic notch appears.): On entering the pulmonary artery the systolic pressure remains similar, but the diastolic pressure rises because the pulmonic valve closes and the arterial wall recoils, creating a dicrotic notch. A drop to about 4 mmHg with a and v waves marks the right atrium, and low diastolic pressure with a steep upstroke describes the right ventricle.
  6. The correct answer is A (Mann-Whitney U test, which compares rank distributions without assuming normally distributed creatinine values): The groups are independent and the outcome is skewed with outliers, so a rank-based test is the appropriate choice and the Mann-Whitney U test fits. A paired t-test requires each observation in one arm to be linked to one in the other, which randomized unrelated patients are not. The independent t-test compares means and assumes approximate normality, which the skewed creatinine data violate.
  7. The correct answer is B (Resume cardiopulmonary bypass after reinstituting full heparin, while continuing epinephrine and supportive therapy.): Refractory cardiovascular collapse from a protamine reaction should be rescued by returning to bypass, which supports perfusion while the reaction is treated. This requires reheparinization to full dose first so the circuit does not clot. Continuing protamine or waiting for a fluid response delays rescue, and antihistamines alone do not reverse severe shock.
  8. The correct answer is A (Gap junctions made of connexin 43 at intercalated discs, giving low-resistance ionic coupling.): Connexin 43 forms gap junction channels in the intercalated discs, which allow ions to pass between neighboring myocytes and make the ventricle behave as a functional syncytium. Ischemia causes gap junction uncoupling and remodeling, slowing conduction and promoting reentry. Desmosomes provide mechanical adhesion and carry no ionic current. T-tubule dyads couple excitation to contraction inside a single cell.
  9. The correct answer is B (A score of 5, intermediate probability; stop heparin and send PF4 antibody testing.): A fall of about 71 percent with a nadir of 70 scores 2 for thrombocytopenia, onset on day 7 scores 2 for timing, no thrombosis scores 0, and possible sepsis as another cause scores 1, for a total of 5. A score of 4 to 5 is intermediate, which calls for stopping heparin and sending the immunoassay. A score of 3 or 7 misreads the components.
  10. The correct answer is C (Altered thoracic fluid and electrode field geometry distort the electrical signal, making absolute values unreliable.): Thoracic bioimpedance and bioreactance infer stroke volume from changes in electrical signals across the chest, so pleural fluid, an open chest, electrocautery and electrode placement alter the signal. Lithium dilution is a separate technology, and these devices do not rely on the arterial waveform contour, so those distractors describe other monitors.
  11. The correct answer is A (54 mmHg, found by subtracting aortic peak pressure from left ventricular peak pressure): The peak-to-peak gradient is the difference between maximum left ventricular and maximum aortic pressures: 188 minus 134 equals 54 mmHg. Subtracting end-diastolic pressure gives 170, which is not a transvalvular gradient. The end-diastolic pressure of 18 mmHg reflects ventricular filling, not the valve.
  12. The correct answer is A (Valve oversized relative to the sinotubular junction, causing leaflet distortion and central malcoaptation): Stentless valves rely on matching the valve diameter to the sinotubular junction, and oversizing distorts the commissures so leaflets fail to coapt centrally. Stentless prostheses have no struts, so strut impingement cannot occur. Mismatch produces a high gradient, not regurgitation, and the gradient here is low.
  13. The correct answer is B (Maintain pressure near 10 mmHg, draining no more than about 15 to 20 mL per hour): Spinal cord perfusion pressure equals mean arterial pressure minus the greater of CSF or venous pressure, so draining to about 10 mmHg raises it. Drainage is limited to roughly 15 to 20 mL per hour because rapid or excessive drainage can cause intracranial subdural hematoma. A target of 20 mmHg gives little protective benefit.
  14. The correct answer is A (A giant v wave with high right atrial and normal wedge pressures indicates isolated tricuspid regurgitation): Severe tricuspid regurgitation fills the right atrium during systole, producing a giant v wave that obliterates the x descent and raises mean right atrial pressure. The normal wedge of 12 mmHg excludes left heart failure as the cause. Cannon a waves reflect atrial contraction against a closed valve and do not explain this tracing.
  15. The correct answer is C (Stop ticagrelor, begin cangrelor 0.75 mcg/kg/min, and discontinue it one to six hours before incision.): Early stent thrombosis is a risk when P2Y12 inhibition is stopped within the first months. Cangrelor is an intravenous reversible P2Y12 inhibitor with a half-life of minutes, so it bridges the interval and is stopped one to six hours before incision. Heparin does not inhibit platelets and does not prevent stent thrombosis. Clopidogrel loading adds irreversible inhibition that would increase surgical bleeding.
  16. The correct answer is A (Redose every 15 to 20 minutes, adding retrograde delivery because hypertrophied muscle is poorly perfused antegrade.): Returning electrical activity signals rewarming and washout of the arresting solution, and thick myocardium has greater oxygen demand and uneven distribution. Frequent redosing with a retrograde component reaches the subendocardium that antegrade flow underperfuses. Longer intervals risk ischemia, and the larger muscle mass needs more, not less, solution.
  17. The correct answer is C (Inhalational induction is slowed because shunted blood bypasses alveolar uptake, while intravenous induction is faster.): In a right-to-left shunt, a fraction of venous blood bypasses the alveoli, so arterial partial pressure of inhaled agent rises more slowly and induction is delayed, especially for poorly soluble agents. Intravenous drugs avoid first-pass pulmonary uptake and reach the systemic circulation sooner, so onset is faster. Both reversed claims fail on those mechanisms.
  18. The correct answer is A (Vasoplegic syndrome, with high cardiac index, low resistance, and hypotension despite adequate filling): Vasoplegia after bypass is hypotension with a normal or high cardiac index and low SVR, typically below 800 dyn s/cm5, despite adequate filling. Hypovolemic and cardiogenic shock both lower cardiac index and raise resistance. The numbers here, index 3.8 and SVR 600, fit only vasoplegia.
  19. The correct answer is B (Higher end-systolic volume, lower stroke volume, and a taller, narrower loop shifted toward higher volumes.): Increased afterload raises the pressure at which the aortic valve opens and the ventricle ejects against the unchanged end-systolic pressure-volume line, so end-systolic volume rises. With constant filling, stroke volume falls and the loop becomes taller and narrower. A loop that widens leftward would indicate lower afterload or higher contractility. Preload is stated as unchanged, so end-diastolic volume is not the primary change.
  20. The correct answer is C (A percutaneous microaxial pump drawing from the left ventricle into the ascending aorta): A transvalvular microaxial pump removes blood from the ventricle, lowers wall stress and adds antegrade flow. A balloon pump gives only modest unloading and often is not sufficient on its own. Septostomy decompresses the left atrium and so lowers pulmonary pressure, but it does not drain the ventricle or add aortic flow.
  21. The correct answer is A (Cool to about 18 to 20 degrees Celsius for intermittent circulatory arrest, anticipating reperfusion pulmonary edema.): Bronchial collateral flow floods the pulmonary arteries and obscures the dissection plane, so deep hypothermia with circulatory arrest periods of about twenty minutes is used. Reperfusion injury with edema and hemorrhage in the endarterectomized lung is a known complication that affects postoperative ventilation. Mild hypothermia with continuous flow gives a poor field.
  22. The correct answer is B (Cardiac tamponade from clotted retained blood, requiring urgent surgical re-exploration of the chest): Sudden cessation of drainage with tachycardia, hypotension, rising CVP and equalized diastolic pressures indicates tamponade from clot compressing the heart despite apparently improved bleeding. Treatment is surgical evacuation, not volume or drugs. Protamine reaction would cause pulmonary hypertension with a high PA systolic pressure and would not coincide with abrupt drain cessation. Hypovolemia lowers CVP and PA pressures rather than raising and equalizing them.
  23. The correct answer is B (Benzodiazepine plus a vasodilator such as nicardipine, avoiding an isolated pure beta blocker bolus): Cocaine causes intense sympathetic stimulation, so a benzodiazepine reduces central drive and a vasodilator controls pressure. Isolated beta blockade may leave unopposed alpha stimulation, worsening hypertension and coronary vasospasm. Hydralazine produces reflex tachycardia and raises the rate of pressure rise, which is undesirable in dissection.
  24. The correct answer is A (Euglycemic ketoacidosis from the SGLT2 inhibitor, treated with insulin infusion plus dextrose containing fluids): SGLT2 inhibitors promote ketogenesis and urinary glucose loss, so ketoacidosis can occur with near normal glucose in the perioperative stress state. These drugs should be held about 3 days before surgery. Treatment requires insulin to stop ketone production with dextrose to prevent hypoglycemia. A high anion gap argues against hyperchloremic acidosis.
  25. The correct answer is B (It remains prognostic, but renal dysfunction raises baseline values, so it needs higher thresholds): BNP rises with wall stress and also with reduced renal clearance, age, and atrial fibrillation, so values in chronic kidney disease run higher. Even so, elevated BNP predicts postoperative events independently. BNP is cleared by receptors and enzymes as well as the kidney, and a clinically compensated patient does not need cancellation for this value alone.
  26. The correct answer is B (Mitral valve repair now, since an ejection fraction of 60 percent or less indicates early dysfunction): In chronic severe primary mitral regurgitation, surgery is indicated in asymptomatic patients with an ejection fraction of 60 percent or less or an end systolic dimension of 40 mm or more, because ventricular damage is already starting. Waiting for an ejection fraction near 30 percent leaves irreversible dysfunction. Vasodilators have no proven role in delaying surgery for primary regurgitation, and atrial fibrillation as a trigger applies when the ventricle remains normal.
  27. The correct answer is A (Use epicardial ventricular wires placed at surgery rather than a transvenous lead crossing the repaired valve): A transvenous lead passing through a freshly repaired tricuspid valve can entangle the ring or tear leaflets, impair coaptation and cause regurgitation, so epicardial wires placed under direct vision are preferred. Blind passage of a balloon-tipped lead does not protect the ring. Drug support with isoproterenol is unreliable for complete heart block and adds arrhythmia risk.
  28. The correct answer is A (Maintain perfusion pressure and brisk urine flow with mannitol and furosemide to limit hemoglobin tubular obstruction.): Free hemoglobin overwhelms haptoglobin, filters into tubules, and causes cast formation and oxidative injury. Maintaining pressure and urine flow with an osmotic diuretic and a loop diuretic is the standard supportive approach, with alkalinization sometimes added. Fluid restriction would concentrate pigment, and low-dose dopamine has not been shown to protect the kidney.
  29. The correct answer is B (Asynchronous pacing at a fixed, manufacturer-specific magnet rate that ignores the intrinsic rhythm.): Most pacemakers respond to a magnet with asynchronous pacing at a fixed rate that depends on the manufacturer and battery status, and the pacing rate may exceed the intrinsic rate. An ICD magnet only suspends tachy therapies and does not change pacing. The magnet does not leave sensing active or force a lower rate.
  30. The correct answer is B (Synchronized cardioversion, since hypotension from the new rhythm marks hemodynamic instability.): New atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response and hypotension is unstable and should be treated with synchronized electrical cardioversion. Rate control with a beta blocker may worsen hypotension, and amiodarone is slower and suited to stable patients.
  31. The correct answer is C (Return to bypass for re-repair or replacement, since structural regurgitation will not respond to loading changes): The exhibit shows persistent prolapse, a vena contracta of 0.8 cm, a jet reaching the posterior left atrial wall and pulmonary vein systolic flow reversal, despite a normal mean arterial pressure and heart rate. These findings indicate severe residual regurgitation that is structural rather than load dependent. Leaving it risks heart failure and early reoperation. Phenylephrine only changes loading conditions without correcting the defect, and reverse remodeling does not repair a structural lesion.
  32. The correct answer is B (Extent II, which runs from the left subclavian artery to the aortic bifurcation): Extent II runs from the left subclavian artery to the aortic bifurcation and carries the highest risk of spinal cord ischemia and renal injury. Extent I ends above the renal arteries. Extent III begins at the sixth intercostal space and extends below the renal arteries, so it is shorter proximally than the aneurysm described.
  33. The correct answer is C (Gradient exceeded 10 degrees C at 20 and 30 minutes, so cooling should have been slower): The tracing shows an arterial outlet temperature 12 degrees C below nasopharyngeal at 20 minutes (16 versus 28) and 11 degrees C below at 30 minutes (11 versus 22). Cooling is normally kept to a gradient of about 10 degrees C or less to avoid gas bubble formation and uneven brain cooling. A steady fall in nasopharyngeal temperature does not excuse an excessive gradient. Cooling over 30 to 40 minutes is typical, so reaching target in 10 minutes is neither expected nor safe.
  34. The correct answer is B (Tachyarrhythmia detection and shocks are suspended, while bradycardia pacing continues at the programmed settings): A magnet over most ICDs inhibits tachyarrhythmia detection and therapy, but unlike a pacemaker it does not change bradycardia pacing, so a pacing-dependent patient will not be placed in asynchronous mode. If asynchronous pacing is required, the device must be reprogrammed. Shock therapy is the function that the magnet removes, so the idea that shocks remain active is incorrect.
  35. The correct answer is A (Raise the heart rate to 90 to 100 and lower systemic vascular resistance with a vasodilator.): In acute mitral regurgitation, a modestly faster heart rate shortens the time for regurgitation and maintains forward cardiac output because stroke volume is limited. Reducing afterload favors forward flow across the aortic valve rather than backward through the leaking mitral valve. Bradycardia and elevated resistance both increase the regurgitant volume and worsen pulmonary edema.
  36. The correct answer is C (Junctional rhythm, with atrial contraction against a closed tricuspid valve producing cannon a waves.): Cannon a waves appear when the atrium contracts while the tricuspid valve is closed, as in junctional rhythm, ventricular rhythms or AV dissociation. The regular narrow QRS without preceding P waves points to a junctional focus. Atrial fibrillation abolishes the a wave entirely, and tricuspid regurgitation produces a systolic wave instead of an atrial one.
  37. The correct answer is C (Stratify quality metrics by language and insurance, then add interpreter access and standardized family meetings): Disparities that persist after severity adjustment suggest process and communication gaps. Stratifying metrics makes them visible, and standardized interpreter use and family meetings address likely mechanisms. Assuming patient preference without evidence excuses a possible inequity, and removing the fields hides the problem instead of fixing it.
  38. The correct answer is B (Demand ischemia from surgical stimulation, treated by deepening opioid anesthesia, controlling rate and pressure, and nitroglycerin.): Sternotomy and spreading are intense stimuli that raise heart rate and pressure and increase oxygen demand in a heart with fixed critical stenoses. The response is to deepen anesthesia and analgesia and lower demand, adding nitroglycerin or a beta blocker if necessary. Tachycardia and hypertension argue against hypovolemia, and no cardioplegia has been given before bypass.
  39. The correct answer is C (The right coronary artery, including its posterior descending branch supplying the inferior wall and septum): The inferior wall and the inferior third of the septum are supplied by the posterior descending artery, which arises from the right coronary in a right dominant system. Anterior wall and anterior septal changes would implicate the left anterior descending and its diagonals. Isolated lateral wall changes would point to the circumflex.
  40. The correct answer is A (The surgeon removes the thrombus at coring and inspects the cavity; prosthesis motion is checked.): A left ventricular thrombus is a surgical concern but not a contraindication, since the apex is cored and the cavity is cleared and inspected during implantation. TEE findings of a thrombus and mechanical valve guide the surgeon to look for residual clot and check leaflet motion. Thrombolysis during an operation would greatly raise bleeding risk.
  41. The correct answer is A (Inflammatory nitric oxide-mediated vasodilation, treated with vasopressin and methylene blue when catecholamines are inadequate.): A high cardiac index with very low systemic resistance and a high venous saturation indicates vasodilatory shock, and in endocarditis surgery sepsis and bypass inflammation drive nitric oxide release. Vasopressin and methylene blue restore vascular tone when catecholamines fail. A high cardiac index argues against stunned myocardium, and capillary leak would produce low filling pressures.
  42. The correct answer is B (About 15 mmHg, reflecting high systemic venous pressure behind the stenotic tricuspid inflow): An inferior vena cava larger than 2.1 cm that collapses less than 50% with a sniff estimates right atrial pressure at about 15 mmHg. In tricuspid stenosis, obstruction at the valve elevates right atrial and systemic venous pressure, with hepatic congestion. Intermediate pressure requires a smaller vein or partial collapse, and a normal variant would collapse briskly.
  43. The correct answer is A (A partial occlusion clamp and arterial cannulation placed directly through the diseased ascending segment): Manipulating a heavily diseased ascending aorta with a clamp or a cannula jet can release atheroma and cause embolic stroke. Axillary inflow, avoiding clamps, and scanning to find a plaque free segment are standard risk reduction measures. Epiaortic ultrasound is more sensitive than palpation or TEE for identifying ascending plaque.
  44. The correct answer is C (Accept systolic pressure near 80 to 90 mmHg with large-bore access, inducing after prepping and draping): In contained rupture, permissive hypotension limits further bleeding by avoiding clot disruption. Large-bore access and blood products are prepared. Induction removes sympathetic tone and can collapse pressure, so it is done after prepping and draping so the surgeon can control the aorta quickly. Large crystalloid volumes dilute clotting factors and raise pressure.
  45. The correct answer is C (Confirm purge flow, battery charge, and that the catheter depth mark at the skin is unchanged): The purge fluid prevents blood from entering the motor, and a charged battery and a verified insertion depth guard against both pump failure and migration. Setting the level to P-0 allows backflow of aortic blood into the left ventricle, which is harmful. Pulling the catheter back deliberately risks the inlet leaving the ventricle altogether.
  46. The correct answer is B (Titrate heparin to the anti-Xa level, since liver-related factor deficiency prolongs the aPTT independent of heparin): Impaired synthesis of clotting factors lengthens the aPTT even without much heparin effect, so the two tests disagree. The anti-Xa assay measures heparin activity directly and is the better guide here. Acting on the aPTT would reduce anticoagulation in a circuit already below its heparin target and invite thrombosis.
  47. The correct answer is C (25 patients treated to prevent one additional case of acute kidney injury): Absolute risk reduction is 12 percent minus 8 percent, or 4 percent, which is 0.04. The number needed to treat is 1 divided by 0.04, which equals 25. The value of 4 reports the percentage points of risk difference rather than its reciprocal, and 3 comes from inverting the relative risk reduction of 33 percent.
  48. The correct answer is B (They help in small studies or animal models but lack confirmation in large human trials): Cerebrospinal fluid drainage, distal perfusion, and pressure management carry the strongest evidence for spinal cord protection. Naloxone, steroids and magnesium have shown promise in animal work or small series but have not been confirmed consistently in large human trials. No trial supports replacing drainage with magnesium, and steroid benefit is unproven.
  49. The correct answer is B (Elevated left ventricular filling pressure consistent with heart failure with preserved ejection fraction): An average E/e prime above 14, a left atrial volume index above 34 mL/m2 and a TR velocity above 2.8 m/s each indicate raised filling pressure, which supports diastolic heart failure. A preserved ejection fraction does not exclude diastolic dysfunction. Mitral stenosis would show a prolonged pressure half-time and thickened leaflets, not a high E/e prime.
  50. The correct answer is C (A composite of quality, cost, improvement and interoperability scores that adjusts later Medicare payments): MIPS combines performance categories, namely quality, cost, improvement activities and promoting interoperability, into a score that produces a positive, neutral or negative adjustment to Medicare Part B payments. It does not reward volume alone. Capitation is a different payment model that pays a fixed amount per patient regardless of services delivered, and MIPS keeps fee-for-service billing.
  51. The correct answer is C (Percutaneous balloon valvuloplasty, preferred for a mobile doming valve with a peak gradient above 64 mmHg): Balloon valvuloplasty is first-line therapy for symptomatic severe pulmonic stenosis with a mobile, dome-shaped, non-dysplastic valve, a peak gradient above 64 mmHg and little regurgitation. Surgery is reserved for dysplastic valves or failed balloon therapy. Beta blockade does not relieve fixed obstruction, and waiting for heart failure allows irreversible right ventricular damage.
  52. The correct answer is B (Peripheral venoarterial ECMO, with consideration of left ventricular venting by a route avoiding the aortic valve): Passing a microaxial catheter through a mechanical aortic prosthesis can damage the leaflets or jam them, so it is contraindicated, as is a left ventricular thrombus. The purge solution used does not change that risk. ECMO supports the circulation without crossing the valve. The Impella 5.5 also crosses the valve, because its inlet sits within the left ventricle and not above the leaflets.
  53. The correct answer is B (Persistent truncus arteriosus, from failure of the aorticopulmonary septum to divide the outflow tract.): Cardiac neural crest cells migrate into the outflow tract and form the aorticopulmonary septum, which divides the truncus into the aorta and pulmonary trunk. Failure produces persistent truncus arteriosus and related conotruncal defects such as tetralogy and interrupted aortic arch, often with 22q11.2 deletion. Secundum defects arise from septum primum resorption. Ebstein anomaly reflects abnormal tricuspid leaflet development.
  54. The correct answer is C (Discrete thrombus visualized in the appendage, which must be resolved on anticoagulation before implantation): Thrombus within the left atrial appendage is an absolute contraindication because device deployment can dislodge it and cause embolism. Implantation can be reconsidered after documented resolution on anticoagulation. Spontaneous echo contrast and low emptying velocity indicate high thrombotic risk but are not a mass, and a small foramen ovale is not an obstacle to transseptal access.
  55. The correct answer is C (Withhold nitrates, since tadalafil remains active about 48 hours, and treat ischemia with a beta-blocker.): Phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors potentiate nitric oxide pathways, and combined use with nitrates can cause profound refractory hypotension. The required nitrate-free interval is 24 hours after sildenafil or vardenafil but 48 hours after tadalafil, so twenty hours is inadequate. Ischemia can be treated with beta-blockade, and an intra-aortic balloon pump or early surgery are alternatives. The sildenafil interval cannot be generalized to tadalafil.
  56. The correct answer is B (Central venous 17, right ventricular diastolic 17, pulmonary diastolic 17, wedge 16 mmHg, absent y descent.): Tamponade equalizes diastolic pressures across the chambers because the pericardial pressure surrounds all of them, and the y descent is lost since early diastolic filling is impeded. Left failure raises wedge and pulmonary diastolic pressure above central venous pressure. Right failure raises central venous pressure above wedge pressure.
  57. The correct answer is C (Right radial overestimates systemic pressure from direct arterial jet, so the left radial should guide management): With right axillary perfusion the right radial line sits in the direct path of the arterial jet and often reads higher than true systemic pressure. The left radial value of about 60 mmHg is typical of adequate bypass perfusion, so management should follow the left side. Raising pump flow to match the right reading would cause hypertension and excessive flow. A stenosis explanation lacks supporting findings and the gap is an artifact, not true vasodilation, so a vasoconstrictor is not indicated.
  58. The correct answer is C (Severe, persistent right ventricular failure with refractory congestion despite inotropes and pulmonary vasodilators.): A durable left-sided pump depends on adequate right ventricular output to fill the left ventricle. Severe, refractory right failure predicts poor outcome and usually needs biventricular support or a different strategy. Obesity of this degree, controlled diabetes, and moderate mitral regurgitation are not contraindications.
  59. The correct answer is A (Hyperperfusion of the right arm from unrestricted graft flow, which risks edema and compartment syndrome): A radial pressure far above the contralateral side with swelling suggests the graft is delivering high flow into the arm with no outflow restriction, causing hyperperfusion injury. Management includes limiting flow to the arm, for example by partially snaring the artery distal to the graft. Kinking or innominate dissection would lower rather than raise the pressure.
  60. The correct answer is A (Cardiac magnetic resonance imaging showing myocardial edema and nonischemic late gadolinium enhancement in a subepicardial pattern.): Cardiac MRI using Lake Louise criteria detects edema and a nonischemic, often subepicardial, pattern of late gadolinium enhancement, and is the best noninvasive confirmatory test. Echocardiographic hypokinesis occurs in many cardiomyopathies and is not specific. Serologic viral titers do not confirm myocarditis because positive results are common without disease. Endomyocardial biopsy remains the invasive reference.
  61. The correct answer is C (Return the heart to its anatomic position, then give volume and a vasopressor): Verticalizing the heart kinks the right ventricle and venous inflow, producing hypotension, bradycardia and ischemia. Restoring the heart position removes the cause. Atropine does not correct the mechanical obstruction, and nitroglycerin lowers preload and worsens hypotension.
  62. The correct answer is B (Cardiac tamponade, with equalized diastolic pressures and a prominent x descent but absent y descent.): Equalization of right atrial, right ventricular end-diastolic, and wedge pressures at about 18 mmHg points to pericardial compression, and the preserved x descent with a blunted y descent reflects impaired diastolic filling in tamponade. The marked inspiratory fall in systolic pressure, pulsus paradoxus, is also typical. Constriction shows a prominent y descent and a dip-and-plateau waveform. Right ventricular infarction would not equalize the left-sided wedge pressure.
  63. The correct answer is C (Calcium chloride 1 g intravenously, which restores threshold potential relative to the depolarized resting membrane.): Hyperkalemia depolarizes the resting membrane, inactivates sodium channels and slows conduction, producing peaked T waves and a wide QRS. Calcium raises the threshold potential back toward the resting value within minutes without changing the potassium level. Insulin with dextrose shifts potassium into cells over 15 to 30 minutes. Furosemide removes potassium over hours and does not act on the membrane.
  64. The correct answer is B (High risk, so plan for possible temporary right ventricular support and optimize volume status before implantation): A pulmonary artery pulsatility index below about 1.85 and a central venous pressure to wedge ratio above about 0.63 both predict postoperative right ventricular failure. Here the index is about 1.4 and the ratio 0.75. Elevated pulmonary pressure is not reassuring and, by itself, can increase risk.
  65. The correct answer is A (Grade II diastolic dysfunction, indicating elevated left ventricular filling pressure and a pseudonormal mitral inflow pattern): The E to A ratio is 1.5 and average E over e' is 15, which exceeds 14. The tricuspid regurgitation velocity of 3.0 m/s exceeds 2.8 m/s and the left atrial volume index exceeds 34 mL/m2, so all three markers indicate elevated filling pressure. This pattern defines grade II. Grade III requires an E to A ratio of 2 or more, and grade I would show a ratio at or below 0.8 with normal filling pressure.
  66. The correct answer is C (Right atrial late-diastolic and right ventricular early-diastolic collapse, with exaggerated respiratory variation in mitral inflow.): Tamponade raises pericardial pressure above the pressure in the thin-walled right-sided chambers during their low-pressure phases, producing right atrial and right ventricular diastolic collapse. Ventricular interdependence also causes exaggerated respiratory variation in transvalvular flow. A restrictive filling pattern points to diastolic dysfunction, and systolic anterior motion points to dynamic outflow obstruction.
  67. The correct answer is A (Frailty predicts delayed recovery either way, and transfemoral TAVR is favored at her age if feasible.): Slow gait speed and weight loss are components of frailty, which independently predicts mortality and functional decline after both approaches. Guidelines favor transfemoral TAVR for patients over 80 when anatomy allows, and shared decision-making should include realistic recovery goals. Frailty measures are validated in cardiac surgical and transcatheter populations. The claim that surgery has lower early mortality in frail octogenarians is not supported.
  68. The correct answer is B (Solution leaks back through the incompetent valve; deliver retrograde or by ostia, and vent the ventricle.): With significant aortic regurgitation, cardioplegia given in the root flows into the left ventricle instead of the coronaries, so the heart distends and fails to arrest. Distention also raises wall tension and harms subendocardial perfusion. The solution should be given retrograde through the coronary sinus or directly into the ostia after aortotomy, with left ventricular venting. Repeating the antegrade dose, even colder or with more potassium, does not fix delivery.
  69. The correct answer is B (Bivalirudin by bolus and infusion with monitoring of clotting time, avoiding stasis anywhere in the circuit.): With active antibodies, heparin re-exposure risks thrombosis, so a direct thrombin inhibitor is used. Bivalirudin has guideline support for bypass in these patients, but blood stasis in the circuit or the chest causes clot because it is metabolized by proteases in plasma. Low-molecular-weight heparin has high cross-reactivity.
  70. The correct answer is C (Perform concomitant tricuspid annuloplasty, since annular dilation exceeds 40 mm and later progression is likely): Guidelines support tricuspid annuloplasty during left-sided surgery when the annulus exceeds 40 mm or 21 mm/m2, because functional regurgitation often progresses afterward. Regression is unreliable once the annulus is dilated. Functional regurgitation arises from annular dilation with structurally normal leaflets, so replacement is unnecessary.
  71. The correct answer is B (Medullary ischemia from raised intracranial pressure drives sympathetic vasoconstriction, then baroreflex bradycardia.): The Cushing response is a central ischemic reflex in which raised intracranial pressure reduces brainstem perfusion, and intense sympathetic activation raises arterial pressure to restore cerebral perfusion. The resulting hypertension stimulates baroreceptors and causes reflex bradycardia, with irregular respiration from brainstem dysfunction. Vagal stimulation would not initiate the hypertension. Adrenal catecholamines would be expected to raise, not lower, the heart rate.
  72. The correct answer is B (Vasopressin, which acts through V1 receptors independent of the blocked renin angiotensin pathway.): Sacubitril-valsartan blocks angiotensin II receptors and increases vasodilating peptides, so the resulting vasoplegia responds poorly to catecholamines and is best treated with vasopressin, which works through V1 receptors on a separate pathway. The cardiac index is preserved, so low contractility is not the problem. Angiotensin II acts at the AT1 receptor, which valsartan occupies, so it is expected to be ineffective at usual doses.
  73. The correct answer is C (Lung isolation with a double-lumen tube or bronchial blocker, plus external defibrillation pads placed before positioning.): Thoracoscopic epicardial access needs a quiet operative field, so one-lung ventilation is required, and pads must be on before positioning because pericardial access and arrhythmias can cause sudden instability. Generous tidal volumes with both lungs ventilated interfere with exposure, and spontaneous ventilation causes motion and risks hypoventilation in a patient with a low ejection fraction.
  74. The correct answer is C (Fibrinogen concentrate or cryoprecipitate to correct the low functional fibrinogen clot strength): A low EXTEM amplitude with an equally low FIBTEM amplitude shows the weak clot is due to fibrinogen deficiency rather than platelets, since FIBTEM blocks platelet contribution. Treatment is fibrinogen concentrate or cryoprecipitate. The heparinase test and normal clotting time exclude a residual heparin effect, so more protamine would not help.
  75. The correct answer is B (Continuing perioperative beta blockade, with prophylactic amiodarone considered in patients at high risk): Beta blockers reduce the incidence of postoperative atrial fibrillation and should be continued, and amiodarone prophylaxis is an option in higher-risk patients. Digoxin controls rate but does not prevent the arrhythmia. Statin loading showed benefit in small early studies, but a large randomized trial found no reduction, so it is not recommended for this purpose.
  76. The correct answer is C (Embolization of calcific and leaflet debris dislodged during balloon predilation or valve deployment): Histology of filter contents shows calcific debris, valve tissue, and thrombus, which supports a particulate embolic mechanism from manipulating the stenotic native valve. Brief rapid pacing seldom causes infarction in patients with intact cerebral autoregulation. Air through a venous or arterial sheath is rare and not the dominant source of stroke.
  77. The correct answer is B (Giving a fluid bolus based solely on the pulse pressure variation value of 16 percent): Pulse pressure variation depends on regular beat intervals, so irregular beat-to-beat filling in atrial fibrillation or frequent ectopy produces false elevations. The 16 percent value therefore cannot be trusted to predict fluid responsiveness. A passive leg raise with direct stroke volume measurement and a small fluid challenge with dynamic response assessment are both valid in arrhythmia.
  78. The correct answer is C (Aortic root dilation, a root pathology leaving structurally normal leaflets with a central regurgitant jet.): When the root and sinotubular junction dilate, normal leaflets are pulled apart and fail to coapt centrally, which produces a central jet through a structurally normal valve. Bicuspid prolapse gives an eccentric jet, and rheumatic disease thickens and retracts the leaflets. Recognizing root disease changes the operation, since it may need root replacement rather than isolated valve repair.
  79. The correct answer is A (Trypanosoma cruzi, transmitted by triatomine bugs, causing chronic myocarditis with conduction disease and apical aneurysm.): Chronic Chagas cardiomyopathy from Trypanosoma cruzi produces conduction disease, ventricular arrhythmias, heart failure and a characteristic apical aneurysm in people from endemic regions. Coxsackievirus causes acute myocarditis that usually resolves, and Lyme carditis causes reversible heart block after tick bites.
  80. The correct answer is B (Synchronized cardioversion, since fast rate and lost atrial contribution cause pulmonary edema in a stiff ventricle.): A noncompliant ventricle depends on atrial contraction and long diastole, so fast atrial fibrillation produces abrupt pulmonary congestion with hypotension. Hemodynamic compromise justifies synchronized cardioversion, and diuresis relieves congestion. Volume loading would worsen the edema, and phenylephrine adds afterload without addressing the rate or rhythm.
  81. The correct answer is A (Chronic left atrial hypertension triggers pulmonary arteriolar vasoconstriction and remodeling that adds a precapillary component): Long-standing elevation of left atrial pressure produces endothelial injury, medial hypertrophy, and vasoconstriction in the pulmonary arterioles, creating combined pre and post capillary hypertension. The resistance is calculated as (45 minus 28) divided by 4, or about 4.3 Wood units, which is elevated. Passive transmission alone would leave resistance normal.
  82. The correct answer is B (Inadequate renal perfusion pressure in chronic hypertension; raise mean pressure to 70 mmHg with vasopressors): Patients with chronic hypertension have a right shifted renal autoregulation curve, so a pressure of 48 mmHg falls below the threshold for adequate perfusion. Raising mean arterial pressure with phenylephrine or norepinephrine restores flow to the kidney. Diuretics treat the numbers without fixing the cause, and reducing pump flow would worsen perfusion.
  83. The correct answer is B (Watchman is an endocardial transseptal plug; a clip is applied epicardially at surgery.): Watchman is a percutaneous endocardial device placed through the femoral vein and a transseptal puncture with TEE guidance. A surgical clip is applied to the base of the appendage from the epicardial side during open or thoracoscopic surgery. The two approaches differ in access and in postimplant antithrombotic needs.
  84. The correct answer is A (Dissection flap across the right coronary ostium, reducing right coronary inflow to the inferior wall): The right coronary ostium is the most commonly involved coronary origin in type A dissection, and its malperfusion produces inferior ST elevation, nodal rhythm disturbance and inferior hypokinesis. Left main involvement would give anterolateral changes and widespread dysfunction. Tamponade causes hypotension and chamber collapse but not a regional wall motion abnormality in one coronary territory.
  85. The correct answer is B (Severe rheumatic mitral stenosis, with a valve area at or below 1.5 cm2): The planimetered valve area of 0.9 cm2 and the pressure half time of 240 ms, which gives an area of about 0.9 cm2 by dividing 220 by 240, agree with each other and fall under the 1.5 cm2 threshold for severe stenosis. The elevated gradient of 14 mmHg at a heart rate of 72 supports that. A pressure half time over 150 ms is already abnormal, so it does not exclude disease.
  86. The correct answer is A (Isolate the bleeding lung with a bronchial blocker or double-lumen tube, bleeding side dependent.): Pulmonary artery rupture is more likely with pulmonary hypertension, anticoagulation, hypothermia and distal balloon placement. Priority is protecting the healthy lung by lung isolation and positioning the bleeding side down, followed by reversing anticoagulation when feasible. Advancing the catheter or waiting passively risks flooding the healthy lung.
  87. The correct answer is A (Blockade of L-type calcium channels slows phase 0 depolarization in AV nodal cells, prolonging conduction.): Nodal cells depolarize through L-type calcium current rather than fast sodium current, so verapamil slows phase 0 in the SA and AV nodes and prolongs AV conduction. That reduces the number of atrial impulses reaching the ventricle during fibrillation. Fast sodium blockade is the mechanism of class I agents in working myocardium. Potassium channel blockade describes class III drugs such as amiodarone and sotalol.
  88. The correct answer is B (Allowing nasopharyngeal temperature to reach 38 C to limit later hypothermia and bleeding.): Brain temperature above 37 C after ischemic risk worsens neurologic injury, so the nasopharyngeal temperature is capped at about 37 C and warming is stopped near 36.5 C. The peripheral shell lags the core, and the gradient limit of 10 C protects against gas bubble emboli from outgassing. Stopping near 36 C allows equilibration, so a mild afterdrop is accepted.
  89. The correct answer is B (Invasive coronary angiography, because demonstrating obstructive epicardial disease defines an ischemic origin): Establishing or excluding significant coronary disease requires imaging the coronaries, and with normal renal function angiography is appropriate. Global hypokinesis can occur in both ischemic and idiopathic disease. Biopsy is reserved for suspected specific diagnoses and does not separate the two causes.
  90. The correct answer is A (Anticipate difficult separation from bypass and prepare inotropes and mechanical support before induction): Severe ventricular dysfunction raises perioperative mortality and the chance of low output after bypass, so inotropes, pacing, TEE and support devices should be ready. Poor function is not a contraindication to bypass, and indeed such patients often benefit. Monitoring should be more extensive, not less.
  91. The correct answer is A (Clotted mediastinal blood compressing the atria from incomplete drainage by the chest tubes): Early tamponade after cardiac surgery is usually due to surgical bleeding with clot that occludes drains, which explains the sudden fall in chest tube output with rising filling pressure. Right ventricular infarction raises central venous pressure but would not stop the drainage. Postpericardiotomy syndrome appears weeks later, not on the first day.
  92. The correct answer is C (Calcific degeneration with leaflet stiffening or tears, typically after ten years and faster in younger patients.): Structural valve deterioration of a bioprosthesis is caused by calcification and collagen fatigue, with tears or stiffening of the cusps, and failure rates rise after about ten years. Younger age, chronic kidney disease and mitral position accelerate degeneration. Pannus and thrombus typically occur earlier and have different echocardiographic appearances, with normal leaflet thickness rather than diffuse calcification.
  93. The correct answer is A (75 mL, from an orifice area of 0.50 cm2 and velocity time integral of 150 cm): The effective regurgitant orifice area equals 2 times pi times the radius squared times the aliasing velocity divided by the peak regurgitant velocity: 6.28 times 1.0 times 40 divided by 500 gives about 0.50 cm2. The regurgitant volume is the area times the regurgitant velocity time integral, 0.50 times 150 equals about 75 mL, which is in the severe range.
  94. The correct answer is C (Restore atrioventricular sequential pacing near 90 and add milrinone with an inhaled pulmonary vasodilator): A failing right ventricle with a junctional rhythm loses atrial kick, so sequential pacing at a faster rate restores filling and output. Milrinone and an inhaled vasodilator lower pulmonary resistance and improve contractility. Further volume overdistends the right ventricle and shifts the septum, and hypercapnia raises pulmonary vascular resistance.
  95. The correct answer is B (Stop protamine, give epinephrine with an inhaled pulmonary vasodilator, and resume bypass if refractory): Catastrophic pulmonary vasoconstriction after protamine raises pulmonary artery pressure and right ventricular afterload while the left atrial pressure stays low, causing right heart failure and systemic hypotension. Treatment is to stop protamine, support the right ventricle with epinephrine, lower pulmonary vascular resistance with inhaled agents, and return to bypass with heparin if refractory. Phenylephrine and further volume worsen right ventricular strain, and antihistamines do not address this mechanism.
  96. The correct answer is A (Prosthetic valve obstruction from thrombus or pannus that restricts leaflet motion): A mechanical valve normally produces a crisp closing click, so a muffled or absent click with a new high gradient signals a leaflet that is not opening or closing fully, from thrombus or pannus. Normal aging does not soften the click or raise the gradient. Annular calcification with normal leaflet motion would not produce a restricted inflow gradient of this size.
  97. The correct answer is C (ACT below 480 seconds despite about 500 units/kg of heparin; give antithrombin or plasma.): Heparin resistance is commonly defined as failure to reach an ACT of 480 seconds after about 500 units/kg of heparin, and the first treatment is antithrombin concentrate or fresh frozen plasma. The other thresholds do not match the accepted definition, and protamine would reverse rather than add anticoagulation.
  98. The correct answer is A (Use dexamethasone for prophylaxis and avoid ondansetron, droperidol and haloperidol): Ondansetron, droperidol and haloperidol prolong the QT interval and can precipitate torsades de pointes, a particular risk in congenital long QT syndrome. Dexamethasone has no effect on repolarization and is effective for prophylaxis. Low doses of QT-prolonging antiemetics still carry risk when baseline QTc is already markedly prolonged, so a safer alternative should be chosen.
  99. The correct answer is C (Acute cellular rejection, supported by timing and the lymphocytic infiltrate with myocyte damage): Acute cellular rejection is most common in the first weeks to months and is graded by biopsy on the presence of lymphocytes and myocyte injury. Primary graft dysfunction appears within the first day or so. Allograft vasculopathy takes years and does not give a lymphocytic infiltrate.
  100. The correct answer is A (Little rise in stroke volume, because the ventricle operates on the flat portion of its curve.): A failing ventricle has a depressed, flattened Frank-Starling curve, and a wedge pressure of 24 mmHg places it on the flat segment where more preload adds little stroke volume. The bolus mainly raises filling pressures and risks pulmonary edema. Preload responsiveness on the steep limb would be expected with a low wedge pressure. Added volume would raise, not lower, the wedge pressure.
  101. The correct answer is C (An air-filled trachea and main bronchus lies between the esophagus and that segment, blocking ultrasound transmission): The esophagus lies posterior to the airway, and the trachea and adjoining main bronchus interpose air between the probe and the distal ascending aorta and proximal arch, creating a blind spot. Epiaortic ultrasound can image this segment directly. The atrial appendage and pulmonary artery do not lie in the ultrasound path.
  102. The correct answer is B (Slower rate and reduced contractility, with increased preload, reduce dynamic obstruction and increase LV cavity size.): Takotsubo with a basal hypercontractile segment can develop dynamic LVOT obstruction. Lowering the rate and contractility and adding volume enlarge the outflow tract and reduce the gradient, so catecholamines and inotropes should be avoided. Beta blockers do not enhance vasoconstriction, and volume raises rather than lowers end diastolic volume.
  103. The correct answer is A (Suture or ring distortion of the left circumflex artery, with lateral ST elevation and posterolateral hypokinesis): The left circumflex artery runs in the atrioventricular groove close to the posterior mitral annulus, so a stitch or ring can kink or occlude it. The resulting ischemia matches the lateral leads and the posterolateral wall. Air in the right coronary produces inferior changes and right ventricular dysfunction, and spasm would not give regional wall motion loss with ST elevation in one territory.
  104. The correct answer is C (5.0 Wood units, calculated from the transpulmonary gradient of 20 mmHg divided by cardiac output.): Pulmonary vascular resistance equals mean pulmonary artery pressure minus wedge pressure, divided by cardiac output: (38 minus 18) divided by 4.0 equals 5.0 Wood units, or about 400 dyn s cm-5. Dividing the mean pressure alone by output ignores the downstream wedge pressure and gives 9.5. Subtracting an assumed right atrial pressure from the wedge pressure gives 2.0, which is not a resistance across the lungs.
  105. The correct answer is C (The right ventricular free wall, inferior left ventricular wall, posterior septum, and AV node.): In a right dominant system the RCA gives off the acute marginal branches to the right ventricle and the posterior descending artery to the inferior wall and posterior septum. The AV nodal artery arises from the RCA in about 90 percent of patients, so proximal occlusion causes inferior and right ventricular infarction with AV block. The anterior wall, septum and apex belong to the LAD territory, and the lateral wall belongs to the circumflex.
  106. The correct answer is B (Slowly titrated low-dose epidural with local anesthetic and opioid, avoiding abrupt sympathectomy and single-shot spinal.): A fall in systemic vascular resistance increases right-to-left shunting and can be fatal in Eisenmenger syndrome, so a rapid sympathectomy must be avoided. A carefully titrated epidural with low-dose agents gives good analgesia with slow, controllable hemodynamic effects. A single-shot spinal causes abrupt vasodilation. Neuraxial analgesia is not contraindicated by cyanosis when coagulation is acceptable.
  107. The correct answer is C (The Society of Thoracic Surgeons risk score, which integrates patient comorbidities and operative variables for CABG.): The STS Predicted Risk of Mortality model was built from a national cardiac surgery database and combines diabetes, renal function, ejection fraction, urgency and other variables to estimate CABG mortality. The Revised Cardiac Risk Index addresses noncardiac surgery and does not capture cardiac operative complexity. The Framingham score estimates long term community risk of coronary events, not operative outcome.
  108. The correct answer is A (Wire the left main and place a stent, with bypass and mechanical support ready.): A low coronary ostial height with sudden ST elevation and ventricular dysfunction after deployment indicates coronary obstruction by the displaced native leaflet or the valve frame. This is a life-threatening complication that needs rapid restoration of coronary flow with percutaneous stenting, with support available. Post-dilation may worsen the obstruction, and transient elevation is not an acceptable assumption in an unstable patient.
  109. The correct answer is C (Transseptal cannula drains the left atrium, and a centrifugal pump returns blood to the femoral artery): The device uses a cannula passed from the femoral vein across the interatrial septum into the left atrium, with a centrifugal pump and return through a femoral arterial cannula. The microaxial pump crossing the valve describes the Impella. Draining the right atrium to the pulmonary artery describes a percutaneous right ventricular support circuit.
  110. The correct answer is B (Minimize exposure time, maximize distance from the source, and use aprons and mobile barriers.): Radiation protection rests on time, distance, and shielding. Dose rises with exposure duration, falls with the square of distance from the source, and is reduced by lead aprons, thyroid collars, and mobile shields. Wider fields increase scatter, and standing close to the tube increases exposure.
  111. The correct answer is A (Urgent coronary angiography planning PCI or regrafting, while treating possible spasm with nitroglycerin): New ST elevation with new regional dysfunction soon after CABG indicates graft failure or occlusion until proven otherwise. Prompt angiography allows PCI or return to surgery, and nitroglycerin covers spasm. Waiting risks irreversible infarction, and thrombolysis within hours of sternotomy carries prohibitive bleeding risk.
  112. The correct answer is A (Withdraw the cannula and redirect it into mid left atrium, confirming position with bicaval views.): A tip against the appendage or pulmonary vein ostium causes suction and inadequate drainage. Pulling back and repositioning in the mid left atrium, with TEE confirmation, restores flow. Raising speed increases negative pressure and hemolysis without fixing the obstruction.
  113. The correct answer is C (Proceed with colectomy using invasive monitoring and hemodynamic control, with valvuloplasty as a bridge if unstable.): For urgent noncardiac surgery in symptomatic severe stenosis, an anesthetic with tight hemodynamic control is acceptable, and balloon valvuloplasty or TAVR can be considered if the surgery cannot proceed safely. Avoiding hypotension, tachycardia, and loss of sinus rhythm is critical because the fixed outlet obstruction limits compensation. Delaying a time-sensitive cancer operation for months is not reasonable. A spinal anesthetic produces abrupt vasodilation and is poorly tolerated.
  114. The correct answer is A (Tricuspid annuloplasty, because severe regurgitation with dilated annulus may persist and load the right ventricle.): Severe tricuspid regurgitation with annular dilation of about 40 mm or more is commonly repaired at the time of implant, because the lesion may not resolve and adds to right ventricular volume load. Left ventricular unloading does not reliably cure it. Mechanical replacement adds thrombosis risk and is not a standard choice here.
  115. The correct answer is C (Minimize fasting with clear liquids until two hours before surgery, or start maintenance intravenous fluids.): Dehydration raises viscosity and the risk of cerebral and renal thrombosis, so fasting is shortened and fluids are given. Phlebotomy is reserved for symptomatic hyperviscosity with hematocrit above about 65 percent after dehydration is excluded, and she has no symptoms. Prolonged fasting without fluids risks hypovolemia, which in a shunting patient lowers systemic resistance and worsens the right-to-left flow.
  116. The correct answer is B (Start inhaled nitric oxide or epoprostenol, correct hypercapnia and acidosis, and lower PEEP while maintaining MAP): Hypercapnia, acidosis and high intrathoracic pressure all raise pulmonary vascular resistance, so correcting them while giving inhaled pulmonary vasodilators unloads the right ventricle without lowering systemic pressure. An already distended right ventricle worsens with more volume. Systemic vasodilators like nitroglycerin drop MAP and coronary perfusion, and permissive hypercapnia raises resistance.
  117. The correct answer is C (Hyperkalemia, treated with calcium chloride, then insulin with glucose and hyperventilation): Peaked T waves, loss of P waves and a widened QRS with a potassium of 7.4 mEq/L establish hyperkalemia, a recognized consequence of potassium-rich cardioplegia. Calcium chloride stabilizes the myocardial membrane first, and insulin with glucose and alkalinization shift potassium into cells. Air embolism produces ST elevation in the right coronary territory, not diffuse peaked T waves.
  118. The correct answer is A (It provides greater forward flow and unloading than a balloon, independent of native rhythm and ejection.): The microaxial pump moves blood from the left ventricle to the ascending aorta, giving up to 3.5 L/min or more of flow regardless of rhythm or native contractility. A balloon pump depends on a trigger and on native ejection, and its flow benefit is modest. The catheter actually carries more hemolysis and access complications than a balloon pump, not fewer.
  119. The correct answer is B (Detecting coronary compression by the expanded outflow tract stent, which would contraindicate valve deployment.): The coronary arteries can lie close to the right ventricular outflow tract in repaired congenital heart disease, and an expanded stent or balloon can compress them and cause ischemia and death. Test balloon inflation with coronary angiography identifies this risk before deployment. The test is not meant to measure gradient or evaluate pulmonary veins.
  120. The correct answer is B (A left sided intercostal or lumbar branch usually T9 to L2 supplying the anterior spinal artery): The artery of Adamkiewicz, the arteria radicularis magna, usually arises on the left from an intercostal or lumbar artery between T9 and L2 and feeds the anterior spinal artery, which supplies the anterior two thirds of the cord. Its loss causes anterior spinal syndrome with paraplegia and sparing of dorsal column function. The vertebral artery supplies the upper cord and not the lumbar cord through posterior arteries. A renal artery branch does not supply the cord.
  121. The correct answer is C (Continue temporary epicardial pacing and observe about five to seven days before permanent implantation): Atrioventricular block after cardiac surgery often results from edema or contusion near the conduction system and frequently resolves within days. Guidelines support observation with temporary pacing for roughly five to seven days before implanting a permanent device if block persists. Immediate implantation sacrifices the chance of recovery, and four weeks of temporary wires exposes the patient to infection and wire failure.
  122. The correct answer is B (Differential hypoxemia from native lung blood reaching the arch; improve ventilation or change cannulation.): The right radial PaO2 of 51 mmHg against a femoral PaO2 of 290 mmHg shows that the upper body is perfused by poorly oxygenated blood from the lungs and native ejection. The oxygenator is working, as the lower body value shows, so replacing the membrane would not help. Vasoconstriction does not produce a gradient this large. Treatment is better lung oxygenation or changing the configuration so oxygenated blood reaches the arch.
  123. The correct answer is B (Glucagon 3 to 5 mg intravenous bolus, which raises cyclic AMP independent of beta receptors): Glucagon activates adenylyl cyclase through its own receptor and so bypasses the blocked beta receptor, increasing heart rate and contractility. Atropine fails because the problem is beta blockade rather than excess vagal tone. Phenylephrine raises pressure but can worsen bradycardia through the baroreceptor reflex.
  124. The correct answer is C (Higher diastolic pressure raises coronary perfusion, while lower end-diastolic aortic pressure reduces afterload and wall tension): Inflation in diastole raises aortic root pressure and coronary perfusion pressure, increasing supply. Rapid deflation just before systole lowers end-diastolic pressure, so the ventricle ejects against less afterload with lower wall tension and oxygen demand. Systolic pressure falls rather than rises, and diastolic pressure rises rather than falls.
  125. The correct answer is A (Dobutamine infusion, which raises infundibular contractility and worsens dynamic obstruction): A hypertrophied infundibulum narrows further with vigorous contraction, so inotropes and tachycardia worsen the dynamic gradient and lower output. Volume, beta blockade, and a pure alpha agonist improve the situation by increasing filling and reducing contractility while supporting coronary perfusion. The incorrect claims about phenylephrine and esmolol reverse their real effects.
  126. Injection between pectoralis and serratus targets lateral branches, and an erector spinae injection reaches dorsal rami.
  127. The correct answer is C (Regurgitant flow recirculates the cold injectate through the right heart, distorting the thermodilution curve): With severe tricuspid regurgitation, part of the cold injectate refluxes into the right atrium and returns repeatedly, so the temperature curve is prolonged and irregular and the area calculation becomes inconsistent. Variation between injections is the clue. A fixed calibration error would be constant between injections, and lumen warming is a general error that is not specific to this valve lesion.
  128. Oxygenation depends mainly on blood flow, so cutting blood flow would risk hypoxemia without fixing the alkalosis. Raising sweep would deepen the hypocapnia.
  129. The correct answer is C (Transgastric and deep transgastric views, which place the beam through the ventricle before reaching the prosthesis): In midesophageal views the beam crosses the left atrium and then the prosthesis, so the metallic components reflect it and shadow the ventricular side. Transgastric views approach from the ventricular side, allowing assessment of jets and the outflow tract without the shadow. Changing power or frequency cannot penetrate metal.

Hydrocortisone 50 mg every 6 hours is a reasonable adjunct that lowers vasopressor requirements. Escalating catecholamines adds toxicity and risks peripheral ischemia. The cardiac index is already elevated, so an inotrope such as dobutamine does not address the low resistance.
  131.
