Because the heparinase cup is also prolonged, residual heparin is not the cause, so protamine is not indicated. A normal amplitude means platelet function and fibrinogen are adequate, so platelets will not help.
  132. The correct answer is A (Maintain 70 to 80 mmHg, since autoregulation shifts rightward with chronic hypertension and cerebrovascular disease.): Cerebral autoregulation holds flow constant between about 50 and 150 mmHg in healthy people, but chronic hypertension and diseased cerebral vessels shift the lower limit upward. A MAP of 70 to 80 mmHg gives a safety margin in such a patient. Pressures of 40 to 50 mmHg risk watershed ischemia, and 50 to 60 mmHg fits a patient without these risk factors.
  133. The correct answer is B (Reduce tidal volume to 6 to 8 mL/kg, lower PEEP, and raise rate to normalize PaCO2.): Hypercapnia, acidosis, hypoxia and high lung volumes or PEEP all raise pulmonary vascular resistance and RV afterload. Lowering tidal volume and PEEP reduces mean airway pressure and overdistension, while a higher respiratory rate corrects hypercapnia. More PEEP compresses alveolar vessels and worsens RV load. Permissive hypercapnia raises resistance further and is poorly tolerated with a failing RV.
  134. The correct answer is C (Aortic root dilation with aortic regurgitation, plus mitral valve prolapse with regurgitation): Fibrillin-1 deficiency weakens the aortic root, causing annular and sinus dilation with aortic regurgitation, and produces myxomatous mitral leaflets that prolapse and leak. Stenosis with calcification or commissural fusion reflects degenerative or rheumatic disease, not a connective tissue disorder.
  135. The correct answer is C (Vasodilator stress perfusion imaging, because exercise heart rate rise creates artifactual septal defects here): In left bundle branch block, rate-related septal asynchrony produces false positive septal defects during exercise or dobutamine. Vasodilator stress with adenosine or regadenoson avoids the heart rate rise and gives more specific perfusion results. A higher heart rate target worsens the artifact, and ST analysis is uninterpretable with this baseline block.
  136. The correct answer is B (Midline septum, intermittent aortic valve opening, and no more than mild mitral regurgitation): The optimal speed unloads the ventricle enough to center the septum, reduce mitral regurgitation, and still allow the aortic valve to open at least occasionally. A rightward septum and slit ventricle mean overdrive with suction and added strain on a weak right ventricle. A leftward septum with a dilated ventricle means underdrive.
  137. The correct answer is B (Low body mass and albumin predict higher mortality and infection after transplant, so optimize nutrition): Cardiac cachexia and hypoalbuminemia are linked to higher perioperative mortality, infection and poor wound healing. Donor matching is not improved by cachexia, and wasting is associated with worse survival. Low albumin reflects poor nutrition and inflammation as well as congestion.
  138. The correct answer is A (Place the arterial line in the arm opposite the planned harvest or in a femoral artery.): A radial artery used as a conduit must be free of cannulation injury, thrombosis and intimal damage, and pressure monitoring is also lost on the harvest side once the artery is taken. The contralateral radial or a femoral artery avoids both problems. Cannulating the harvest side risks a damaged graft. Noninvasive cuffs are unreliable during nonpulsatile bypass.
  139. The correct answer is A (Maintain adequate bypass perfusion pressure, avoid severe hemodilution, and avoid nephrotoxins including NSAIDs and contrast.): Cardiac surgery associated kidney injury is driven by low renal perfusion, severe anemia from hemodilution, hemolysis and nephrotoxic exposures, so protecting perfusion pressure and oxygen delivery while avoiding nephrotoxins is the supported approach. Low dose dopamine and loop diuretics have not been shown to prevent kidney injury. Diuretics can improve urine volume without improving renal outcome.
  140. The correct answer is B (Use the lowest effective inotrope dose and move early to mechanical support, avoiding escalating catecholamines.): Inflamed myocardium is sensitive to catecholamines, which worsen arrhythmia and myocyte injury, so inotropes are kept to the lowest effective dose and temporary support such as venoarterial ECMO bridges to recovery. Milrinone has a long half-life, especially with reduced renal perfusion, and vasodilates, making hypotension hard to reverse. Waiting for arrest worsens outcome.
  141. The correct answer is C (Transplant-free survival is poor, so immunosuppression is started and early transplant or mechanical support is planned.): Giant cell myocarditis is an aggressive autoimmune disease with a median transplant-free survival of several months and frequent refractory ventricular arrhythmia. Combined immunosuppression, often steroids with cyclosporine, may prolong survival, but most patients need transplant or ventricular assist support. Steroids alone are insufficient.
  142. The correct answer is A (Descending aortic flow is a fixed fraction, roughly 70 percent, of total cardiac output): Esophageal Doppler measures velocity in the descending thoracic aorta, where about 70 percent of cardiac output flows because the head and upper limb vessels take the rest. The device multiplies the velocity integral by an estimated aortic area and corrects with this fixed fraction. The fraction can change with aortic clamping or marked shifts in vascular tone, which is a main limitation.
  143. The correct answer is C (Maintain coronary perfusion pressure with a vasopressor, apply defibrillator pads, and have bypass ready before induction): Ischemia from a left main dissection can progress to arrest during induction, so perfusion pressure, pads and a ready perfusionist matter most. High-dose propofol lowers diastolic pressure and coronary flow. Protamine is inappropriate because anticoagulation is needed for bypass and heparin reversal can promote thrombosis.
  144. The correct answer is A (Atheroembolism from descending aortic plaque dislodged by retrograde flow, so screen the aorta and iliofemoral vessels): Retrograde femoral perfusion pushes blood up the descending aorta, which can dislodge atheroma and cause embolic stroke, visceral or limb injury, and also retrograde dissection or false lumen perfusion. Preoperative CT angiography and intraoperative TEE of the descending aorta are used to select patients. Innominate directed flow and coronary ostial obstruction are not features of femoral cannulation, since the cannula tip lies in the iliac or femoral artery.
  145. The correct answer is C (Chronic venous hypertension causes arteriolar vasoconstriction and medial hypertrophy, adding a reactive component to passive transmission.): In mitral stenosis the pulmonary pressure first rises passively with left atrial pressure, but long-standing venous hypertension triggers arteriolar vasoconstriction and medial hypertrophy, so the transpulmonary gradient and resistance increase. This reactive component can improve after valve relief but may persist. Thromboembolism and hypoxic vasoconstriction can contribute but do not explain the characteristic disproportionate rise.
  146. The correct answer is A (HFrEF, defined by symptoms, LVEF of 40% or less, and an elevated natriuretic peptide level.): Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction requires an LVEF of 40% or less together with symptoms and objective evidence such as raised natriuretic peptides. An LVEF of 30% clearly meets that definition. The mildly reduced category applies only to an LVEF of 41% to 49%. The preserved category requires an LVEF of 50% or more, so it does not fit this patient.
  147. The correct answer is C (The pedicled graft lies in the field, so the surgeon must preserve it and avoid hypotension.): The right gastroepiploic artery is used as a pedicled conduit based on the celiac axis through the gastroduodenal artery, and its pedicle travels along the greater curvature of the stomach. Dividing it during gastrectomy would interrupt inflow to the right coronary territory. Hypotension would further endanger a graft that supplies myocardium.
  148. The correct answer is B (Three to five days before surgery, since ticagrelor binding is reversible and its effect wanes): Ticagrelor is a reversible P2Y12 inhibitor, and guidelines advise stopping it 3 to 5 days before elective surgery. Clopidogrel needs about 5 days and prasugrel 7 days because of irreversible binding. Waiting only a day leaves substantial platelet inhibition.
  149. The correct answer is A (Axillary perfusion or innominate clamping distorts right radial pressure, so the left radial guides systemic pressure): Right axillary cannulation can make the right radial pressure read high from the arterial jet, and innominate clamping or compromised flow can make it read low. A left radial line gives an independent estimate of systemic pressure. Pulse pressure variation is meaningless during circulatory arrest and on nonpulsatile bypass flow. Sampling convenience is not the reason, as either arm can serve for sampling if monitoring is unaffected.
  150. The correct answer is A (Left phrenic nerve injury from topical cold near the pericardium, usually recovering over months): The phrenic nerve runs along the pericardium and is vulnerable to cold injury from iced slush and to direct trauma during dissection, producing unilateral diaphragmatic paralysis with paradoxical motion. Vagus injury causes vocal cord paralysis, not diaphragm paralysis. Cord ischemia at that level would cause bilateral findings and limb deficits. Recovery typically takes weeks to months.
  151. The correct answer is B (Low-dose propofol titrated carefully, avoiding dexmedetomidine because it adds bradycardia and further hypotension): Dexmedetomidine causes dose-related bradycardia and hypotension, a bad choice when heart rate is 46/min and pacing cannot rescue it. Low-dose propofol titrated slowly may lower pressure but does not slow the heart, and the vasopressor is already running. A midazolam bolus risks hypotension and prolonged sedation, and benzodiazepines contribute to delirium.
  152. The correct answer is A (Give phenylephrine and a crystalloid bolus to raise systemic resistance and preload, reducing the shunt.): The spell reflects infundibular spasm and a fall in systemic resistance, which increase right-to-left shunting across the large ventricular septal defect. Phenylephrine raises systemic resistance, and volume increases right ventricular filling, both lowering the shunt fraction. Milrinone lowers systemic resistance and worsens the shunt. Isoproterenol increases contractility and heart rate, which intensifies infundibular obstruction.
  153. The correct answer is B (Hypothermia lowers BIS independent of depth, so give amnestic and anesthetic doses before rewarming.): Cooling slows neuronal activity and the EEG, so BIS falls without any change in drug effect. As temperature returns to normal, brain activity and awareness risk return while drug concentrations have fallen from hemodilution. A rising BIS during rewarming is a real signal of lighter depth. Dosing should anticipate rewarming rather than rely on the cold value.
  154. The correct answer is B (Cessation at least four weeks before surgery reduces postoperative pulmonary complications in cardiac surgery): Benefits on mucociliary clearance and airway reactivity accumulate over weeks, and abstinence for at least four weeks reduces pulmonary complications, with greater benefit at eight weeks or longer. Quitting only a day or two before surgery does not remove risk. Six months is not required.
  155. The correct answer is C (Dobutamine stress echocardiography, which avoids the airway constriction caused by adenosine receptor stimulation): Adenosine acts on A1 and A2B receptors in airway smooth muscle and can trigger severe bronchospasm, so it is relatively contraindicated in active asthma. Dipyridamole raises endogenous adenosine and carries the same risk. Dobutamine stress echocardiography relies on beta-adrenergic stimulation, which does not constrict the airways and is the usual alternative.
  156. The correct answer is C (Giving ephedrine 10 mg for pressure 112/60 mmHg, which raises heart rate and oxygen demand.): Tachycardia increases oxygen demand and shortens diastole, the period when most left ventricular coronary flow occurs, which is critical with left main disease. Ephedrine raises heart rate and contractility, worsening this balance. Slowing the rate and correcting anemia both improve the balance.
  157. The correct answer is B (Low aortic diastolic pressure with high LV end diastolic pressure lowers the gradient driving coronary flow.): Coronary perfusion pressure is approximately the aortic diastolic pressure minus the LV end diastolic pressure. Severe aortic regurgitation lowers diastolic pressure and raises LV end diastolic pressure, so here the gradient is only about 7 mmHg, which endangers subendocardial perfusion especially with a fixed stenosis. Systolic compression and right atrial pressure are minor contributors.
  158. The correct answer is A (Titrated etomidate with fentanyl, with an epinephrine or norepinephrine infusion ready to preserve contractility and rate.): With severe systolic dysfunction the goals are to preserve contractility, avoid abrupt falls in preload and afterload, and avoid bradycardia. Etomidate causes little hemodynamic change, and a cautious opioid dose blunts laryngoscopy. High-dose volatile and a propofol bolus both depress contractility and vasomotor tone, and propofol does not provide vagolytic support.
  159. The correct answer is B (Radiation-induced pericardial fibrosis, which also commonly damages the valves and coronary arteries.): Mediastinal radiation causes progressive pericardial fibrosis and calcification years later, often with accompanying valvular, coronary and myocardial injury that worsens operative risk. Idiopathic or postsurgical disease predominates in developed countries, and tuberculosis dominates only in endemic regions. His radiotherapy history makes radiation the best explanation.
  160. The correct answer is A (Review hiring and promotion data, sponsor underrepresented colleagues for leadership, and report progress regularly): Sustained allyship changes structures: examining data, sponsoring individuals into opportunities and tracking measurable progress with accountability. A statement and an annual event are visible gestures that do not alter outcomes. Loading the one underrepresented faculty member with unpaid committee leadership places the burden on those affected and is a common form of performative inclusion.
  161. The correct answer is C (Use normothermic bypass with warm blood cardioplegia, avoiding systemic cooling below the thermal amplitude.): A thermal amplitude of 30 C means the antibody binds red cells whenever temperature falls below that point, so cooling or cold cardioplegia can cause agglutination and coronary microvascular occlusion. Warm bypass with warm blood cardioplegia keeps the patient above the threshold. Titer alone does not remove risk when the thermal amplitude is high, and rituximab does not act within hours.
  162. The correct answer is C (Place lines in parallel with room setup, using a second provider or a separate induction area): The data point to serial line placement as the largest contributor, so redesigning the workflow to do it in parallel directly removes that delay, and tracking on-time starts confirms the effect. Moving the scheduled start earlier shifts the problem without addressing its cause. Reporting individual delay minutes is punitive, ignores the system design and addresses a smaller contributor.
  163. The correct answer is A (Aortic dissection from the cannula or jet, with the cannula tip lying in the false lumen.): A high line pressure with a low systemic pressure means flow is not reaching the true arterial lumen, and a spreading bluish aortic wall is the surgeon's view of blood dissecting the layers. A clotted filter would also raise line pressure but would not discolor the aorta. A venous air lock lowers drainage and reservoir level and would not raise arterial inflow pressure. Bypass should be stopped and the cannula repositioned or relocated.
  164. The correct answer is C (Janeway lesions are painless septic emboli, while Osler nodes are painful and immune complex mediated): Janeway lesions are nontender hemorrhagic macules on the palms and soles that represent microabscesses from septic emboli. Osler nodes are tender nodules on the finger and toe pads and are thought to be immune complex deposition. Reversing the pain and mechanism, or calling both embolic, gives an incorrect picture.
  165. The correct answer is A (High-voltage pulses stimulate skeletal muscle and the diaphragm, producing movement that can displace catheters): Pulsed field ablation uses brief high-voltage electrical fields that cause nonthermal cell death by irreversible electroporation. The same fields depolarize nearby skeletal muscle, which produces vigorous diaphragm and body movement that threatens catheter stability. Neuromuscular blockers do not affect muscarinic receptors or smooth muscle peristalsis, and the lesion is not thermal, so the proposed vagal and thermal mechanisms do not apply.
  166. The correct answer is C (Stop protamine, give inhaled epoprostenol or nitric oxide with epinephrine, and prepare to resume bypass.): The exhibit shows pulmonary hypertension with right ventricular failure and an underfilled left ventricle, the picture of catastrophic pulmonary vasoconstriction after protamine. Treatment is to stop protamine, lower pulmonary resistance with an inhaled vasodilator, support the right ventricle with epinephrine, and return to bypass if refractory. Volume loading worsens right ventricular distension, and more protamine continues the trigger.
  167. The correct answer is C (Stop all heparin including flushes, start argatroban or bivalirudin, and send PF4 antibody testing.): A fall in platelets of more than 50 percent five to ten days after heparin with new thrombosis and a high 4T score is HIT with thrombosis until proven otherwise. All heparin exposure must stop and a non-heparin anticoagulant must begin without waiting for the assay. Low molecular weight heparin cross-reacts with the antibody. Platelet transfusion can fuel further thrombosis.
  168. The correct answer is A (Skeletonized mammary harvest to preserve sternal collateral flow, or using only a single mammary artery.): The sternum is supplied mainly by the internal mammary arteries, with collateral flow through the posterior intercostal branches that a pedicled harvest tends to divide. Skeletonizing the vessel preserves more sternal perfusion, and many surgeons limit diabetic obese patients with COPD to one mammary artery. Pedicled bilateral harvest removes more collateral supply, not less. Heavier wires reduce motion but do not restore blood flow to the bone.
  169. The correct answer is B (Particulate or gaseous emboli passing through the insonated artery, detected as high-intensity transient signals.): High-intensity transient signals are brief, unidirectional, short-duration spikes with a chirp that reflect emboli, either solid atheroma or air, crossing the sample volume. They are commonly seen after aortic manipulation and clamp release. Vasospasm and hypercapnia change mean velocity or spectral pattern but do not create discrete transient embolic signals.
  170. The correct answer is C (Right-sided double-lumen tube with bronchoscopic confirmation of right upper lobe slot alignment): When an aneurysm compresses the left mainstem bronchus, a left-sided tube risks airway trauma, cuff malposition and obstruction. A right-sided double-lumen tube avoids the narrowed segment, though the right upper lobe takeoff is short and needs bronchoscopic confirmation of slot alignment. Inflating a blocker within a compressed bronchus invites mucosal injury and poor seal.
  171. The correct answer is A (Structurally normal pliable leaflets, with regurgitation caused by annular and sinus dilation): Valve-sparing root replacement, as in the David or Yacoub procedure, works when leaflets are pliable and structurally normal and regurgitation comes from annular or sinus dilation. Marfan patients are the main beneficiaries because it avoids lifelong anticoagulation. Heavily calcified stenotic leaflets cannot be preserved. Active endocarditis with abscess requires debridement and valve replacement rather than leaflet preservation.
  172. The correct answer is B (Coronary embolization of vegetation fragments, so avoid fibrinolytics and pursue catheter or surgical retrieval): The aortic valve sits next to the coronary ostia, so septic fragments readily embolize and cause infarction in a young patient with no atherosclerosis. Fibrinolysis is hazardous because of the risk of hemorrhage from mycotic aneurysm and could extend the infection. Retrieval or surgery is therefore preferred, and vasospasm would not explain a vegetation source.
  173. The correct answer is B (Alpha-stat preserves autoregulation and flow-metabolism coupling, whereas pH-stat adds CO2 and makes flow pressure passive): Alpha-stat leaves total CO2 unchanged during cooling, so the patient becomes relatively alkalotic and cerebral autoregulation and flow-metabolism coupling are preserved. pH-stat adds CO2 to hold pH at 7.40 at the patient temperature, which causes cerebral vasodilation and pressure passive flow with higher embolic load. The statement reversing the two strategies is therefore wrong. Claiming that they behave equally ignores the vasodilating effect of added CO2.
  174. The correct answer is C (Escalate now to temporary mechanical circulatory support with an appropriate device): Worsening lactate and organ perfusion despite two vasoactive agents mark advancing shock, and early mechanical support improves survival. Delay with escalating drugs raises arrhythmias and myocardial oxygen demand. A third inotrope adds little when end-organ perfusion is already deteriorating.
  175. The correct answer is C (Rewarm above the thermal amplitude, use warm blood cardioplegia, and warm all circuit fluids.): Cold agglutinins bind red cells below the thermal amplitude and fix complement, causing intravascular hemolysis during cooling. Management is to avoid cooling below that temperature, warm the patient, and use warm blood cardioplegia and warmed fluids. Deeper cooling intensifies agglutination. Cold red cells would agglutinate and hemolyze in the same way, so blood products are warmed.
  176. The correct answer is B (Catheter tip in West zone 1 or 2, so wedge reflects alveolar rather than atrial pressure.): In zones 1 and 2, alveolar pressure exceeds pulmonary venous pressure, so the column of blood between the catheter and left atrium is interrupted and the wedge reflects airway pressure. High PEEP and hypovolemia promote this, and the tip above the atrium makes it more likely. In zone 3 the venous pressure exceeds alveolar pressure and the wedge is reliable. A damped continuous-flow tracing would more likely indicate failure to wedge than a falsely high reading.
  177. The correct answer is B (Chewed aspirin, parenteral anticoagulation, an oral P2Y12 inhibitor, high-intensity statin, and oral beta blocker.): Initial therapy for non-ST-elevation acute coronary syndrome includes aspirin, an anticoagulant such as heparin or enoxaparin, a P2Y12 inhibitor, a high-intensity statin, and a beta blocker when no heart failure or shock is present. Thrombolysis is not indicated without ST elevation and can cause harm. Anticoagulation reduces thrombotic events before angiography.
  178. The correct answer is C (Prolonged high pressure recruits alveoli and raises mean airway pressure, but reduces venous return): APRV uses a prolonged high pressure phase with brief releases, which recruits alveoli and raises mean airway pressure, improving oxygenation. The higher intrathoracic pressure reduces venous return and can load a vulnerable right ventricle. The release phase is short, and the mode is designed to permit, not suppress, spontaneous breathing.
  179. The correct answer is C (Coronary steal into the low resistance pulmonary artery causes ischemia, so avoid pulmonary vasodilation and tachycardia.): With collateral development, blood flows retrograde from the left coronary into the pulmonary artery, which is a low pressure sink, so the left ventricle is perfused poorly and ischemia results. Interventions that lower pulmonary vascular resistance increase the steal, and tachycardia worsens the supply and demand balance. Volume overload from a shunt is not the dominant problem, and hyperventilation with oxygen would worsen the steal.
  180. The correct answer is A (Right ventricular systolic pressure above two thirds of systemic pressure, or right ventricular failure symptoms.): Conduit stenosis creates chronic right ventricular pressure overload, and the accepted threshold for intervention is right ventricular pressure above two thirds of systemic pressure or symptoms and dysfunction. Calcification alone does not mandate replacement, and a modest gradient with normal ventricular pressure can be followed.
  181. The correct answer is B (Heparin 8,000 units (100 units/kg) with a target activated clotting time above 250 seconds): Endovascular aortic work uses a lower heparin dose than full cardiopulmonary bypass, commonly 100 units/kg, for an ACT above about 250 seconds: 80 kg x 100 units/kg gives 8,000 units. Three hundred units/kg with an ACT above 480 seconds is the bypass regimen. A 30 units/kg dose is below the level needed to protect large-bore catheters and grafts from thrombosis.
  182. The correct answer is A (Add lead V5, which covers the anterior and lateral left ventricle that lead II misses.): Lead II alone detects a minority of ischemic episodes, mostly inferior, while V5 detects most anterior and lateral events, and combining II with V5 raises sensitivity to roughly 80 percent. Lead III adds little beyond lead II, and aVL is a high lateral lead, not a right coronary lead.
  183. The correct answer is A (Constrictive pericarditis, since interdependence causes respiratory inflow variation and annular e' velocity stays preserved.): In constriction the pericardium isolates the cardiac chambers from intrathoracic pressure changes, producing ventricular interdependence and marked respiratory variation of inflow, while the myocardium itself relaxes normally so annular e' is preserved or high. Restrictive cardiomyopathy shows little respiratory variation and a low e' because the myocardium is stiff. Tamponade is a fluid driven condition with an effusion on imaging.
  184. The correct answer is C (Tamponade the tear, give blood, and start femoral bypass with heparin while bleeding is controlled.): Immediate control of a ventricular tear is by pressure, with volume resuscitation using blood and rapid institution of bypass, ideally by femoral cannulation that does not require a completed sternotomy. Heparin is given for bypass, but bleeding control and resuscitation proceed in parallel instead of waiting. Crystalloid alone dilutes clotting factors and delays the definitive support that the patient needs.
  185. The correct answer is B (Prepare for redo sternotomy with large-bore access, cross-matched blood and a plan for right ventricular support.): Redo sternotomy through adherent tissue and a driveline carries a high risk of bleeding, and the new pump can unmask right ventricular failure, so access, products and right-sided support are planned. A balloon pump does not meaningfully augment a continuous-flow pump. Anticoagulation is managed with the surgeon, and continuing through incision would increase bleeding.
  186. The correct answer is B (Open the aorta and give cardioplegia directly into each coronary ostium, adding retrograde delivery): With severe regurgitation, root cardioplegia leaks back into the left ventricle, so the coronary arteries receive too little solution and the ventricle distends. Opening the aorta and perfusing each ostium directly with handheld cannulas delivers reliable arrest, and retrograde delivery adds protection for the left ventricle. Raising pressure to 150 mmHg injures the root and still leaks across the valve. Retrograde cardioplegia alone protects the right ventricle and posterior septum poorly because the coronary sinus drains little from those areas.
  187. The correct answer is A (Larger cross-section channel containing cobweb strands and a beak sign, with later contrast opacification): The false lumen is usually the larger channel, with a beak-shaped wedge of hematoma at the flap origin, cobwebs of residual medial strands, and slower opacification. The true lumen is typically smaller, fills early, and is continuous with the undissected aorta. Calcified intimal plaque indicates the original lumen wall, so that channel is the true lumen.
  188. The correct answer is B (Idarucizumab 5 g intravenously, given as two consecutive 2.5 g infusions): Idarucizumab is a humanized antibody fragment that binds dabigatran with high affinity and reverses its effect within minutes, and the standard dose is 5 g. Andexanet alfa reverses factor Xa inhibitors, not direct thrombin inhibitors. Prothrombin complex concentrate may be a fallback when idarucizumab is unavailable but it is not a specific antidote.
  189. The correct answer is A (He remains a candidate because PVR is reversible to below 3 Wood units with vasodilator testing.): The baseline PVR is (45 minus 28) divided by 4.0, about 4.3 Wood units, and a fall to 2.4 with nitroprusside shows reactive, reversible pulmonary hypertension. Reversibility to below about 3 Wood units with an acceptable systemic pressure generally permits isolated heart transplant. Baseline elevation is not an absolute bar, and fixed disease would show no response to vasodilators.
  190. The correct answer is C (It will be overestimated, because the smaller blood temperature change produces a smaller curve area.): Cardiac output is inversely related to the area under the thermodilution curve. Warmer injectate produces a smaller temperature drop and a smaller area, which the computer interprets as higher flow, so output is overestimated. The computation constant assumes the programmed injectate temperature and volume, so it cannot correct for a different actual temperature.
  191. The correct answer is A (Precapillary pulmonary hypertension, since wedge pressure is normal and pulmonary vascular resistance is elevated): Mean pulmonary artery pressure above 20 mmHg defines pulmonary hypertension. The wedge pressure of 12 mmHg is not elevated, and the pulmonary vascular resistance is 26 / 4.0 = 6.5 Wood units, which is above 2. That combination is precapillary. Postcapillary disease requires a wedge pressure above 15 mmHg, so correcting the valve lesion would not be expected to fix this.
  192. The correct answer is B (Replacement is reasonable at about 4.0 to 4.5 cm, lower than the 5.0 cm Marfan threshold): Loeys-Dietz syndrome causes aggressive aortic disease, with dissection at smaller diameters and in younger patients than Marfan syndrome, so guidelines support elective root replacement at about 4.0 to 4.5 cm. The Marfan threshold is 5.0 cm. The 5.5 cm figure applies to sporadic disease with a tricuspid valve. Loeys-Dietz arises from TGF-beta pathway variants, not fibrillin.
  193. The correct answer is C (Bicaval and short-axis aortic valve views, showing the tented fossa ovalis and mid left atrium cannula.): The bicaval view shows the interatrial septum in profile, so tenting of the fossa ovalis is seen during puncture, and the short-axis aortic valve view helps confirm a posterior, inferior puncture and the final cannula tip. The left ventricle and descending aorta are not the target sites for a left atrial inflow cannula.
  194. The correct answer is A (Squeezing the heart to palpate or reposition the left atrium before bypass is established.): Myxomas are friable and can fragment, so direct manipulation of the atrium before the heart is isolated risks embolization to the brain or systemic arteries. Early clamping and cardioplegia isolate the heart and are standard practice. Tachycardia shortens diastole and reduces the time the tumor sits in the mitral orifice, so it is not the main embolic hazard here.
  195. The correct answer is B (Reduced left ventricular filling, from low volume or right heart dysfunction, lowers the index; assess volume.): The pulsatility index reflects the ventricular contribution to pump flow, so it falls when the ventricle is underfilled and rises with greater filling. A transient fall with reduced flow suggests a low-preload event from hypovolemia or right-sided failure. Overload raises the index, and thrombosis produces persistent power and hemolysis abnormalities.
  196. The correct answer is A (A patient with Eisenmenger syndrome from a complex cardiac lesion that cannot be repaired.): Combined heart-lung transplant is reserved for end-stage disease involving both organs, such as Eisenmenger syndrome with complex unrepairable defects or advanced pulmonary hypertension with biventricular failure. Pulmonary hypertension with preserved ventricular function is treated with lung transplantation or drug therapy, and reversible resistance is managed with heart transplantation alone.
  197. The correct answer is B (Offer early titrated epidural analgesia to limit tachycardia, with standardized pain scoring against undertreatment): In mitral stenosis, tachycardia shortens diastolic filling and raises left atrial pressure, so early, gradually titrated epidural analgesia that controls pain and heart rate is preferred. Standardized pain scoring reduces the subjective undertreatment documented in Black patients. Delaying analgesia worsens tachycardia, and meperidine can add tachycardia while neuraxial block is not contraindicated in this lesion.
  198. The correct answer is C (Start at 20 ppm, monitor methemoglobin, and wean gradually to avoid rebound pulmonary hypertension.): Inhaled nitric oxide is typically started at 5 to 40 ppm, with 20 ppm common, and is selective for the pulmonary vasculature because hemoglobin inactivates it on contact. Methemoglobin and nitrogen dioxide are monitored, and weaning is gradual because abrupt cessation can cause rebound pulmonary hypertension. A dose of 80 ppm adds toxicity, and systemic hypotension is not expected.
  199. The correct answer is B (Area 1.0 cm2 or less, mean gradient 40 mmHg or more, velocity 4.0 m/s or more.): Severe aortic stenosis is defined by a valve area of 1.0 cm2 or less, a mean gradient of 40 mmHg or more, and a peak jet velocity of 4.0 m/s or more. The 1.5 cm2 and 3.0 m/s thresholds correspond to mild-to-moderate disease. Valve areas of 0.6 cm2 and gradients above 60 mmHg describe very severe disease, but severe stenosis does not need to reach these values to qualify for intervention.
  200. The correct answer is C (About 4.6 L/min, which is 2.4 multiplied by the stated body surface area): Full bypass flow is the product of cardiac index and body surface area. For this patient 2.4 L/min/m2 times 1.90 m2 equals 4.56 L/min, rounded to 4.6 L/min. The other values correspond to cardiac indexes of 1.9 and 3.0 and do not match the planned index.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Air lodging in the right coronary ostium, so raise perfusion pressure with a vasopressor): The right coronary ostium is the most anterior and superior, so air that enters the aorta tends to rise into it, causing inferior ST elevation and right ventricular dysfunction. A higher perfusion pressure drives the air through the microcirculation, and bypass can be resumed if needed. The territory involved matches the inferior leads and right ventricle, not the anterior wall, and nitroglycerin would lower pressure.
  2. The correct answer is A (State the concern with CUS language, then invoke the chain of command if it stays unresolved): The target ACT for bypass is above roughly 400 to 480 seconds, so 310 seconds is a real safety concern. CUS language is a clear, structured escalation tool, and the chain of command is the next step if the issue remains unresolved. Repeating a vague hint does not escalate, and delayed documentation cannot prevent the immediate patient hazard.
  3. The correct answer is A (Severe tricuspid regurgitation producing a ventricularized right atrial waveform with a fused cv wave): Severe tricuspid regurgitation fills the right atrium during systole, which raises the v wave and abolishes the x descent so the tracing looks like a right ventricular waveform. Tamponade preserves the x descent and blunts the y descent. Constriction produces a rapid y descent with a prominent x descent, giving an M or W shape.
  4. The correct answer is B (Confirm loss of perfusion, check controller and driveline connections, and begin chest compressions): Continuous-flow device recipients normally have no palpable pulse, so absent pulse alone does not define arrest. Here the arterial waveform and end-tidal CO2 both confirm loss of perfusion, and current guidance supports compressions in that situation while the controller, battery and driveline are checked. Delaying compressions for interrogation risks prolonged hypoperfusion.
  5. The correct answer is C (Ejection fraction above 25 percent with aortic velocity time integral of at least 10 cm.): Weaning criteria at low flow include left ventricular ejection fraction above about 20 to 25 percent, aortic velocity time integral of at least 10 cm, and lateral mitral annular S prime above 6 cm/s. The other findings show inadequate left ventricular output or right ventricular failure that would fail wean. Hemodynamic stability on low flow accompanies these echocardiographic targets.
  6. The correct answer is A (The gradient fall shows improved opening, and the large v wave suggests new regurgitation from tearing.): A falling mean gradient and left atrial pressure show successful relief of stenosis. A new large v wave after commissurotomy suggests significant mitral regurgitation, which can result from leaflet tearing or commissural splitting, and should prompt echocardiographic confirmation. Residual stenosis would keep the mean pressure and gradient high, and restored compliance does not account for a large v wave.
  7. The correct answer is B (The left anterior descending artery, which supplies the anterior wall and anterior septum.): At the midpapillary level the anterior wall and anterior septum are perfused by the left anterior descending artery. The right coronary artery usually supplies the inferior wall and inferior septum, and the circumflex supplies the anterolateral and inferolateral walls. Normal inferior and lateral motion makes either of those vessels an unlikely source.
  8. The correct answer is A (It was withdrawn after a randomized trial showed higher mortality than lysine analogues, with renal concerns.): The BART trial found higher mortality with aprotinin than with tranexamic acid or aminocaproic acid, and observational data linked it to renal dysfunction, so it was suspended in 2007. Seizures are a recognized risk of high-dose tranexamic acid rather than aprotinin. Aprotinin lengthens some kaolin ACT values but was not withdrawn for that reason.
  9. The correct answer is C (Limit drainage to about 10 to 15 mL per hour to avoid subdural hematoma): Overdrainage of CSF lowers intracranial pressure, stretches bridging veins and can cause subdural hematoma, so drainage is limited to roughly 10 to 15 mL per hour and titrated to pressure. Draining 30 to 40 mL per hour raises that risk considerably. Setting the drain at shoulder level produces uncontrolled drainage from gravity and excessive volume. Controlled intermittent drainage targeting a CSF pressure near 10 mmHg balances spinal perfusion against intracranial hypotension.
  10. The correct answer is A (Patient 1, because LBBB with a QRS over 150 ms predicts the greatest response.): Class I benefit is strongest with left bundle branch block, a QRS of 150 ms or more, sinus rhythm, an ejection fraction of 35% or less, and NYHA class II to III symptoms. Right bundle branch block and nonspecific conduction delay with a shorter QRS show weaker response, and atrial fibrillation reduces effective biventricular pacing.
  11. The correct answer is B (Emergent pericardial drainage or sternotomy with volume resuscitation, and rapid transfer to surgical repair on bypass.): Pulseless electrical activity with an effusion after infarction indicates free wall rupture with tamponade, which is reversible only by relieving the tamponade and surgically repairing the rupture. Standard compressions do not work with a pericardium that is filling, and thrombolytics worsen bleeding. Balloon pumping does not address tamponade.
  12. The correct answer is A (Start an inotrope and an inhaled pulmonary vasodilator, and lower pump speed to preserve septal position.): A TAPSE of 8 mm, fractional area change of 22 percent, and an elevated central venous pressure show significant right ventricular dysfunction. Inotropic support, pulmonary vasodilation, and a slightly lower pump speed protect the right ventricle and septum. Weaning without treatment risks acute right failure, and higher speed would shift the septum and worsen it.
  13. The correct answer is C (Right atrial pressure exceeds left atrial pressure, so the shunt reverses, sending desaturated blood leftward.): After transseptal puncture the defect usually shunts left to right because left atrial pressure is higher, but a patient with elevated right-sided pressures after relief of mitral regurgitation can develop right-to-left flow. Desaturated blood then bypasses the lungs, and hypoxemia does not correct with oxygen. Left-to-right shunting does not lower arterial saturation, and residual regurgitation would be visible on the echocardiogram.
  14. The correct answer is B (Serology for Coxiella burnetii, where a phase I IgG titer above 1:800 supports endocarditis): Culture-negative endocarditis with livestock exposure points to Coxiella burnetii (Q fever), diagnosed by serology with a phase I IgG titer above 1:800 supporting chronic infection. Vegetation size cannot identify an organism. Autoimmune markers do not diagnose nonbacterial thrombotic endocarditis and are not the best test in an exposure history with fever and an infected valve.
  15. The correct answer is B (Treat the underlying disease and start therapeutic anticoagulation with warfarin because of the thromboembolic history): Libman-Sacks lesions are sterile fibrin and immune complex deposits, so antibiotics have no role. With antiphospholipid antibodies and a prior embolic event, warfarin anticoagulation plus control of the autoimmune disease is recommended. Surgery is reserved for severe dysfunction or recurrent embolism despite therapy, not for a small vegetation with mild regurgitation.
  16. The correct answer is A (Left subclavian or internal jugular catheter, which would follow the persistent vein to the coronary sinus): A left-sided catheter follows the persistent left vena cava into the coronary sinus, where the tip can cause arrhythmia or perforation and interferes with retrograde cardioplegia delivery. A right internal jugular line enters the normal right superior vena cava and is the preferred route. A femoral line avoids the problem entirely.
  17. The correct answer is A (Dynamic outflow obstruction, with a larger gradient and narrower aortic pulse pressure after the pause): After a premature beat, the longer filling and greater contractility increase obstruction in a dynamic outflow lesion, so left ventricular pressure rises while the aortic pressure and pulse pressure fall, the Brockenbrough-Braunwald-Morrow sign. In fixed valvular stenosis both pressures rise after the pause. Aortic regurgitation does not behave this way.
  18. The correct answer is B (Lower preload and afterload under anesthesia reduce ventricular size and systolic pressure, decreasing regurgitant volume): Functional regurgitation is load dependent. General anesthesia reduces systemic vascular resistance and preload, which shrinks the ventricle and the transmitral systolic pressure gradient, so the regurgitant volume appears smaller than awake. Surgeons must therefore interpret intraoperative severity with loading conditions restored, for example with phenylephrine. Positive pressure ventilation and volatile agents do not produce the described changes.
  19. Volume loss would not respond to repositioning, and the field shows no significant bleeding. A cannula thrombus is very unlikely under full anticoagulation. Upper abdominal cautery places current near the generator and can cause oversensing and inhibition. The reliable plan is reprogramming to asynchronous pacing at a safe rate with rate response off.
  21. The correct answer is C (Proceed, start low-dose levothyroxine, and anticipate slowed drug metabolism and greater hemodynamic sensitivity to anesthetics.): Mild to moderate hypothyroidism without myxedema does not increase mortality enough to delay urgent coronary surgery. Replacement should start at a low dose because rapid correction can provoke myocardial ischemia. Hypothyroid patients have reduced drug clearance, blunted baroreflexes, and heightened sensitivity to anesthetics. Waiting for euthyroidism is reserved for elective cases, and a large loading dose is hazardous.
  22. The correct answer is B (Advancing the cannula deep into the great cardiac vein, risking perforation and poor posterior septal protection.): The coronary sinus ostium lies near the tricuspid annulus, and the catheter tip should sit only 1 to 2 cm inside the sinus. Advancing too deep places the tip beyond the middle cardiac vein inlet, so the posterior septum and right ventricle receive poor cardioplegia, and risks perforation. A coronary sinus pressure of 30 to 50 mmHg confirms correct placement, and tip palpation is an accepted check.

Raising pump speed worsens septal shift and suction. With CVP already 24 mmHg, more volume further distends the failing right ventricle.
  24. The gradient moves from the valve to below it, producing hypotension, a phenomenon called the suicidal right ventricle. Leaflet avulsion or rupture would present with regurgitation or tamponade rather than a new subvalvular gradient.
  25. The correct answer is C (Use epicardial atrial and ventricular pacing wires and pace in a dual-chamber mode until conduction recovers.): The AV node sits at the apex of the triangle of Koch, bordered by the tendon of Todaro, the septal tricuspid annulus and the coronary sinus ostium, so sutures there can injure it. Block from edema or suture injury may persist for days, so atrial and ventricular wires with AV sequential pacing preserve atrial kick and cardiac output. Atropine acts on the node poorly when the injury is structural and does not replace a pacing plan. Ventricular-only pacing sacrifices atrial contribution to filling.

Leaving the sheath or lead in place partly tamponades the defect while blood is transfused and surgical repair is prepared.   27. The correct answer is A (Oxygen consumption is usually estimated from a nomogram instead of measured, and anesthesia alters it): The Fick principle divides oxygen consumption by the arteriovenous oxygen content difference. The content difference is measured, but oxygen consumption is typically assumed at about 125 mL/min/m2, and anesthesia, hypothermia and sepsis change it substantially. Hemoglobin is measured from the same samples, and the normal heart has no shunt of the kind described.

Longitudinal fibers lie in the subendocardium, which is affected early by toxins and ischemia, so strain often falls before ejection fraction does. Strain is more sensitive than ejection fraction for this early subendocardial dysfunction.

Preoperative exposure is associated with vasoplegia, bradycardia, atrioventricular block, and postoperative lung injury including respiratory distress syndrome, especially with high oxygen concentrations. Normal thyroid tests argue against clinically important hypothyroidism, and amiodarone prolongs, not shortens, the QT interval.

Permanent ischemic injury is rare, and bloodstream infection or pseudoaneurysm occur in small percentages. The other statements overstate the risk.

Peripheral cannulation (axillary, femoral) with bypass initiated and decompression of the heart before re-entry lets the surgeon open the chest with the aneurysm decompressed.   33. The correct answer is A (Retrograde infusion through the coronary sinus, or direct ostial delivery after aortotomy, avoiding left ventricular distension.): With a competent valve, root cardioplegia fills the coronary ostia, but with severe regurgitation it flows back into the ventricle, distending it and failing to perfuse the myocardium. Retrograde delivery through the coronary sinus, or direct ostial infusion once the aorta is opened, bypasses the leaking valve. Higher pressure worsens ventricular distension, and fibrillation without cardioplegia leaves the myocardium poorly protected.
  34. Early extubation restores negative-pressure breathing, and control of pain, acidosis and carbon dioxide prevents increases in pulmonary vascular resistance. Prolonged ventilation with high PEEP, high tidal volumes or hypercapnia all impair passive flow.

A middle rate is the compromise. Preload should be maintained for forward flow, and pulmonary resistance must stay low because pulmonary hypertension and right ventricular strain are common in this combination.

That unloads the ventricle, lowers wedge pressure and wall stress, and raises forward flow and mean arterial pressure. Repolarization, the T wave, ends close to the end of ejection and aortic valve closure, after which isovolumic relaxation begins. The P wave precedes atrial contraction rather than ventricular contraction. Mitral valve opening occurs after the T wave, during relaxation.

Placing a pad over the generator pocket can shunt current and risks device damage and reduced myocardial delivery. Two anterior pads give a poor vector across the ventricles.

This lowers net forward flow and leaves the ventricle overloaded.   71. The correct answer is B (Aortic valve closure is delayed so A2 follows P2, reversing the usual closure sequence.): Paradoxical splitting occurs when A2 is delayed beyond P2, as in left bundle branch block or severe aortic stenosis. During inspiration P2 moves later and merges with A2, so the split narrows, and during expiration the order is reversed so the split is heard. Delayed P2 produces normal or wide physiologic splitting. A fixed split that does not vary with respiration is the typical finding of an atrial septal defect.

  79. Very high pressures are not needed for stenoses, which are better managed by retrograde delivery, and pressures of 20 to 40 mmHg deliver too little solution.

  85. The correct answer is A (The morphologic right ventricle cannot sustain systemic afterload long term, so tricuspid regurgitation worsens with dilation.): After an atrial switch, the morphologic right ventricle supports the systemic circulation and the tricuspid valve is the systemic atrioventricular valve.   86. It is not a phosphodiesterase inhibitor and is not an endothelin agonist.

A pacemaker that was not reprogrammed and was exposed only to bipolar cautery below the umbilicus has a very low chance of malfunction. Removing a magnet restores the prior programmed behavior of an ICD, so no further interrogation is generally required if the course was uneventful.
  91. The correct answer is B (A low-titer ELISA with a negative functional assay makes HIT unlikely, so brief heparin is acceptable.): ELISAs detect antibodies that bind PF4-heparin, but optical densities between 0.4 and 1.0 have low positive predictive value, while the serotonin release assay detects platelet-activating antibodies and has high specificity. A negative functional assay with an intermediate clinical score makes clinically significant HIT unlikely. Delaying urgent surgery for 100 days is not justified, and positivity alone is not diagnostic.

This matters for a right ventricle that tolerates volume poorly. Large plasma volumes can precipitate right heart failure, and further protamine is not indicated when the activated clotting time is already near baseline.
  93. The correct answer is B (Use unfractionated heparin for bypass only, with non-heparin anticoagulants for all other perioperative needs): When HIT antibodies have cleared, a brief single re-exposure to heparin during bypass is acceptable because the risk of antibody recurrence is low, while avoiding heparin flushes, lines, and postoperative prophylaxis. Low molecular weight heparin cross-reacts with the antibody. Bivalirudin is reserved for positive antibodies and lacks a reversal agent.
  94. Large doses of vasodilating agents drop flow, and light sedation leaves the airway unprotected in a bleeding patient.
  95. The correct answer is A (DeBakey type III and Stanford type B, because the tear and flap spare the proximal aorta): Stanford classification depends on involvement of the ascending aorta: type A involves it and type B does not. DeBakey classification depends on the origin of the tear, so type III arises distal to the left subclavian artery and is confined to the descending aorta, with type IIIb extending below the diaphragm. DeBakey type II is confined to the ascending aorta. DeBakey type I starts in the ascending aorta and extends distally, and would be Stanford type A.

The correct answer is C (Anesthesia drugs, supplies and services count toward one episode target, so reducing low-value variation lowers spending): In a bundled episode, one payment covers the hospital stay, professional services and the postoperative window, so anesthesia resources are part of the cost the entity must manage against a target. Reducing unwarranted variation in drugs, devices and testing therefore lowers total episode cost. Separate time-unit payment outside the bundle and payment that increases with resource use both describe fee-for-service, not a bundle.
  97. The correct answer is A (It slows the sinus rate and AV nodal conduction, which can make induction difficult.): Dexmedetomidine reduces sympathetic outflow and increases vagal tone through central alpha-2 stimulation, slowing sinus node automaticity and AV nodal conduction. This can suppress or prevent induction of supraventricular tachycardia and prolong sinus node recovery. It does not shorten AV nodal refractoriness, and it is not known to speed accessory pathway conduction.
  98. The correct answer is C (Reduce the infusion to about 0.2 to 0.3 mcg/kg/min and consider omitting the loading dose): Milrinone is a phosphodiesterase III inhibitor that is cleared by the kidney, so its half life lengthens markedly in renal impairment. The infusion should be reduced, and the usual 50 mcg/kg loading dose may be omitted or reduced because of vasodilation and hypotension. Hepatic metabolism is not the main route, and a larger loading dose would worsen hypotension.

Meanwhile, bleeding is treated with fibrinogen and platelet correction. Very low flow promotes stasis and clot, and full-dose heparin during active surgical bleeding is unsafe.

  101. The correct answer is C (Nasopharyngeal, because it lies near the skull base and follows cerebral temperature changes quickly.): The nasopharyngeal probe sits near the base of the brain and tracks cerebral temperature closely during rapid cooling and rewarming.   102. The correct answer is B (Hold for 72 hours before surgery because reduced renal clearance prolongs the half-life of apixaban.): Apixaban is partly cleared by the kidney, and a creatinine clearance of 15 to 29 mL/min prolongs its half-life. For high bleeding risk surgery, the usual 48-hour hold is extended to about 72 hours in this renal range. A 24-hour hold applies only to low bleeding risk procedures with preserved renal function. Shorter holds raise the chance of anticoagulant effect at the time of heparinization and incision.
  103. The correct answer is B (Severe regurgitation, since vena contracta exceeds 0.6 cm and pressure half-time is below 200 ms): Severe aortic regurgitation is defined by a vena contracta above 0.6 cm, a jet width of 65 percent or more of the LVOT, a pressure half-time below 200 ms and holodiastolic flow reversal in the descending aorta. This patient meets every criterion. Moderate disease has a vena contracta of 0.3 to 0.6 cm and a half-time of 200 to 500 ms. The jet width cutoff for mild disease is below 25 percent.

The correct answer is C (Heart rate 80 to 100, preserved preload, and reduced systemic vascular resistance): Regurgitant volume depends on the systolic gradient across the valve and the regurgitant orifice, so lowering afterload and avoiding bradycardia reduce the fraction that goes backward. Preload must be maintained to keep the ventricle filled and the annulus from collapsing. Slow rates lengthen systole and fill time for regurgitation, and vasopressors raise the ventricular to atrial gradient.

A no-touch strategy with in situ arterial grafts removes aortic manipulation altogether. A side-biting clamp still manipulates the aorta and can release emboli. Lowering flow does not reduce the shear and mechanical trauma from the clamp.

Regional function in such segments can recover after restoring flow. A fixed defect that does not change would suggest scar.   114. Placing it just above the sinotubular junction crowds the clamp and root, and aiming at the innominate artery increases cerebral flow and embolic risk. The arch is a poor site.
  115. The correct answer is C (Acquired von Willebrand syndrome from shear-induced multimer cleavage, usually correcting within days after valve replacement.): High shear across a stenotic aortic valve unfolds von Willebrand factor and exposes it to ADAMTS13 cleavage, depleting the large multimers and producing mucocutaneous bleeding, known as Heyde syndrome. Once the stenosis is relieved the multimer pattern normalizes in most patients. The platelet count is normal and the multimer loss argues against immune thrombocytopenia or a hereditary deficiency.
  116. The correct answer is B (300 mg infused slowly over about 10 minutes with a vasopressor ready for hypotension): At 1 mg per 100 units, 30,000 units requires 30,000 / 100 = 300 mg of protamine. Rapid administration releases histamine and causes vasodilation, hypotension and pulmonary vasoconstriction, so it is given slowly over about 10 minutes with a vasopressor available. A rapid bolus of 150 mg is both underdosed and hemodynamically hazardous. Giving 450 mg corresponds to 1.5 mg per 100 units, which overdoses and can itself impair coagulation.

  118. The correct answer is A (Approximately 9,200 units, using a measured response of 0.87 seconds per unit/kg): The observed rise of 260 seconds per 300 units/kg gives a slope of 0.87 seconds per unit/kg. A rise of 360 seconds above baseline needs about 415 units/kg in total, so the shortfall is about 115 units/kg. Multiplying 115 by 80 kg gives approximately 9,200 units. The other values come from halving the shortfall or ignoring the response already achieved.
  119. Heparin does not act by activating protein C.

The correct answer is A (About 0.7 cm2, consistent with severe aortic stenosis): The simplified formula divides cardiac output in liters per minute by the square root of the mean gradient, or 4.2 divided by the square root of 36. The square root of 36 is 6, so the area is 4.2 divided by 6, or 0.7 cm2. The full Gorlin calculation gives 4200 divided by 70 times 0.33 times 44.3 times 6, which equals 0.68 cm2, in agreement.

  139. The correct answer is A (Moderate, with regurgitant volume of 50 mL and a fraction near 42 percent, below severe thresholds.): The regurgitant volume equals aortic stroke volume minus mitral stroke volume, which is 120 minus 70, or 50 mL. The regurgitant fraction is 50 divided by 120, about 42 percent. Severe regurgitation requires a regurgitant volume of at least 60 mL and a fraction of at least 50 percent, and mild is below 30 mL and 30 percent, so these values fall in the moderate range.

Hypothermia, hemodilution and thrombocytopenia all prolong it, so a value of 520 seconds may overestimate heparin effect.   145. The correct answer is A (Phenylephrine or vasopressin to restore systemic vascular resistance and reduce right-to-left shunting across the defect.): In Eisenmenger physiology pulmonary resistance is fixed and high, so shunt direction is governed by the ratio of systemic to pulmonary resistance. Vasodilation lowers systemic resistance and increases right-to-left shunting, causing the desaturation seen here. A vasoconstrictor such as phenylephrine or vasopressin raises systemic resistance and reverses the trend. Nitroglycerin and deeper volatile anesthesia further drop systemic resistance without meaningfully reducing the fixed pulmonary resistance.

Shock can therefore precipitate ventricular fibrillation that is hard to treat. Digoxin does not block calcium channels, and increased vagal tone is not the main concern at this toxicity level.

  156.
