  157. The correct answer is C (Keep arterial outlet within 10 degrees C of venous return and below 37 degrees C): Rapid or excessive rewarming causes gas bubble formation from falling gas solubility and cerebral hyperthermia, both associated with neurologic injury. Standard practice limits the arterial outlet to venous return gradient to about 10 degrees C and keeps the outlet below 37 degrees C. An outlet of 42 degrees C risks cerebral hyperthermia and a 20 degree C gradient promotes bubble formation. Rewarming also should be gradual, with the nasopharyngeal temperature rising slowly toward 36 degrees C.
  158. The correct answer is A (Urgent surgical repair, since ascending intramural hematoma can progress to dissection or rupture): Intramural hematoma of the ascending aorta is treated like a type A dissection because a substantial fraction progress to classic dissection, pericardial rupture or tamponade. Urgent open repair is therefore recommended for most surgical candidates. Delayed imaging with medical therapy is reserved for selected stable patients with thin hematomas, which is not the default. Endovascular repair is impractical because no entry tear exists and the ascending aorta has no suitable landing zone.
  159. The correct answer is C (Avoid advancing wires or catheters into the right heart, and keep defibrillator pads applied.): The atrialized right ventricle and frequent accessory pathways make mechanical stimulation by wires or catheters a trigger for atrial arrhythmia. Tachycardia then raises right atrial pressure and increases right-to-left shunting across the patent foramen ovale, lowering saturation. Pulmonary artery catheters add little and carry a high risk. Short catheters and ultrasound guidance, with pads in place, reduce harm.
  160. The correct answer is A (The left recurrent laryngeal nerve, which loops beneath the aortic arch lateral to the ligamentum arteriosum.): The left recurrent laryngeal nerve wraps under the aortic arch beside the ligamentum arteriosum, so dissection there causes vocal cord paralysis with a breathy voice and aspiration. The left phrenic nerve runs anterior to the hilum and would produce an elevated hemidiaphragm, which the radiograph excludes. The right recurrent nerve loops beneath the right subclavian artery and lies away from this operative field.
  161. The correct answer is C (Observation with surveillance echocardiography, since excision is reserved for symptomatic or mobile lesions.): Papillary fibroelastomas are benign and can embolize, so left-sided symptomatic tumors and large, mobile lesions are excised. A small, nonmobile incidental tumor in an asymptomatic patient may be followed with imaging. Anticoagulation does not shrink the lesion and valve replacement is unnecessary because excision usually spares the valve.
  162. The correct answer is C (Plan cardiopulmonary bypass, with possible circulatory arrest, and monitor the thrombus continuously with TEE during manipulation.): A thrombus reaching the right atrium risks pulmonary tumor embolism and obstruction of the tricuspid valve, so surgeons often use bypass and sometimes circulatory arrest, with TEE tracking the thrombus. Advancing a catheter through the right atrium can dislodge the thrombus. Antifibrinolytics do not control a tumor thrombus and can add thrombotic risk.
  163. The correct answer is B (Disclose promptly to patient and family, explain the facts and monitoring plan, apologize, and report): Professional and institutional standards call for timely, honest disclosure of an adverse event or error that reached the patient, even without harm, along with an apology, a description of the monitoring plan and a safety event report. Delaying disclosure until harm appears erodes trust. Documentation and risk notification alone leave the patient unaware of a relevant event.
  164. The correct answer is A (Known esophageal stricture with progressive dysphagia to solid food): Absolute contraindications include esophageal stricture, tumor, perforation, laceration, diverticulum and active upper gastrointestinal bleeding. A treated Barrett esophagus without symptoms and a small hiatal hernia are relative considerations that call for care but not an automatic prohibition.
  165. The correct answer is B (Recalibrate the model on local data, assess dataset shift, and monitor performance after deployment): Discrimination can remain good while calibration fails, and here predictions overestimate risk by about twofold, a sign of dataset shift. Recalibration to local data and ongoing monitoring correct this before clinical use. A high area under the curve does not guarantee accurate probabilities, and a small drop from 0.85 to 0.83 does not make the model invalid.
  166. The correct answer is A (Use low airway pressures, pad joints in neutral alignment, and avoid forceful jaw or neck manipulation): Marfan syndrome brings apical bleb and pneumothorax risk, so ventilation should avoid barotrauma. Ligamentous laxity predisposes to temporomandibular, cervical and limb joint injury during laryngoscopy and positioning. Dural ectasia makes neuraxial spread and dural puncture unpredictable rather than reliable, and high pressures would raise pneumothorax risk.
  167. The correct answer is B (The patient with Fontan circulation and protein-losing enteropathy needing open colectomy.): Guidelines advise that patients with complex or cyanotic lesions, Fontan circulation, pulmonary hypertension or ventricular dysfunction undergo intermediate or high-risk surgery at a center with congenital expertise. The Fontan patient with protein-losing enteropathy has all of these features. The two other patients have simple, repaired lesions and low-risk procedures that can be done locally.
  168. The correct answer is C (Move to anterior-posterior pads with firm pressure, and give ibutilide or amiodarone before repeating the shock.): An anterior-posterior vector sends more current through the atria, firm pad pressure reduces thoracic impedance, and an antiarrhythmic such as ibutilide or amiodarone lowers the atrial defibrillation threshold. Lowering energy cannot help after failed 200 J shocks. Anterior-only pads give a poor atrial vector, and adenosine has no effect on atrial defibrillation threshold.
  169. The correct answer is C (Use unfractionated heparin for bypass only, avoiding all other exposure including flushes and postoperative prophylaxis.): HIT antibodies disappear within months, and when assays are negative a brief intraoperative heparin exposure for bypass is acceptable and better studied than alternatives. All other heparin exposure must be avoided, because renewed exposure outside the operation could restart antibody formation. Bivalirudin lacks a reversal agent and has stasis problems. Postoperative heparin prophylaxis would add avoidable exposure.
  170. The correct answer is B (Total artificial heart as a bridge to transplant, replacing both ventricles and removing the arrhythmic substrate.): A total artificial heart excises both ventricles and thus treats biventricular failure, intractable ventricular arrhythmia, and ventricular thrombus together, serving as a bridge to transplant. A left ventricular assist device depends on the native right ventricle and apex. ECMO is a temporary support and cannot be used for long-term home therapy.
  171. The correct answer is B (Class C with 11 points, indicating prohibitive on-pump risk and consideration of less invasive alternatives): Points are bilirubin over 3 (3), albumin under 2.8 (3), INR 1.7 to 2.3 (2), mild ascites (2), and no encephalopathy (1), totaling 11, which is class C. Class C cirrhosis carries very high mortality with bypass, so transcatheter or off-pump options deserve consideration.
  172. The correct answer is A (The sinoatrial node artery, which arises from the right coronary artery in most patients.): The sinoatrial node artery arises from the right coronary artery in about 55 to 60 percent of people, ascends along the anterior right atrium and encircles the superior cavoatrial junction. Injury or kinking from cannulation or atriotomy produces node ischemia, loss of sinus rhythm and a junctional escape. The LAD does not supply the sinus node. A vagal branch crossing the superior vena cava is not a recognized anatomic cause.
  173. The correct answer is C (Tissue factor in shed blood drives thrombin generation, consuming platelets and factors, with hemolysis adding injury.): Blood aspirated from the wound is exposed to tissue factor, air and high shear in the suction, which activates thrombin generation and platelets and stimulates fibrinolysis. Red cell lysis adds free hemoglobin and further platelet activation. Heparin coatings do not release heparin into the patient, and there is no specific factor VII deficiency from the filter, so the picture follows suction-driven consumption.
  174. The correct answer is B (A fully magnetically levitated centrifugal rotor with wide blood-flow gaps generates low shear stress): The rotor is suspended by magnetic fields without contact bearings, and the wide gaps lower shear stress on blood cells. That reduces hemolysis and pump thrombosis in comparison with earlier axial pumps. Bearing contact generates shear and heat, and the device is a continuous-flow pump rather than a pneumatic pulsatile one.
  175. The correct answer is B (Generally not, since the proxy applies substituted judgment, and only unforeseen circumstances justify reinterpreting the directive): A proxy acts as the patient's voice and must use substituted judgment, following the patient's known wishes. The proxy's own preferences do not outrank a clear directive, though circumstances the patient could not have anticipated may warrant careful reinterpretation with the team. Neither proxy authority nor clinician opinion alone overrides documented patient wishes.
  176. The correct answer is C (Subxiphoid transdiaphragmatic epicardial radiofrequency ablation of the posterior left atrium, then endocardial catheter ablation): The convergent procedure combines a subxiphoid, transdiaphragmatic approach to ablate the posterior left atrial wall from the epicardial surface with endocardial catheter ablation, usually pulmonary vein isolation and gap closure. It does not require sternotomy or thoracoscopy. The other descriptions refer to thoracoscopic hybrid procedures and surgical maze operations.
  177. The correct answer is A (Continuous telemetry with echocardiography to evaluate wall motion and valve injury, and hospital admission for monitoring.): Cardiac contusion with arrhythmia and elevated troponin warrants admission with continuous telemetry and echocardiography to look for regional wall motion abnormality, valve damage or effusion. Troponin rise after blunt trauma is usually from myocardial injury, not atherosclerotic plaque rupture, so emergent angiography is not first line. Prophylactic pacing is not indicated, since advanced block is uncommon and treated if it occurs.
  178. The correct answer is B (Immediate operative decompression, inducing only after preparation and draping, with minimal doses of ketamine): Penetrating tamponade is treated by surgical decompression and repair, and delay for imaging lowers survival. Induction can precipitate arrest, so the surgeon should be ready. Needle drainage often fails because blood clots, and it should not replace surgery.
  179. The correct answer is A (Severe, because vena contracta above 0.6 cm and pressure half-time under 200 milliseconds meet criteria.): Pressure half-time is the time for the peak velocity to fall by the square root of two, here from 4.5 to about 3.2 m/s, which occurs at about 180 milliseconds. A half-time under 200 milliseconds and a vena contracta above 0.6 cm indicate severe aortic regurgitation. Moderate and mild grades would require a vena contracta of 0.6 cm or less and slower velocity decay.
  180. The correct answer is A (Discuss it with the patient and surgeon beforehand: full suspension, procedure-directed limits, or values-based limits): Professional guidance requires required reconsideration of a DNR order before anesthesia, because interventions such as vasopressors and defibrillation are often part of routine care. The team and patient should decide among full suspension, procedure-directed limits or values-based limits. Automatic suspension disregards autonomy, and applying the order unchanged may deny expected intraoperative treatment.
  181. The correct answer is C (Reduce tidal volume to about 410 mL to keep plateau pressure below 30 cmH2O): The ARDSNet strategy sets tidal volume by predicted body weight, not actual weight, at 6 mL/kg (68 kg x 6 = about 410 mL), titrating to a plateau pressure below 30 cmH2O. Using 8 mL/kg or accepting a plateau of 35 cmH2O leaves the lung exposed to excess stretch. Adding PEEP at a fixed tidal volume raises plateau pressure rather than lowering it.
  182. The correct answer is B (Esmolol to heart rate 60 to 80, then vasodilator for systolic 100 to 120 mmHg): Beta blockade lowers heart rate and the rate of pressure rise (dP/dt) first, which reduces shear stress on the dissected aortic wall. Starting a vasodilator before beta blockade causes reflex tachycardia and raises dP/dt, which can propagate the dissection. Guidelines target a heart rate of 60 to 80 beats/min and a systolic pressure of 100 to 120 mmHg as end organ perfusion allows. Nitroprusside to a mean of 70 mmHg overshoots the target and omits rate control, and a systolic target of 140 mmHg with a fast rate leaves the wall under high dP/dt.
  183. The correct answer is B (Doppler during cuff deflation; opening pressure approximates mean pressure, target 70 to 80 mmHg): With continuous flow the pulse is minimal or absent, so Doppler is used to detect the first return of flow when the cuff is deflated. That opening pressure approximates mean arterial pressure, and the usual goal is 70 to 80 mmHg, generally not above 90. Korotkoff sounds are unreliable and oximeter waveforms do not measure pressure.
  184. The correct answer is A (Continuous transesophageal echocardiography viewing the pericardial space, plus a radial arterial line for beat-to-beat pressure.): Extraction perforation can cause tamponade within seconds, and TEE shows a new pericardial effusion before hypotension, with the arterial line confirming hemodynamic effect. Central venous pressure rises late and is unreliable with sheath manipulation in the superior vena cava. Pulse oximetry and end-tidal carbon dioxide change late, after cardiac output has already fallen.
  185. The correct answer is B (Radial artery graft spasm, treated with vasodilators such as nitroglycerin, diltiazem or milrinone.): Radial grafts are muscular and prone to vasospasm after bypass, and flow that improves with a vasodilator points to spasm instead of a fixed mechanical defect. Treatment is intravenous nitroglycerin, a calcium channel blocker or milrinone. A fixed anastomotic stenosis would not respond to vasodilation, and a left main dissection would produce more extensive changes.
  186. The correct answer is B (Slow the heart rate with esmolol to lengthen diastolic coronary perfusion time while preserving perfusion pressure.): Coronary flow occurs mainly in diastole, and tachycardia shortens diastole while raising oxygen demand, so slowing the rate improves the supply and demand balance in a patient with good ventricular function. Perfusion pressure must be maintained while doing so. Raising the heart rate worsens ischemia. Extra preload increases wall tension and demand in a ventricle that is already adequately filled.
  187. The correct answer is A (Flow of 200 to 300 mL/min with venous pressure held at or below 25 mmHg): Retrograde cerebral perfusion is run at about 200 to 300 mL/min with venous pressure kept at or below 25 mmHg, because higher pressures cause cerebral edema and injury. Its benefits are mainly cooling, flushing of air and debris, and modest metabolic support, not full oxygen delivery. Higher flows and pressures raise edema risk without added benefit.
  188. The correct answer is C (Blunted heparin response from low antithrombin activity, so antithrombin concentrate is preferred over more heparin.): The curve rises only about 0.55 seconds per unit/kg, which is a blunted slope and a recognized pattern in patients treated with heparin infusions who have antithrombin depletion. An ACT of 380 seconds is below the 480 second threshold for bypass. Antithrombin concentrate or plasma restores heparin effect, while repeated large doses add little. Poor mixing would not explain a linear, blunted curve.
  189. The correct answer is A (Leftward septal shift reduces LV compliance and filling, lowering stroke volume through ventricular interdependence.): When RV pressure or volume overload exceeds the pericardial and septal limits, the septum flattens or bows leftward, reducing LV cavity size and compliance. LV filling falls and stroke volume drops, even though LV contractility is preserved. A small, hyperdynamic LV argues against primary LV failure. An effusion would restrict both ventricles and is not suggested by the echo findings.
  190. The correct answer is C (Give magnesium sulfate 2 g intravenously and start insulin to keep glucose below about 180 mg/dL): Hypomagnesemia after bypass is common and predisposes to arrhythmia, and intravenous magnesium reduces ectopy. Hyperglycemia is associated with worse outcomes, so insulin to maintain glucose below about 180 mg/dL is advised. Very tight control to 80 to 110 mg/dL increases hypoglycemia without benefit, and hyperglycemia is not protective.
  191. The correct answer is C (Regional tamponade from clot compressing the left atrium, requiring prompt surgical re-exploration): After cardiac surgery, loculated clot can compress individual chambers without the classic diffuse effusion, and TEE is more sensitive than transthoracic imaging. Falling blood pressure, rising central venous pressure and abruptly reduced chest tube output indicate that clot is retained rather than drained. Right ventricular failure would produce a dilated, hypokinetic right ventricle rather than a compressing mass. Hypovolemia would lower filling pressures and not raise central venous pressure.
  192. The correct answer is A (Continue aspirin, stop clopidogrel five days before surgery, and consider cangrelor bridging for high thrombotic risk.): Aspirin is continued through CABG because it reduces graft and stent thrombosis, while clopidogrel is stopped about five days before to limit bleeding, with short acting intravenous cangrelor considered when stent thrombosis risk is high, as with a recent stent. Stopping both agents leaves the recent stent unprotected. Continuing clopidogrel to the day of surgery increases bleeding and platelet transfusion needs.
  193. The correct answer is C (V1 receptor stimulation constricts systemic arterioles with little rise in pulmonary resistance, sparing right ventricular afterload.): Vasopressin acts on V1 receptors to raise systemic vascular resistance and, at usual doses, has little effect on pulmonary resistance and may even promote pulmonary vasodilation through nitric oxide release. That helps a failing right ventricle that is sensitive to afterload. It does not act on alpha receptors and it does not inhibit nitric oxide.
  194. The correct answer is A (Inhaled pulmonary vasodilator, milrinone, vasopressin for perfusion pressure, and stopping further fluid loading.): The exhibit shows a rising CVP with a falling arterial pressure, the pattern of right ventricular failure from overload. Treatment lowers right ventricular afterload with an inhaled pulmonary vasodilator, supports contractility with milrinone, and maintains coronary perfusion pressure with vasopressin while avoiding further volume. Added fluid distends the failing ventricle, and a pure vasoconstrictor does not address the primary problem.
  195. The correct answer is B (Chronic severe aortic regurgitation with rapid diastolic runoff into the left ventricle): The tracing shows a systolic pressure of 158 mmHg and a diastolic of 42 mmHg, giving a pulse pressure of 116 mmHg with a brisk rise and rapid fall. This pattern results from a large stroke volume ejected into the aorta followed by diastolic runoff into the ventricle. Aortic stenosis gives a slow upstroke with a narrow pulse pressure, and obstructive cardiomyopathy gives a spike and dome contour without a low diastolic pressure.
  196. The correct answer is B (Raise mean arterial pressure above 90 mmHg with vasopressor and drain CSF to about 10 mmHg): Delayed deficits from spinal cord ischemia are time sensitive. Spinal cord perfusion pressure equals mean arterial pressure minus CSF pressure, so pressure is raised and CSF drained at once, with drainage keeping the pressure at or below 10 mmHg. Imaging should not delay treatment, and corticosteroids lack evidence as primary therapy.
  197. The correct answer is C (Hemodilution by a lactated crystalloid prime lowers potassium and bicarbonate and adds lactate, without any hypoperfusion.): Venous saturation of 82 percent shows oxygen delivery is adequate, and chloride of 104 mmol/L is normal, which excludes hypoperfusion and a saline prime effect. The prime dilutes potassium and bicarbonate, producing a mild metabolic acidosis, and lactated Ringer's supplies exogenous lactate. This pattern is expected at onset and is not treated by flow changes.
  198. The correct answer is B (About 32 percent, because 2,352 mL of red cells is diluted into 7,400 mL.): Blood volume is 80 x 70 = 5,600 mL, and red cell volume is 5,600 x 0.42 = 2,352 mL. After adding 1,800 mL of prime, total volume is 7,400 mL, and the hematocrit is 2,352 / 7,400 = 0.318. The lower value assumes a larger circulating volume than the numbers support. The higher value assumes incomplete mixing, which does not occur.
  199. The correct answer is B (Add norepinephrine, which constricts capacitance veins and recruits unstressed volume.): Norepinephrine has alpha and beta-1 activity and constricts capacitance veins, mobilizing unstressed volume into the circulation and raising venous return. Phenylephrine acts mainly on arterioles and produces a smaller venous effect, so increasing it raises afterload with little added preload. Milrinone is a venodilator and an inodilator and would lower filling pressures further.
  200. The correct answer is C (General anesthesia with invasive monitoring and blood available, anticipating reentry injury and bleeding): Sternal reexploration may reach the mediastinum, where grafts or the right ventricle lie close to the sternum, so reentry injury and bleeding are real risks. General anesthesia with arterial monitoring and crossmatched blood is appropriate. Parasternal blocks alone cannot cover an unstable sternum, and sedation without airway control is unsafe for this procedure.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Ischemia and reperfusion injury with calcium overload cause reversible stunning that often recovers over days.): Longer ischemic times increase the severity of stunning, which is contractile dysfunction without necrosis, driven by oxygen free radicals and calcium handling abnormalities during reperfusion. Function usually returns gradually, with inotropic support bridging the interval. Complete irreversible loss across the ventricle would show new infarct signs, and cardioplegia potassium is washed out quickly after the clamp is removed.
  2. The correct answer is B (Clarify which products and techniques he accepts, then optimize hemoglobin and use cell salvage and antifibrinolytics): A competent adult's refusal is binding, and many Witnesses accept certain fractions, cell salvage or circuit-connected techniques, so individual preferences should be clarified. Preoperative hemoglobin optimization, cell salvage, antifibrinolytics and meticulous hemostasis lower risk. Transfusing against a documented refusal violates autonomy, and refusing to operate is not required when informed consent is obtained.
  3. The correct answer is C (Minimal change in heart rate, because the baroreceptor reflex arc to the donor node is interrupted.): The carotid and aortic baroreceptors normally respond to a fall in pressure by increasing sympathetic outflow and withdrawing vagal tone. In a transplanted heart those efferent nerves are cut, so the sinus node does not receive the signal and the rate stays near the intrinsic 90 to 100 per minute. Circulating catecholamines may produce a slow, delayed rise. Neither reflex tachycardia nor bradycardia is expected.
  4. The correct answer is C (Severe ARDS, since PaO2/FiO2 ratio is below 100 with PEEP of at least 5): PaO2 62 mmHg on FiO2 0.80 gives a ratio of about 78, which is below 100, and with PEEP 10, bilateral opacities, onset within a week, and no hydrostatic explanation this is severe ARDS. Moderate ARDS requires a ratio of 101 to 200. A normal occlusion pressure and ejection fraction argue against cardiogenic edema.
  5. The correct answer is B (Nonischemic dilated cardiomyopathy, with a midwall septal stripe and no coronary obstruction): A linear midwall stripe of enhancement in the septum, with no subendocardial involvement and no coronary stenosis, is the classic pattern of dilated nonischemic cardiomyopathy. Ischemic scar begins in the subendocardium and follows a vascular territory. Amyloid produces diffuse subendocardial or transmural enhancement.
  6. The correct answer is B (Relying on a right radial line alone to judge systemic perfusion pressure during cannula flow.): With right axillary or innominate cannulation, jet flow and altered arm perfusion can make right radial pressures falsely high or unrepresentative of systemic pressure. A second site in the left arm or femoral artery allows comparison and detects right arm hyperperfusion or malperfusion. Using two sites is recommended, not a hazard.
  7. The correct answer is C (Ventricular septal rupture, with an intra-aortic balloon pump and vasodilator while surgery is arranged): The oxygen saturation rises from 66 percent in the right atrium to 84 percent in the right ventricle, which is a left-to-right shunt at the ventricular level. Afterload reduction with balloon support lowers shunt flow. Raising afterload would increase the shunt, and a free wall rupture would not produce a step-up.
  8. The correct answer is B (Stop warfarin 5 days before surgery and start therapeutic heparin once the INR falls under 2.0): A mechanical mitral valve with atrial fibrillation is a high thrombotic risk setting, so bridging is indicated. Warfarin is held about 5 days to allow the INR to normalize, and heparin begins when the INR is subtherapeutic, then stops hours before surgery. A shorter hold leaves residual anticoagulant effect, and starting heparin on day one prolongs the exposure without benefit.
  9. The correct answer is A (Status 2, which includes a percutaneous endovascular device, an intra-aortic balloon pump, or sustained ventricular arrhythmia.): The 2018 allocation policy places patients on venoarterial ECMO, nondischargeable surgical biventricular support or devices with life threatening arrhythmia at Status 1. Percutaneous endovascular support, an intra-aortic balloon pump, a nondischargeable surgical LVAD and ventricular arrhythmia fall in Status 2. Status 4 and above describe more stable patients, so it does not match a patient who requires temporary mechanical support.
  10. The correct answer is A (The septal jet draws the cusp into the defect, so closure with cusp resuspension is favored.): In a perimembranous or outlet defect, the high-velocity left-to-right jet creates a Venturi effect that pulls the adjacent, unsupported right coronary cusp into the defect. The cusp prolapses and produces an eccentric aortic regurgitation jet. Early defect closure with cusp repair is favored to halt progression. The normal root excludes root-dilation causes, and nothing in the history suggests rheumatic valve disease.
  11. The correct answer is B (Tranexamic acid 1 g intravenously to inhibit plasmin driven clot lysis.): The tracing shows a normal clotting time and amplitude, with a markedly raised percent lysis at 30 minutes, indicating hyperfibrinolysis. An antifibrinolytic such as tranexamic acid blocks plasminogen binding and stops the clot being degraded. The maximum amplitude is normal, so platelets are not the problem. The reaction time is normal, so factor replacement from plasma would add volume without benefit.
  12. The correct answer is B (Paces atrium and ventricle, senses atrium and ventricle, and responds by both triggering and inhibiting.): In the NBG code, the first letter identifies the chamber paced, the second the chamber sensed, and the third the response to sensing. D indicates both chambers in the first two positions and dual response in the third, meaning inhibition in the atrium and triggering of ventricular pacing after atrial sensing.
  13. The correct answer is A (Replace the oxygenator on a planned basis, since progressive clot impairs gas exchange and risks hemolysis.): A rising transmembrane pressure drop with falling postoxygenator PaO2 and visible fibrin shows a failing membrane lung. Planned change-out is safer than waiting for emergent failure. Escalated heparin does not dissolve established clot and adds bleeding risk, and higher flow raises the pressure drop further.
  14. The correct answer is C (Air in the right coronary artery, whose anterior ostium collects aortic air, clearing with higher pressure.): Air in the aortic root is carried preferentially into the right coronary ostium, the most anterior, producing inferior ST elevation and right or inferior dysfunction that often resolves with increased perfusion pressure. A protamine pulmonary vasoconstriction reaction would raise pulmonary pressure, and a graft to the left anterior descending territory would produce anterior changes.
  15. The correct answer is A (Deep valve frame compresses the membranous septum bundle; preexisting right bundle block is the strongest predictor): The atrioventricular conduction bundle runs in the membranous septum just beneath the aortic annulus, where a deeply seated or oversized valve and post-dilation compress it. Preexisting right bundle branch block is the strongest predictor of high-grade block, and self-expanding valves carry greater risk. Sinoatrial ischemia does not cause this block, and there is no thermal injury mechanism.
  16. The correct answer is C (Roller pumps keep turning and create extreme negative inflow pressure, while centrifugal output falls): A roller pump is positive displacement, so with obstructed inflow it continues to pump and can create very high negative pressure, which cavitates dissolved gas and can collapse or rupture tubing. A centrifugal pump is preload and afterload dependent, and its flow falls when inflow is restricted, so it cannot produce the same extreme negative pressure. The idea that both behave alike ignores this difference.
  17. The correct answer is B (Intravenous lidocaine given for analgesia, a sodium channel blocker that raises the threshold for reentry.): Lidocaine slows conduction and depresses excitability in scarred myocardium, which can make scar-related ventricular tachycardia hard to induce. Isoproterenol enhances automaticity and facilitates induction rather than suppressing it. Ketamine increases sympathetic tone and does not lengthen the action potential, so it does not suppress reentry.
  18. The correct answer is B (Reexplore, because output exceeded 200 mL/hour for several consecutive hours with normal coagulation): Common criteria for reexploration include output above 200 mL per hour for 2 to 4 consecutive hours, more than 400 mL in any hour, or a sudden surge. The chart shows output above 200 mL per hour for five consecutive hours with normal coagulation, indicating surgical bleeding. Waiting or giving blood products delays definitive control.
  19. The correct answer is C (Place an arterial line, since cuff mismatch gives unpredictable errors and misses rapid changes.): A cuff too small for the arm circumference overestimates pressure, and conical arms make fit unpredictable. Forearm cuffs can also read higher than upper arm values. Because off-pump grafting involves rapid hemodynamic swings, beat-to-beat invasive monitoring is the best option.
  20. The correct answer is B (Upregulated beta receptors producing exaggerated sympathetic responsiveness after abrupt removal of blockade): Chronic beta blockade increases receptor density, so abrupt withdrawal unmasks heightened catecholamine sensitivity with tachycardia, hypertension and ischemia. Receptor downregulation would give the opposite picture, a blunted response. Beta blockers do not suppress the adrenal axis in a way that would explain this presentation.
  21. The correct answer is C (Suspend tachyarrhythmia detection with a programmer or magnet and apply external defibrillation pads before the incision): Surgery above the umbilicus with monopolar cautery places the ICD in the field of electromagnetic interference, which can be oversensed as ventricular fibrillation and trigger shocks. Detection should be suspended before the incision, with defibrillation pads in place so rescue therapy is available while the device is off. Short cautery bursts reduce but do not remove oversensing risk. Waiting until bypass leaves the whole pre-bypass dissection unprotected.
  22. The correct answer is B (Flecainide, because class IC agents raised mortality in postinfarction patients with ventricular dysfunction): The CAST trial showed excess mortality with flecainide and encainide in patients with prior infarction and ventricular dysfunction, and class IC drugs are avoided in structural heart disease. Amiodarone is a standard choice in reduced ejection fraction. Beta-blockers are used in compensated patients and also reduce postoperative atrial fibrillation.
  23. The correct answer is A (Postoperative day 8, sinus rate 36 beats/min with dizziness and documented pauses of 4 seconds.): Permanent pacing is Class I for symptomatic sinus node dysfunction with documented bradycardia or pauses, and postoperative dysfunction is usually observed for several days to a week because many patients recover. The asymptomatic and drug-related examples do not meet that threshold.
  24. The correct answer is A (Manage to the uncorrected 37 C values; no carbon dioxide is added and results are acceptable): Under alpha stat management the uncorrected values measured at 37 C are targeted, here pH 7.40 and PaCO2 40 mmHg, which keeps protein charge state normal. Corrected values at 28 C are pH about 7.53 and PaCO2 about 27 mmHg, as expected physiologically. Adding carbon dioxide to reach corrected pH 7.40 is pH stat management, and a measured PaCO2 of 60 mmHg is not a target for alpha stat.
  25. The correct answer is B (Heart block progresses about 2 percent yearly, and systemic valve regurgitation predicts systemic ventricular failure.): In this lesion the atrioventricular node and bundle are displaced anteriorly and the system is fragile, so spontaneous block develops at about 2 percent per year and surgery carries a high risk of block. The morphologic right ventricle serves the systemic circulation, and regurgitation of its tricuspid valve is the strongest marker of impending ventricular failure. The left ventricle is subpulmonary.
  26. The correct answer is C (Warfarin with an INR target of 2.5 for 3 to 6 months, then aspirin alone): Guidelines support a vitamin K antagonist with INR 2.5 for 3 to 6 months after surgical bioprosthetic aortic replacement when bleeding risk is low, because early leaflet and sewing ring thrombosis risk is highest in that window. Afterward aspirin alone is sufficient in sinus rhythm. Lifelong warfarin at a higher target adds bleeding without benefit, and omitting the early vitamin K antagonist ignores the early thrombosis window.
  27. The correct answer is B (Synchronized biphasic shock starting at 50 to 100 joules, escalating if needed): Atrial flutter is organized and converts at lower energy than atrial fibrillation, so an initial synchronized biphasic shock of 50 to 100 joules is recommended, with escalation if needed. Starting at 200 joules delivers more energy than needed and increases myocardial injury. An unsynchronized shock is reserved for pulseless or polymorphic rhythms, and synchronization avoids the vulnerable period in this organized rhythm.
  28. The correct answer is B (The left nerve hooks around the aortic arch beside the ligamentum arteriosum, within the surgical field): The left recurrent laryngeal nerve branches from the vagus and loops under the aortic arch beside the ligamentum arteriosum before ascending in the tracheoesophageal groove. Arch and descending aortic dissection or clamping therefore exposes it to direct injury. The right nerve loops under the right subclavian artery and is far from the field.
  29. The correct answer is C (Use modest tidal volumes, low PEEP and a short inspiratory time to limit mean intrathoracic pressure.): Pulmonary blood flow in a Fontan is passive and depends on the gradient from systemic venous pressure to the left atrium. Positive pressure ventilation, high PEEP and long inspiratory times raise intrathoracic pressure and reduce the gradient. Hypercapnia increases pulmonary vascular resistance, which also impairs flow. Low mean airway pressure and normocapnia, with early return to spontaneous breathing when feasible, are best.
  30. The correct answer is A (Start an intravenous insulin infusion targeting a glucose below 180 mg/dL, with frequent monitoring.): Perioperative hyperglycemia increases infection and neurologic injury, and the STS guideline recommends intravenous insulin to keep glucose under 180 mg/dL. Very tight targets of 80 to 110 mg/dL increase hypoglycemia and did not improve outcomes in large trials. Waiting for a value of 300 mg/dL leaves patients above target for too long.
  31. The correct answer is A (Reduced venous return and higher pulmonary vascular resistance at high lung volumes worsen right ventricular output): Positive pressure ventilation raises intrathoracic pressure, which impedes systemic venous return and lowers preload. Overdistended alveoli compress intra alveolar vessels and increase pulmonary vascular resistance, loading a dysfunctional right ventricle that is already volume overloaded. Recruitment helps only when lung volume is below functional residual capacity.
  32. The correct answer is C (Restrictive filling with elevated left atrial pressure, so volume loading carries high risk of pulmonary congestion.): An E/A ratio above 2 with a short deceleration time, E/e' above 14, an enlarged left atrium and blunted pulmonary vein systolic flow indicate grade 3 diastolic dysfunction with high left atrial pressure. The stiff ventricle tolerates volume poorly, and tachycardia reduces filling time. Normal pressure patterns belong to grade 1 dysfunction.
  33. The correct answer is C (A sustained inflation recruitment maneuver to 30 to 40 cmH2O under direct vision before separation): Atelectasis follows lung collapse, absent ventilation and surgical compression, and contributes to postbypass shunt and hypoxemia. A sustained inflation recruitment performed before weaning, watched by the surgeon to avoid graft or mammary injury, reopens collapsed lung. Continuous large tidal volume ventilation on bypass is not recommended, and delaying reinflation until after protamine prolongs the atelectasis.

Positional obstruction explains her presyncope on standing. Tachycardia and underfilling worsen the gradient, and aggressive diuresis reduces the preload that keeps the orifice open.
  35. The first response is to raise distal perfusion pressure and optimize CSF drainage, and also to consider hemoglobin and reimplanting intercostal arteries. Anesthetic depth has been constant, so changing it does not explain an abrupt drop. Waiting while assuming a technical cause wastes the short window in which the cord can be rescued.
  36. The correct answer is C (Raise mean arterial pressure toward 70 to 80 mmHg, because autoregulation shifts rightward in chronic hypertension): Chronic hypertension and carotid stenosis shift the lower limit of autoregulation to a higher pressure, so a mean pressure of 45 mmHg is likely below it, as the oximetry decline shows. Raising pressure toward an individualized target of roughly 70 to 80 mmHg restores flow. Hypothermia reduces demand but does not remove the right shift. Pressures above 100 mmHg add aortic atheroma embolization risk without further benefit.
  37. The correct answer is A (Dynamic outflow obstruction from systolic anterior motion, provoked by hypovolemia and inotropes after afterload relief): After relief of aortic stenosis, a small hypertrophic ventricle with underfilling and catecholamine stimulation can develop systolic anterior motion with dynamic obstruction. Management is volume, phenylephrine and stopping the inotrope, with beta blockade if needed. Mismatch produces a high prosthetic gradient with normal leaflet motion, not SAM. Right ventricular failure would show high CVP.
  38. The correct answer is B (Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate with low-dose intravenous vitamin K 1 to 2.5 mg, accepting thrombotic risk.): Prothrombin complex concentrate reverses the vitamin K dependent factors within minutes, which fits an operation needed within two hours. A small intravenous dose of vitamin K of 1 to 2.5 mg sustains the effect after the concentrate wears off. High doses are avoided in a mechanical valve because they cause prolonged warfarin resistance and raise valve thrombosis risk when anticoagulation is resumed. Oral vitamin K takes 12 to 24 hours to act, and plasma needs large volumes with slow, incomplete correction.
  39. The correct answer is C (Limit disclosure to the minimum necessary for the educational purpose and withhold unrelated history and identifiers): For purposes beyond treatment, such as observation for education, HIPAA requires disclosure of only the minimum necessary information. A medical license does not make a visiting observer part of the treating team or entitle that person to the full record. Sensitive history such as psychiatric and HIV information has no bearing on the observation and should not be shared.
  40. The correct answer is A (Tacrolimus causes afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction and nephrotoxicity, so nephrotoxic drugs and hypovolemia should be avoided.): Calcineurin inhibitors such as tacrolimus constrict the afferent arteriole and cause dose related nephrotoxicity, which is the most common cause of renal impairment after heart transplant. Perioperatively, hypovolemia, NSAIDs, aminoglycosides and contrast add to this injury. Mycophenolate toxicity is mainly gastrointestinal and hematologic, not renal. Chronic steroid users need stress dosing rather than withdrawal, and steroids do not cause renal salt wasting.
  41. The correct answer is B (Severe ascending aortic atheroma, which releases particles during cannulation and clamp manipulation): Atheroma of the ascending aorta is among the strongest predictors of perioperative stroke, because cannulation, clamping and the cannula jet can dislodge plaque. Other mechanisms include hypoperfusion and inflammation. Membrane oxygenators with arterial filters reduce embolic load, and a preserved ejection fraction is not a dominant risk factor for stroke.
  42. Care is supportive, with beta blockade or avoidance of inotropes if dynamic outflow obstruction develops. Permanent defibrillator therapy is not indicated because the dysfunction is reversible. Stenting is unnecessary because angiography showed no culprit lesion.
  43. The correct answer is C (At 18 C, 30 minutes of arrest is tolerated, with risk rising beyond 40 minutes.): Cerebral metabolic rate falls about 6 to 7 percent for each degree of cooling, and at 18 C the brain tolerates about 25 to 30 minutes of arrest, with neurologic injury rising after 40 minutes. EEG silence does not make arrest safe indefinitely. At 28 C metabolism remains substantial, so a 60 minute arrest would cause severe injury. Adjunctive cerebral perfusion extends the safe time.
  44. Volume, a faster rate and vasoconstrictors support output until drainage. Vasodilators and diuretics reduce preload and worsen shock, and higher PEEP and propofol reduce venous return and tone.

In an oliguric patient these measures are temporary, so renal replacement is planned to remove potassium. Delaying calcium or relying on resins and diuretics is too slow and unreliable.

Large trials such as PRESERVE found no benefit from N-acetylcysteine or bicarbonate. Forced diuresis without matched fluid replacement worsens volume depletion and does not protect the kidney.

Deferring replacement until symptoms appear loses the window in which ventricular volume can reverse, and referral thresholds center on volume index. Limiting the gradient to under 10 C and ending near 36 C balances time against these risks. Rewarming cannot wait until after protamine because the patient is still on bypass, and stopping at 34 C leaves the patient cold and coagulopathic.
  49. The correct answer is C (Synchronized cardioversion, since restoring atrial contraction improves filling in a stiff, hypertrophied ventricle.): A stiff hypertrophied ventricle depends on atrial contraction for up to 30 to 40 percent of filling, so loss of sinus rhythm with a small cavity and normal ejection fraction drops stroke volume and pressure. In a hypotensive patient, synchronized cardioversion restores atrial kick and is preferred. Diltiazem slows the rate but does not restore atrial contraction and may lower blood pressure further. Dobutamine raises rate and worsens diastolic filling in a small cavity.
  50. The correct answer is A (Acquired antithrombin deficiency from prolonged heparin; give antithrombin concentrate or plasma, then recheck ACT.): Prolonged heparin exposure consumes antithrombin, and heparin acts only by potentiating antithrombin, so low activity produces a blunted ACT response that further heparin cannot overcome. Antithrombin concentrate or fresh frozen plasma restores the substrate and the ACT then rises. Heparin-induced thrombocytopenia is unlikely with a normal platelet count. Repeated large heparin boluses add exposure without fixing the missing cofactor.
  51. The correct answer is B (Papillary muscle displacement from ventricular dilation tethers the leaflets, while annular dilation further reduces coaptation): In dilated cardiomyopathy, outward papillary muscle displacement tethers the leaflets toward the apex (Carpentier type IIIb) while annular dilation widens the coaptation gap, producing a central regurgitant jet through normal leaflets. Chordal rupture causes prolapse or flail motion, and rheumatic disease thickens and fuses leaflets, none of which are present here.
  52. The correct answer is B (Early decline is common, up to half at discharge, with microemboli and inflammation implicated): Postoperative cognitive dysfunction is common early, affecting up to half of patients at discharge, with improvement over months in many. Cerebral microemboli from the circuit and aorta and the systemic inflammatory response are the leading mechanisms. It is not rare, and it is not confined to off-pump surgery, where bypass-related inflammation is actually reduced.
  53. The correct answer is A (Stop empagliflozin three days before, continue carvedilol, and hold sacubitril-valsartan on the morning of surgery.): Sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 inhibitors are held three days before surgery because of the risk of euglycemic ketoacidosis. Beta-blockers are continued to prevent withdrawal ischemia, while renin-angiotensin agents are commonly held on the morning of surgery to reduce vasoplegia. Giving steroids does not offset ketoacidosis or hypotension. Stopping the beta-blocker invites rebound ischemia in this coronary patient.

It resolves without treatment and routine replacement has not improved outcomes in stable patients. A normal TSH argues against primary hypothyroidism, and nothing suggests pituitary injury or adrenal insufficiency.

Returning to bypass and supporting with VA ECMO allows the graft to recover. With hypotension, synchronized cardioversion is the safest choice, and procainamide or ibutilide may be used when stable.

  60.   61. The correct answer is C (Higher cardiac output and heart rate shorten diastolic filling, raising left atrial pressure): Pregnancy raises blood volume by about 40 to 50 percent and cardiac output by a similar amount, and heart rate rises, which shortens diastole. The gradient across a fixed orifice varies with the square of flow and with the diastolic time, so left atrial pressure climbs and pulmonary edema follows. Labor pain and delivery raise heart rate and preload further, and plasma volume actually expands rather than falls.
  62. The correct answer is A (A tip in the lower superior vena cava or right atrium, angled against the vessel wall.): Perforation is most likely when a stiff catheter tip rests at an acute angle against the wall near or below the pericardial reflection, because cardiac motion repeatedly drives the tip into the wall. Tips placed parallel to the vessel wall in the upper superior vena cava or brachiocephalic vein sit outside the pericardium and carry far less risk of tamponade.
  63. A true aneurysm has a wide neck and a wall made of thinned myocardium that contracts or bulges paradoxically. A loculated effusion lacks any communication with the ventricular cavity on color Doppler.
  64. The correct answer is C (Inflate a balloon in the conduit during selective coronary angiography to exclude coronary compression.): The conduit and the expanded stent often lie close to the proximal coronary arteries, and a radial force on the conduit can compress a coronary artery, causing ischemia and death. The standard test is simultaneous balloon inflation in the conduit with selective coronary angiography. Echocardiography gives a gradient only, and calcium scoring does not address extrinsic compression.
  65. The correct answer is A (Cesarean delivery first, then immediate aortic repair, since bypass threatens placental perfusion in a viable fetus): At 34 weeks the fetus is viable, and cardiopulmonary bypass causes placental hypoperfusion and fetal loss, so cesarean delivery is performed first, followed immediately by aortic repair under the same anesthetic. Type A dissection is a surgical emergency that cannot wait for 37 weeks. Operating with the fetus in utero is reserved for a previable gestation. Medical therapy alone has very high maternal mortality for ascending dissection, and the claim of prohibitive surgical mortality is not accurate.

Values above 0.30 are generally normal, and values below 0.25 suggest obstruction or a small effective orifice. Inverting the ratio or dividing by the sum does not follow the definition.
  67. The correct answer is C (ACT not rechecked after the heparin dose, with a risk priority number of 270): The risk priority number is severity times occurrence times detection. The ACT not rechecked mode scores 9 times 5 times 6, which is 270. The wrong protamine dose scores 8 times 4 times 5, which is 160, protamine before decannulation scores 80, and heparin omitted scores 60. Severity alone does not set the ranking, so the highest product comes first.

The central venous pressure climbing from 11 to 19 mmHg suggests the right ventricle cannot handle the increased venous return, a recognized complication. Histamine-releasing agents such as morphine and atracurium, and catecholamines that provoke mediator release, are avoided. Vasopressin or phenylephrine is preferred for hypotension.

  72.   73. The correct answer is A (Pace above the intrinsic rate, decrease output until capture is lost, then program twice the threshold): The capture threshold is the lowest output that reliably captures the myocardium. It is found by pacing faster than the intrinsic rhythm, starting at a comfortable output and stepping it down until capture is lost, then increasing slightly. Setting the final output at two to three times the threshold provides a safety margin as the threshold rises over days. Sensitivity and sensing tests measure a different property and do not define output.

  75. The correct answer is C (Heat redistributes from the warm core to still cold, poorly perfused tissues, which bladder temperature reflected): The nasopharyngeal site tracks rapidly changing brain and core temperature, while bladder and rectal sites lag because they reflect more slowly equilibrating tissue. Once bypass stops, heat redistributes from the core to cold peripheral tissues, causing core temperature to fall. Rewarming should continue until both sites approach target values. The probe is not wrong, and cardioplegia does not explain a drift in nasopharyngeal temperature.
  76. The correct answer is A (Give cryoprecipitate to replace fibrinogen and von Willebrand factor, and lower the heparin target): High shear forces in the circuit cleave large von Willebrand multimers, producing an acquired deficiency on top of low fibrinogen. Cryoprecipitate supplies both fibrinogen and von Willebrand factor, and reducing anticoagulation lessens the bleeding drive. Platelets alone do not address either defect, and recombinant factor VIIa carries a high risk of circuit thrombosis.
  77. The correct answer is C (Perform mitral surgery during bypass, because guidelines support it as reasonable for severe secondary regurgitation.): The 2020 ACC/AHA valve guideline gives a class 2a recommendation that mitral surgery is reasonable in patients with chronic severe secondary mitral regurgitation who are undergoing coronary bypass. Revascularization alone seldom resolves severe regurgitation when leaflets are tethered. Staged transcatheter repair is not the preferred strategy when surgery is already planned.
  78. A magnet suspends sensing and breaks the loop, and reprogramming the postventricular atrial refractory period prevents recurrence. Cardioversion and amiodarone treat different mechanisms.

Waiting until after bypass or until shock develops forgoes this benefit in a patient at high risk of infarction during induction.

A concentration assay measures drug level more directly, and it is below target here. Trusting the ACT alone leaves the patient under-anticoagulated, and empiric dosing ignores the data in hand.
  87. Reducing the insufflation pressure and adding volume often corrects it. Carbon dioxide absorption causes hypercapnia but not a primary contractility loss at this pressure, and gas embolism through an arterial conduit is not the typical mechanism.
  88. The correct answer is B (About 0.5 cm squared, which meets the severe stenosis threshold): Hakki gives valve area as cardiac output divided by the square root of the gradient, so 4.0 divided by the square root of 64 equals 4.0 divided by 8, or 0.5 cm squared. A value under 1.0 cm squared defines severe stenosis. The other values result from errors such as using a different gradient or skipping the square root.
  89. The correct answer is B (Elevated hemoglobin A1c predicts deep sternal wound infection, so perioperative glucose should stay below 180 mg/dL.): Higher hemoglobin A1c, particularly above 8 percent, is associated with deep sternal wound infection, mediastinitis, and mortality after cardiac surgery. The value reflects average glucose over roughly eight to twelve weeks, not two. Perioperative glucose control with insulin infusion, aiming for under 180 mg/dL, lowers the infection rate. A target as lax as 250 mg/dL does not meet current society recommendations, and bilateral thoracic artery harvest adds risk in diabetes.

Repeating the dose through the same line would repeat the failure, and heparin resistance is considered only after delivery is verified.

A two stage right atrial cannula depends on atrial inflow and loses drainage once the atrium is opened. A femoral venous cannula also leaves caval blood flowing into the opened atrium unless snares are applied.

The head and both arms receive normally saturated blood from the ascending aorta, producing differential cyanosis, and the foot is truly desaturated. A proximal mixing lesion would affect all sites alike. A comparison of pre- and postductal saturation confirms the diagnosis.

Recommendations place the needle at least one hour before systemic heparin, and delay surgery 24 hours if the puncture is traumatic. Placement after reversal or during full heparinization is unsafe, since postoperative coagulopathy and residual anticoagulation persist.
  99. The correct answer is B (The patient with a residual shunt adjacent to a prosthetic patch after ventricular septal defect closure.): Prophylaxis is indicated for prosthetic valves or material, previous endocarditis, unrepaired cyanotic disease, repaired disease with residual defects at or next to prosthetic material, and during the first six months after complete repair with prosthetic material. A suture-closed secundum defect and an uncomplicated bicuspid valve are not indications.
  100. The correct answer is C (Radical debridement of infected tissue and root replacement with a homograft or biologic conduit): Extensive root destruction with abscess and heart block calls for complete debridement of infected tissue and reconstruction of the root with a homograft or a biologic conduit, which resists reinfection. Limited excision leaves the abscess in place and risks dehiscence of the prosthesis. Preserving infected native leaflets is inappropriate with this degree of destruction.
  101. The correct answer is B (Rapid rise in left ventricular diastolic pressure above left atrial pressure in a noncompliant, unadapted ventricle): In acute regurgitation the normal-sized, noncompliant ventricle receives a large regurgitant volume, so diastolic pressure climbs steeply and can exceed left atrial pressure, closing the mitral valve early. This also shortens the time for atrial emptying and raises the wedge pressure. Chronic dilation and SAM are not mechanisms of diastolic closure.
  102. It is not recurrent clot, so thrombolysis would add bleeding risk without benefit. Large fluid volumes and high tidal volumes worsen the edema and lung injury.
  103. The correct answer is A (Cold injury to the left phrenic nerve from ice slush, with an elevated hemidiaphragm): Topical ice slush can injure the left phrenic nerve, which lies against the pericardium, giving hemidiaphragm paralysis and an elevated hemidiaphragm on chest radiograph. A pneumothorax typically flattens the diaphragm and shifts the mediastinum away. The recurrent laryngeal nerve affects the vocal cords, not the diaphragm.
  104. The correct answer is C (About 66 percent, because positive predictive value rises when disease prevalence rises): In cohort B, 300 patients have delirium and 700 do not. With sensitivity 90 percent there are 270 true positives, and with specificity 80 percent there are 140 false positives. PPV is 270 divided by 410, about 66 percent, compared with 90 divided by 270, about 33 percent, in cohort A. PPV rises with prevalence and is not the same as sensitivity.
  105. The correct answer is B (Alpha-stat keeps cerebral flow coupled to metabolism, limiting embolic delivery in adults with atheroma.): In alpha-stat, CO2 is not added, so the brain remains alkaline relative to temperature-corrected values, autoregulation is preserved, and flow follows the lowered metabolic rate. Lower flow means fewer emboli reach the brain, which matters with atheroma. The description of CO2-driven vasodilation and uncoupling applies to pH-stat.
  106. The correct answer is C (Lower mean systemic filling pressure shifts the venous return curve left; volume infusion restores it.): Venodilation moves blood into unstressed volume and lowers mean systemic filling pressure, which shifts the venous return curve to the left and decreases the pressure gradient driving return. Cardiac output falls even though right atrial pressure is lower. Volume infusion or venoconstriction raises mean systemic filling pressure and shifts the curve back. Nitroglycerin does not raise systemic vascular resistance, and a wider gradient would increase output, contradicting the observation.
  107. The correct answer is B (Myocardial rewarming and washout of cardioplegia from collateral flow, so give additional cardioplegia promptly.): Return of electrical activity during cross-clamp means the arrested state is lost, usually because collateral flow rewarms the heart and washes out the potassium. Treatment is another dose of cardioplegia, with attention to adequate distribution and temperature. Removing the clamp before the operation is done reintroduces ischemia and hemorrhage. Systemic potassium would not protect the myocardium.
  108. The correct answer is B (About 47,000 units from actual body weight, then confirm ACT above 480 seconds.): Heparin distributes in plasma volume, which tracks total body weight, so the dose is 118 kg x 400 units/kg = 47,200 units. Dosing from ideal weight gives 70 x 400 = 28,000 units and risks inadequate anticoagulation for bypass. The lower 300 units/kg regimen yields about 35,400 units and is below the standard bypass loading dose. An ACT above 480 seconds confirms adequacy before cannulation.
  109. The correct answer is C (About 0.84 cm2, which meets criteria for severe aortic stenosis by continuity equation area): LVOT area is pi x (1.1 cm)2, or 3.80 cm2. The continuity equation gives valve area as 3.80 x 1.0 / 4.5, or about 0.84 cm2. An area below 1.0 cm2 with a peak velocity of 4.0 m/s or higher meets criteria for severe stenosis. The other values arise from errors such as halving the LVOT area or inverting the ratio.
  110. The correct answer is A (Local anesthesia with conscious sedation and transthoracic echo is reasonable and linked to shorter hospital stay): Randomized and registry data show that monitored care in suitable patients gives similar mortality and stroke rates to general anesthesia, with shorter procedure time, less vasopressor use, and shorter stays. Conversion to general anesthesia is possible if complications occur. Neither required transesophageal echo nor higher mortality with sedation has been shown.
  111. The correct answer is C (Titrate a short acting vasodilator such as nitroprusside while watching left ventricular wall motion on TEE): Cross-clamping the descending aorta abruptly raises afterload, producing proximal hypertension and acute left ventricular strain. A titratable short acting vasodilator such as nitroprusside reduces afterload quickly, and TEE wall motion and chamber size confirm the response. A beta blocker bolus depresses an already failing ventricle and does not lower afterload effectively. Diuresis is too slow for an acute afterload mismatch and does not treat the clamp physiology.
  112. The correct answer is A (Cardiac output about 5.1 L/min, from stroke volume near 68 mL and heart rate 75.): Outflow tract area is pi times radius squared: 3.14 x 1.1 x 1.1 = 3.8 cm2. Stroke volume is area times velocity time integral: 3.8 x 18 = 68 mL. Cardiac output is 68 mL x 75 per minute = 5.1 L/min. Using diameter in place of radius would give a four-fold overestimate, and halving the area gives the lower value.
  113. The correct answer is B (Thrombin in stagnant blood cleaves bivalirudin, allowing clot to form in pooled blood.): Bivalirudin is a direct thrombin inhibitor that is also slowly cleaved by thrombin, so where blood is stagnant its effect is lost locally and thrombus can form. This is why stasis in the circuit and field is avoided and shed blood is kept moving through suction to the pump. It does not depend on antithrombin. Protamine does not reverse it.
  114. The correct answer is B (Commissural fusion from chronic valve closure under sustained root pressure, with continuous regurgitant flow): When the aortic valve stays closed, leaflets fuse at the commissures and the leaflet tissue degenerates under constant pressure from the pump outflow. The regurgitant blood flows back through the valve throughout the cardiac cycle and is recirculated by the pump. Endocarditis would show a vegetation, and the stem describes none.
  115. The correct answer is A (Admit for at least 24 hours of continuous telemetry with serial troponin and CK measurements.): High voltage injury with loss of consciousness carries a risk of arrhythmia and myocardial injury, so observation with continuous telemetry for about 24 hours with serial troponin and CK is advised. A normal initial ECG does not exclude delayed arrhythmia, and deep muscle injury can follow even with limited skin findings. Short observation is reserved for low voltage exposure without risk features.
  116. The correct answer is B (Keep cerebral perfusion pressure at 60 mmHg or above, control heart rate, and defer repair): Competing goals require compromise: secondary brain injury from hypoperfusion is more immediately harmful, so cerebral perfusion pressure is protected while heart rate is controlled to limit aortic wall stress. Minimal aortic injuries often tolerate delayed repair. Nitroprusside can raise intracranial pressure, and unrestrained hypertension risks extension of the tear.
  117. The correct answer is C (Oxygen delivery is inadequate for consumption, with increased extraction driven by anemia and low cardiac output): Mixed venous saturation reflects the balance between oxygen delivery and consumption, and a normal value is about 65 to 75 percent. A value of 55 percent with hemoglobin 7.2 g/dL and a low cardiac output means delivery is reduced and tissues are extracting more oxygen. A left to right shunt would raise the saturation, not lower it, and 55 percent is not a normal anesthetized value.
  118. The correct answer is C (Rely on the experienced visual estimate, noting that endocardial dropout and foreshortening limit the biplane tracing.): Experienced visual assessment correlates well with quantitative methods and is accepted intraoperatively. Simpson biplane tracing is accurate only with clear endocardial borders and nonforeshortened views, which are often unavailable on TEE. One M-mode segment cannot represent global function when regional abnormalities may exist.
  119. The correct answer is A (Positioning the right-sided tube bronchial cuff so it occludes the right upper lobe bronchial orifice.): The right upper lobe bronchus arises only 1.5 to 2.5 cm from the carina, so the cuff of a right-sided tube can obstruct it and cause upper lobe atelectasis and hypoxemia. A right-sided tube needs a slotted cuff aligned to the orifice and bronchoscopic confirmation. A left-sided tube is preferred in most cases because the left main bronchus is longer. Bronchoscopy after repositioning is standard.
  120. The correct answer is B (Overdamping from a bubble or kinked tubing blunts the systolic peak while mean pressure remains accurate): An overdamped system absorbs the high-frequency components of the waveform, so the upstroke is slurred, the notch disappears and the systolic peak is reduced, while the mean pressure stays close to the true value. The single small overshoot on flush confirms excess damping. Underdamping would instead exaggerate the systolic peak. A transducer level error shifts all values by the same amount and would not raise the diastolic reading relative to the cuff while lowering the systolic.
  121. The correct answer is B (A sutureless bioprosthesis shortens cross-clamp time, with a higher rate of permanent pacemaker implantation): Sutureless and rapid-deployment valves are seated without annular sutures, reducing cross-clamp and bypass times, which helps in combined or high-risk operations, at the cost of more conduction disease requiring pacemakers. Mechanical valves need lifelong anticoagulation. A stentless root operation is technically complex and lengthens ischemic time.
  122. The correct answer is C (Give intravenous antibiotics, debride, and continue pump support while evaluating the pocket and cannula.): Driveline infection with bacteremia is a serious device complication, but the patient depends on the pump for output. Management combines culture-directed intravenous antibiotics, surgical source control, and imaging of the pocket and cannula for deeper involvement. Immediate explant is not possible without replacement support, and oral antibiotics alone do not control a bacteremic deep site.
  123. The correct answer is A (A high septal defect near the SVC junction, with a right pulmonary vein draining anomalously.): Sinus venosus defects lie outside the true atrial septum, near the junction of the SVC and right atrium, and are almost always accompanied by partial anomalous return of the right upper pulmonary vein. A low defect with a cleft valve describes a primum defect, and a defect at the fossa ovalis is a secundum defect, which is excluded by the intact fossa.
  124. The correct answer is C (New aortic regurgitation with a vena contracta of 0.7 cm after commissural resuspension): A severe regurgitant jet (vena contracta above 0.6 cm) after resuspension means the valve repair has failed and revision or replacement is needed. A residual distal flap with systolic true lumen expansion is expected and managed medically since the entry tear was resected. A small effusion without compression is not an indication.
  125. The correct answer is A (Arterial line before induction, large-bore venous access, crossmatched blood available and transesophageal echocardiography ready for tamponade): Superior vena cava or right atrial tears during extraction can cause rapid exsanguination, so invasive pressure monitoring, reliable large-bore access, blood in the room and the ability to image the pericardium are needed. A single small line and type and screen do not allow rapid resuscitation. Access on the device side interferes with the extraction field and may be damaged by the procedure.
  126. The correct answer is A (A sentinel event, since a retained foreign object reached the patient and needs root cause analysis): The Joint Commission lists unintended retention of a foreign object after a procedure as a sentinel event, which calls for a comprehensive systematic analysis. A near miss is an event that does not reach the patient, and here the wire did. Retained guidewires are preventable, not an accepted complication, so the event is reportable.
  127. The correct answer is C (Keep the water to blood gradient near 10 degrees Celsius and arterial outlet below 37.): Gases are more soluble in cold blood, so rapid warming releases dissolved gas as microbubbles. Limiting the water to blood gradient to about 10 degrees and keeping arterial outlet temperature below 37 degrees avoids this and avoids cerebral hyperthermia. Larger gradients and higher outlet temperatures increase embolic and neurologic risk.
  128. The correct answer is A (Raise the ventilator FiO2 and PEEP to oxygenate the blood ejected from the native left ventricle.): In differential hypoxemia, the upper body receives blood ejected by the native ventricle, which was oxygenated by the lungs. Improving lung oxygenation with higher FiO2 and PEEP fixes the coronary and cerebral supply. The ECMO return already carries a very high PaO2, so adjusting sweep gas does not change what the native ventricle ejects.
  129. The correct answer is B (Friable necrotic septal tissue at the defect margin gives poor anchoring and risks residual shunting.): Early after infarction the septum around the rupture is necrotic and friable, so devices anchor poorly, may enlarge the defect and leave residual shunts, which is why closure is often delayed or used as a bridge. Occluders exist for defects well over 5 mm, and apical defects can be reached from the venous or arterial side.
  130. The correct answer is B (Lower all-cause mortality at long-term follow-up compared with medical therapy alone): The extended STICH follow-up found a significant reduction in all-cause death with bypass surgery, although early risk was higher. The initial report at five years had been neutral. The viability substudy found no interaction between viability and the benefit of surgery.
  131. The correct answer is A (Bivalirudin given as bolus, infusion and pump prime, monitored by ACT, avoiding stasis in the circuit.): Bivalirudin, a short-acting direct thrombin inhibitor, is the best studied option for bypass in acute HIT with normal renal function, since it has a half-life of about 25 minutes and partial enzymatic clearance. Low molecular weight heparin cross-reacts with the antibody and fondaparinux cannot provide reliable bypass anticoagulation or titration. Circuit stasis must be avoided because bivalirudin is cleaved by thrombin in stagnant blood.
  132. The correct answer is B (Assess decision-specific capacity through understanding, appreciation, reasoning and a stable choice, using teach-back): Capacity is decision-specific and assessed by understanding, appreciation, reasoning and expressing a consistent choice, and teach-back helps verify it. A diagnosis of mild impairment does not equal incapacity, so surrogate consent is premature. A screening test score does not test the patient's grasp of this particular high-risk decision.
  133. The correct answer is A (Ventilation-perfusion scintigraphy, which is more sensitive than CT angiography for chronic thromboembolic disease.): A normal ventilation-perfusion scan effectively excludes chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension, and mismatched segmental defects prompt confirmation with pulmonary angiography and right heart catheterization. CT angiography can miss chronic webs and bands, and echocardiography suggests pulmonary hypertension but cannot determine the cause.
  134. The correct answer is C (Potassium near 20 to 30 mEq/L depolarizes the membrane, inactivating sodium channels and arresting diastole.): Induction cardioplegia contains high potassium, around 20 to 30 mEq/L, which holds the resting membrane potential near minus 50 mV so fast sodium channels stay inactivated and no action potentials fire. The heart then stops in diastole with low energy use. Low potassium concentrations do not produce arrest, and systolic arrest would be damaging.
  135. The correct answer is B (EF 35 percent or lower, NYHA class II or III, at least 40 days after infarction): Primary prevention ICD in ischemic cardiomyopathy requires an ejection fraction of 35 percent or lower with NYHA class II or III symptoms, measured at least 40 days after infarction and at least 90 days after revascularization, on guideline-directed therapy. The ejection fraction may recover early after infarction, so early measurement is not used. Thresholds of 40 to 45 percent do not qualify.
  136. The correct answer is B (The beam may be misaligned with the jet, and a deep transgastric view improves parallel alignment.): Doppler velocity is underestimated when the beam is not parallel to the jet, and the midesophageal long-axis view often has a poor angle across the aortic valve. The deep transgastric and transgastric long-axis views place the beam closer to parallel with flow and record higher velocities. A calcified valve with a small planimetered area and a low gradient should prompt a check of alignment before concluding low flow.
  137. The correct answer is C (Hydroxocobalamin 5 g intravenously, which scavenges nitric oxide and hydrogen sulfide without serotonergic effects): Methylene blue blocks nitric oxide synthase and guanylate cyclase, but it inhibits monoamine oxidase and can cause serotonin syndrome in a patient on serotonergic drugs such as sertraline. Hydroxocobalamin binds nitric oxide and hydrogen sulfide and has no serotonergic effect, so it is a safer alternative. Further alpha agonist is unlikely to help because the catecholamine receptors are already desensitized.
  138. The correct answer is C (Hemoglobin affinity increases, shifting the curve leftward and impairing oxygen unloading at the tissue level): Hypothermia shifts the oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve to the left, so P50 falls and hemoglobin holds oxygen more tightly, which limits release to tissues even though the metabolic rate is lower. Alpha-stat management keeps the temperature-corrected gas values in line with electrochemical neutrality but does not return P50 to normal. A rightward shift occurs with warming, acidosis or hypercapnia.
  139. The correct answer is B (Both have NMDA antagonism, using subanesthetic ketamine infusion and a single intraoperative methadone dose): Ketamine and methadone both antagonize NMDA receptors, which blunts central sensitization and opioid tolerance. Ketamine is used at subanesthetic doses such as 0.1 to 0.5 mg/kg/hour, and a single intraoperative methadone dose of about 0.1 to 0.2 mg/kg provides long analgesia. Neither acts as a GABA agonist, and ketamine does not block mu receptors.
  140. The correct answer is C (Urgent surgery with abscess debridement and valve replacement, since perivalvular extension with heart block is indicated.): New atrioventricular block indicates perivalvular extension, with an abscess eroding into the conduction system near the membranous septum. Guidelines recommend early surgery in such cases because medical therapy alone fails and the abscess enlarges. Delaying operation to sterilize cultures risks embolism, destruction of the root and hemodynamic collapse.
  141. The correct answer is C (Septal displacement of 13 mm/m2 exceeds the 8 mm/m2 criterion, consistent with an atrialized right ventricle.): Apical displacement of the septal tricuspid leaflet by 8 mm/m2 or more of body surface area defines Ebstein anomaly. Here 24 mm divided by 1.8 m2 is about 13 mm/m2, and the thin-walled inlet in continuity with the right atrium is the atrialized right ventricle. The patent foramen ovale with right-to-left flow explains the cyanosis. The 20 mm/m2 criterion and the functional regurgitation explanation are not supported.
  142. The correct answer is A (No outcome benefit is shown for routine use in low-risk patients, so selective use is preferred.): Randomized and observational data have not shown improved mortality or morbidity from routine pulmonary artery catheter use in low-risk cardiac surgery, so catheters are used selectively for pulmonary hypertension, right ventricular dysfunction, low ejection fraction and complex operations. Evidence does not show universal harm, and the catheter retains value in selected cases.
  143. The correct answer is B (Type II, flow into the sac from a patent branch vessel of the native aorta): Endoleaks are classified by source: type I is a failed seal at the attachment zones, type II is retrograde flow from branch vessels such as intercostal, lumbar or inferior mesenteric arteries, type III is a graft fabric defect or module junction failure, type IV is graft porosity, and type V is endotension. Intact seals and junctions with branch vessel inflow identify type II. Type I and type III leaks need urgent treatment because they expose the sac to systemic pressure, while type II is often observed.
  144. The correct answer is B (Aortic dissection with the cannula in a false lumen; stop bypass and confirm true lumen flow): A rising pump line pressure with a falling radial pressure and a spreading aortic hematoma indicates that the cannula is delivering blood outside the true lumen, creating dissection.
