Bypass is stopped, true lumen flow is confirmed, often with TEE, and the cannula is repositioned or another site is used. A filter or arch vessel problem would not produce an aortic hematoma.
  145. The correct answer is C (Tip lies 1 to 2 cm distal to the origin of the left subclavian artery): The tip should sit 1 to 2 cm below the left subclavian origin so that inflation augments coronary and cerebral perfusion without occluding the arch vessels. A tip in the arch can obstruct the left subclavian or carotid flow and raise embolic risk. A tip at the diaphragm is too low, giving poor diastolic augmentation and risk to visceral and renal perfusion.
  146. The correct answer is C (Inhaled nitric oxide at 20 ppm, which dilates pulmonary vessels in ventilated lung units only): Inhaled agents are delivered to ventilated alveoli, reach pulmonary vessels, and are inactivated or diluted before causing systemic vasodilation. That lowers right ventricular afterload and improves left ventricular filling while preserving arterial pressure. Intravenous nitroprusside and epoprostenol dilate the systemic circulation, and intravenous prostacyclin also worsens ventilation-perfusion matching.
  147. The correct answer is B (Low perfusion pressure, hemolysis with free hemoglobin, low nadir hematocrit and nephrotoxins raise risk.): The kidney is vulnerable to hypoperfusion on bypass, and the modifiable contributors include low mean arterial pressure, free hemoglobin from hemolysis that damages tubules, a nadir hematocrit below about 22 to 24 percent, and nephrotoxic drugs. Higher pressures and hematocrits are protective, not harmful. Pulsatile flow and acid-base strategy have not been shown to dominate kidney outcomes.
  148. The correct answer is A (Immobility keeps the map accurate, and controlled ventilation limits motion during painful posterior wall energy delivery.): Patient movement shifts the mapping geometry and catheter contact, and posterior wall ablation is painful, so general anesthesia with controlled ventilation or jet ventilation improves stability and safety in an obese patient with sleep apnea. Anesthesia does not change tissue heat conduction. Anticoagulation with heparin is still required to keep the ACT above 300 seconds during left-sided ablation.
  149. The correct answer is B (Biventricular pacing resynchronizes septal and lateral wall contraction, improving stroke volume and reducing functional mitral regurgitation.): In left bundle branch block the septum activates early and the lateral wall late, wasting work. Pacing both ventricles restores coordinated contraction, which increases stroke volume and reduces functional mitral regurgitation from papillary muscle dyssynchrony. Simple rate increases do not correct dyssynchrony, and atrial pacing does not alter ventricular conduction through the diseased bundle.
  150. The correct answer is B (Start inhaled epoprostenol or nitric oxide, optimize ventilation, and support pressure with vasopressin): Inhaled pulmonary vasodilators reduce right ventricular afterload without lowering systemic pressure, and vasopressin maintains aortic root pressure and coronary perfusion with minimal effect on pulmonary resistance. Systemic nitroglycerin and a large fluid load drop coronary perfusion pressure and overdistend the failing ventricle. High PEEP and tidal volume raise pulmonary vascular resistance by lung overdistention.
  151. The correct answer is C (Prominent v waves exceeding 50 mmHg on the wedge tracing, with preserved or hyperdynamic ventricular function.): Acute severe mitral regurgitation sends a large volume into a small, noncompliant left atrium during systole, producing giant v waves on the wedge tracing and abrupt pulmonary edema despite a normal-sized, hyperdynamic ventricle. Tamponade causes diastolic equalization, not giant v waves. A saturation step-up from right atrium to ventricle indicates ventricular septal rupture, which has a different murmur and normal wedge morphology.
  152. The correct answer is A (Place a temporary pacing wire, since septal ablation commonly causes block that may need permanent pacing.): Alcohol septal ablation infarcts the basal septum, which contains the His bundle and bundle branches, so complete heart block is a well-recognized complication. The escape rhythm is infranodal and does not respond to atropine. A temporary wire is needed, and a permanent pacemaker is required in a meaningful proportion of patients.
  153. The correct answer is B (Selective renal perfusion with cold crystalloid at about 4 degrees C during the period of ischemia): Cold renal perfusion with crystalloid at about 4 degrees C lowers renal metabolic demand and is associated with less postoperative renal failure than normothermic ischemia. Mannitol is widely used for its osmotic and free radical effects, but randomized evidence of reduced renal failure is lacking. Low dose dopamine has repeatedly failed to improve renal outcomes and adds arrhythmia risk. Cold perfusion is therefore the best supported choice.
  154. The correct answer is C (Turbulent aliased flow at the cannula tip with continuous-wave velocity above 1.5 m/s): A partially obstructed inflow cannula narrows the channel, producing aliasing and peak velocities above the normal limit of about 1.5 m/s. Smooth low-velocity inflow is the normal pattern and argues against obstruction. Reversal of flow in the outflow graft implies pump stoppage or severe regurgitation, which is a different problem.
  155. The correct answer is B (Fewer leg wound complications, with similar major adverse cardiac events in the REGROUP randomized trial): Endoscopic harvest reduces leg wound infection and dehiscence, and the REGROUP trial found no difference in major adverse cardiac events at about 4.7 years. Insufflation does not increase wound infection rates. Earlier observational concerns about worse graft outcomes were not confirmed by the randomized data.
  156. The correct answer is A (Intravenous amiodarone for rate and rhythm control, given the reduced ejection fraction and borderline blood pressure.): Amiodarone slows the ventricular rate and can convert the rhythm, and it is relatively well tolerated in patients with reduced ejection fraction and low blood pressure. Diltiazem is a negative inotrope that can precipitate failure when the ejection fraction is 30 percent. Digoxin slows the rate slowly and does not reliably cardiovert, so it is a poor sole agent for a rapid rate.
  157. The correct answer is A (Phenylephrine bolus with volume loading and a beta-blocker to slow the heart rate): Outflow obstruction worsens with tachycardia, reduced preload, reduced afterload, and inotropes. Phenylephrine raises afterload, volume increases preload, and beta-blockade lengthens filling and reduces contractility. Epinephrine and nitroglycerin both increase the gradient and worsen systolic anterior motion.
  158. The correct answer is C (Rapid sequence induction with etomidate and rocuronium, applying cricoid pressure while maintaining the milrinone infusion): A full stomach calls for a rapid sequence technique to protect the airway, and etomidate preserves hemodynamics in a failing ventricle. Inhalational mask induction leaves the airway unprotected and the volatile agent depresses an already poor ventricle. Propofol titrated slowly with mask ventilation both risks aspiration and causes vasodilation and hypotension.
  159. The correct answer is A (Continue the beta-blocker through the morning of surgery to avoid rebound tachycardia and myocardial ischemia.): Abrupt withdrawal of chronic beta-blockade causes rebound tachycardia, hypertension, and ischemia in patients with coronary disease, and guidelines support continuing therapy into the perioperative period. His resting rate and pressure allow the dose to be given safely. Holding the drug for two days reproduces the withdrawal risk. A dihydropyridine blocker lacks the antiadrenergic effect and can produce reflex tachycardia.
  160. The correct answer is B (Give a second 2 g dose about four hours after the first, since hemodilution lowers levels): Cefazolin has a half life of about 1.8 hours and its level falls with long operations and the dilution of bypass, so redosing is advised at about four hours. Guidelines support repeating the same agent. The half life is far shorter than eight hours, and there is no rule against repeating cefazolin during one operation.
  161. The correct answer is B (It may reduce induction ischemia and low output risk, but adds vascular and bleeding complications.): Preoperative counterpulsation lowers afterload and raises diastolic coronary perfusion, which may limit ischemia during induction in very high-risk patients. Randomized evidence of mortality benefit in stable patients is limited, and the device carries limb ischemia, bleeding, and infection risks. Diastolic augmentation improves rather than steals coronary flow.
  162. The correct answer is C (Magnesium sulfate 2 g intravenously, which suppresses early afterdepolarizations, along with potassium repletion.): Hypokalemia and QT-prolonging drugs lengthen repolarization and allow early afterdepolarizations that trigger torsades de pointes. Magnesium 2 g intravenously suppresses these afterdepolarizations even when the serum magnesium is normal, and potassium should be repleted toward 4.5 to 5 mEq/L. Lidocaine does not address the trigger. Procainamide prolongs the QT interval and would worsen the arrhythmia.
  163. The correct answer is C (Defer nonurgent surgery several days until creatinine plateaus, maintaining hydration and avoiding nephrotoxic drugs): Contrast nephropathy peaks at about 48 to 72 hours, and bypass adds ischemic and inflammatory renal injury, so operating at the height of the insult raises the risk of dialysis. For stable disease, surgery should be deferred until creatinine plateaus. N-acetylcysteine has not shown consistent benefit.
  164. The correct answer is A (Confirm double-lumen tube position with fiberoptic bronchoscopy, clear secretions, and set inspired oxygen fraction to 1.0.): Hypoxemia after lung isolation is often caused by tube malposition or secretions, so bronchoscopy and full oxygen are first. Large tidal volumes raise airway pressure and divert blood flow back to the collapsed lung, worsening shunt. Inhaled vasodilator delivered to the nonventilated lung would increase flow there rather than reduce it.
  165. The correct answer is B (Vasodilatory acid metabolites returning from ischemic tissue and reactive hyperemia lowering afterload, with relative hypovolemia): Unclamping reopens a distal vascular bed that is acidotic, hypoxic and vasodilated, so lactate and other mediators wash into the circulation while reactive hyperemia opens the bed to flow. Afterload falls and effective preload is lost to the reperfused bed. Strategy includes volume loading, gradual unclamping and vasopressor readiness.
  166. The correct answer is A (Myocardial injury, since CABG related infarction needs troponin above 10 times the limit plus ischemic evidence.): The Fourth Universal Definition defines CABG related type 5 infarction as troponin above 10 times the 99th percentile within 48 hours plus new Q waves, angiographic graft or native vessel occlusion, or imaging evidence of new ischemic loss. An isolated rise of 8 times without these findings is myocardial injury. Type 4a refers to PCI related infarction.
  167. The correct answer is C (Recognize left ventricular distension and add a left ventricular vent while temporarily lowering ECMO flow): Retrograde aortic flow raises left ventricular afterload, so a failing ventricle cannot open the aortic valve and distends, causing pulmonary edema. Venting by a transvalvular pump, surgical vent or septostomy is needed, and lowering flow in the meantime reduces afterload. Raising flow worsens distension, and diuretics alone do not relieve the underlying mechanical problem.
  168. The correct answer is A (Grade III, a restrictive filling pattern with markedly elevated left atrial pressure.): An E/A ratio above 2, a short deceleration time under 160 ms, E/e' above 14 and an enlarged left atrium indicate a restrictive pattern with high filling pressures, which is grade III. Grade I shows E/A below 0.8 with a prolonged deceleration time and normal pressures. Grade II shows a pseudonormal E/A between 0.8 and 2.0.
  169. The correct answer is A (Cold injury to the left phrenic nerve from direct contact with ice slush.): The left phrenic nerve runs over the pericardium and lies close to the cold slush, which can injure it and cause diaphragm paralysis, seen as elevation and paradoxical motion. Recurrent laryngeal injury affects the vocal cord, not the diaphragm. Pulmonary edema would not cause paradoxical diaphragm motion. Insulating pads and avoiding direct slush contact reduce this complication.
  170. The correct answer is C (Maintain perfusion pressure, avoid nephrotoxins, and optimize glucose and volume with creatinine monitoring): The KDIGO bundle emphasizes maintaining perfusion pressure, avoiding nephrotoxic agents and radiocontrast, optimizing volume and glucose, and monitoring creatinine and urine output. Loop diuretics do not prevent kidney injury and may hide hypovolemia. Low-dose dopamine and prophylactic bicarbonate have not shown renal protection.
  171. The correct answer is B (38 mmHg, which is inadequate, so drain CSF and raise distal pressure): Spinal cord perfusion pressure equals distal mean arterial pressure minus the higher of CSF pressure or central venous pressure. Here CSF pressure (14 mmHg) exceeds central venous pressure (9 mmHg), so the value is 52 minus 14 = 38 mmHg. That is well below the roughly 60 to 70 mmHg usually sought, so CSF should be drained and distal pressure raised. Distal pressure alone ignores the opposing pressure, and subtracting the lower central venous pressure gives a falsely reassuring 43 mmHg.
  172. The correct answer is C (Continue the calcium channel blocker and anticipate additive bradycardia and vasodilation with volatile agents): Calcium channel blockers are generally continued before cardiac surgery to prevent rebound ischemia and coronary spasm. Diltiazem plus a beta-blocker and volatile anesthetics produces additive negative chronotropy, AV conduction delay, and vasodilation, so atropine, calcium, or pacing should be ready. The binding is reversible and clearance over one night is incomplete.
  173. The correct answer is B (Accept current values without change, since drainage saturation and lactate show adequate oxygen delivery): On venovenous ECMO an arterial saturation of 85 to 90 percent is acceptable and expected, because oxygenated circuit blood mixes with unsaturated native venous return. A normal lactate and drainage saturation of 72 percent indicate adequate oxygen delivery. Chasing a higher saturation or converting to arterial support adds risk without benefit.
  174. The correct answer is C (Retrieve contemporaneous system timestamps, add a dated late-entry addendum, and correct the claim accordingly): Billing integrity depends on accurate records. Verifiable timestamps from monitors or equipment can support a clearly labeled late entry and a claim corrected to actual time. Entering estimated times as if contemporaneous falsifies the record, and billing maximum units without support is a false claim that exposes the physician to audit and penalty.
  175. The correct answer is C (About half of baseline, roughly 50 percent of normothermic consumption.): A 7 percent fall per degree gives a factor of 0.93 per degree, and over 10 degrees that is 0.93 raised to the tenth power, or about 0.48. This corresponds to a Q10 of about 2. Cerebral metabolic rate falls similarly, which is why moderate hypothermia lowers oxygen demand during bypass.
  176. The correct answer is A (Pace the phrenic nerve from the superior vena cava, stop cooling if contraction weakens, withhold paralysis): The right phrenic nerve lies close to the right superior pulmonary vein, so pacing it from the superior vena cava while freezing allows the operator to observe or palpate diaphragmatic contraction, or measure the compound motor action potential, and stop energy at the first weakening. This requires the diaphragm to be able to contract, so paralysis must be absent. Vagal bradycardia relates to a different structure, and a radiograph detects injury only after it occurs.
  177. The correct answer is C (cAMP activates protein kinase A, which increases calcium available to bind troponin C.): Beta-1 stimulation activates Gs and adenylyl cyclase, raising cAMP and protein kinase A activity. Protein kinase A phosphorylates L-type calcium channels and phospholamban, increasing calcium entry and sarcoplasmic reticulum cycling, so more calcium binds troponin C and force rises while relaxation speeds. Gi activation would lower cAMP and reduce contractility. Sodium-potassium ATPase inhibition describes digoxin, not catecholamines.
  178. The correct answer is A (Briefly lower arterial pressure with adenosine or rapid ventricular pacing at the moment of deployment): The graft is exposed to pulsatile systolic force during release, which can cause distal migration, called the windsock effect. Transient hypotension from adenosine or rapid ventricular pacing at about 150 to 180 beats/min removes that force for a few seconds. Higher pressure and flow increase displacement rather than seating the graft. Raising airway pressure does not alter aortic wall motion enough to help and impairs venous return.
  179. The correct answer is B (Raise fibrinogen above 150 to 200 mg/dL with cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate): In bleeding cardiac surgery patients, fibrinogen below about 150 mg/dL warrants replacement to 150 to 200 mg/dL using cryoprecipitate or concentrate. Plasma contains too little fibrinogen to reach a high target without volume overload. Platelets do not correct a fibrinogen deficit.
  180. The correct answer is A (Desmopressin 0.3 mcg/kg intravenously, which releases von Willebrand factor stored in vascular endothelium.): Aortic stenosis shear cleaves high molecular weight multimers, creating acquired von Willebrand syndrome, and bypass adds platelet dysfunction. Desmopressin releases endothelial stores of von Willebrand factor and factor VIII and is given at 0.3 mcg/kg. Donor platelets carry too little factor to correct it. Tranexamic acid blocks fibrinolysis but supplies no multimers.
  181. The correct answer is A (More than 400 mL in the first hour, or about 200 mL hourly for several hours.): Commonly used criteria for reexploration include more than 400 mL in the first postoperative hour, more than 200 mL per hour for two to four hours, or sudden massive bleeding. Lower thresholds such as 100 mL in an hour are common after bypass and usually settle with correction of coagulopathy. A threshold of 800 mL per hour would delay reexploration far too long.
  182. The correct answer is B (Establish femoral cannulation and consider starting bypass before dividing the sternum at re-entry.): When the right ventricle or a conduit lies directly under the sternum, a saw injury can cause torrential hemorrhage and arrest. Peripheral cannulation with bypass before re-entry decompresses the heart and allows controlled dissection. Waiting until the heart is exposed leaves no recourse if the structure is lacerated, and full heparin without cannulation adds bleeding risk without benefit.
  183. The correct answer is A (Localized posterior clot can compress one chamber, so pulsus paradoxus may be absent): After cardiac surgery tamponade is often localized, with clot compressing the right atrium, left atrium or a single ventricle. Beck triad, pulsus paradoxus and equalization of diastolic pressures are then frequently missing. Transesophageal echocardiography is usually needed for diagnosis. Neither positive pressure ventilation nor vasoplegia explains the atypical presentation.
  184. The correct answer is C (Systolic blunting is expected in atrial fibrillation, so only true reversal supports severe regurgitation.): Loss of atrial relaxation and elevated left atrial pressure in atrial fibrillation reduce systolic forward flow in the pulmonary veins, so blunting is nonspecific and does not by itself indicate severe regurgitation. Systolic flow reversal is the specific sign. There is no atrial contraction in atrial fibrillation, so no atrial reversal wave is produced.
  185. The correct answer is B (Late inflation, which reduces diastolic augmentation and the coronary perfusion benefit.): The balloon should inflate at the dicrotic notch, at aortic valve closure. Inflation after a visible notch indicates late inflation, which shortens the period of augmented diastolic pressure and lowers coronary perfusion. Early inflation shows augmentation merging with the systolic upstroke, and late deflation shows a prolonged assisted end-diastolic pressure.
  186. The correct answer is B (Return to the operating room now, since normal coagulation points to a surgical source): With normal coagulation studies, heavy bleeding and hemodynamic compromise indicate a surgical source. Prompt reexploration reduces tamponade, transfusion and mortality. Plasma and platelets would not correct a normal profile, and prothrombin complex concentrate adds thrombotic risk without addressing the bleeding vessel.
  187. The correct answer is A (Set ventricular output at two to three times the capture threshold and confirm capture): A pacemaker dependent patient needs a safety margin, and a typical output is two to three times the capture threshold, since thresholds rise in the first days from edema. Pacing at threshold risks sudden loss of capture. A very sensitive setting risks oversensing noise or far field signals, which inhibits pacing in a dependent patient.
  188. The correct answer is C (Etomidate with a moderate fentanyl dose, titrated slowly, with a vasopressor infusion running before induction): Etomidate causes little change in contractility, systemic resistance and venous tone, which suits a failing ventricle that depends on inotropes. A vasopressor already running protects the coronary and right ventricular perfusion pressure. A propofol bolus lowers preload and resistance sharply, and deep volatile induction depresses contractility and vasodilates, both poorly tolerated here.
  189. The correct answer is B (Obstruction to right atrial emptying raises systemic venous pressure, causing hepatic congestion and transudative ascites.): A stenotic tricuspid valve impedes right atrial emptying, so right atrial and systemic venous pressures climb and are transmitted to the hepatic veins, causing sinusoidal congestion and ascites with a high serum albumin gradient. The left ventricle and its filling are normal here. Ischemic hepatitis and cirrhosis would require prolonged low flow, which is not present.
  190. The correct answer is C (Ventricular remodeling causes secondary regurgitation, so guideline directed medical therapy comes first): Secondary regurgitation arises from left ventricular dilation, papillary muscle displacement, and annular enlargement in the setting of normal leaflets. First line management is guideline directed therapy for heart failure and resynchronization when indicated, because treating the ventricle lowers regurgitation. Degenerative leaflet disease belongs to primary regurgitation, and calcific annular disease is a different substrate.
  191. The correct answer is A (Add vasopressin at 0.03 to 0.04 units per minute for vasoplegia): A high cardiac index with low resistance after bypass in a patient on an ACE inhibitor indicates vasoplegia, and vasopressin restores tone through V1 receptors in a state of relative deficiency. More inotropy does not address the low resistance and adds tachycardia. Large volume loading raises filling pressure without correcting the vascular tone.
  192. The correct answer is C (Emergent pericardiocentesis with a drain, protamine to reverse heparin, and volume while arranging surgical backup): The findings of a circumferential effusion with right atrial systolic collapse and elevated central venous pressure indicate tamponade from perforation. Urgent percutaneous drainage restores filling, and heparin is reversed with protamine while volume supports preload. Reducing venous return or waiting for sternotomy delays decompression, which is the lifesaving step.
  193. The correct answer is B (Predicted hematocrit is about 27 percent, so no red cell transfusion is needed at the threshold.): Blood volume is 70 kg times 70 mL/kg, or 4,900 mL, and red cell volume is 4,900 times 0.36, or 1,764 mL. Total volume after the prime is 4,900 plus 1,700, or 6,600 mL, so hematocrit is 1,764 divided by 6,600, or 26.7 percent. That sits above the 24 percent threshold, so transfusion is not needed. The other values come from arithmetic errors in the dilution.
  194. The correct answer is A (He has a class 1 indication for cardiac resynchronization therapy with defibrillator capability.): Resynchronization therapy is a class 1 recommendation for patients in sinus rhythm with ejection fraction 35 percent or lower, NYHA class II to IV symptoms on therapy, and left bundle branch block with QRS of 150 ms or more. Benefit extends to patients with ejection fractions up to 35 percent, so waiting for further decline is unnecessary.
  195. The correct answer is A (Outflow tract obstruction from the displaced anterior leaflet and valve frame, given the small neo-outflow area): A projected neo-outflow tract area under about 1.7 cm2 predicts obstruction after valve deployment, since the displaced anterior leaflet and frame narrow the outflow, and basal septal hypertrophy adds to the risk. The gradient reflects ring stenosis rather than leak risk. Moderate pulmonary hypertension is not a contraindication to valve-in-ring replacement.
  196. The correct answer is B (Vasopressin at 0.04 units/min, restoring systemic resistance without increasing pulmonary vascular resistance): Vasoplegia after implantation is common, and the vasopressor choice should spare the right ventricle. Vasopressin raises systemic resistance with little effect on pulmonary resistance, which matters when right ventricular function is marginal. Phenylephrine can raise pulmonary resistance, and increasing speed when flow is already high risks suction events and further right ventricular strain.
  197. The correct answer is A (Use warm blood cardioplegia, keep systemic temperature above the thermal amplitude, and warm the room.): Cold agglutinins bind red cells below their thermal amplitude, causing agglutination and hemolysis that can occlude the coronary and systemic microcirculation. The strategy is to avoid cold, using warm cardioplegia and keeping temperatures above the amplitude, sometimes after preoperative plasma exchange. Deliberate cooling would provoke agglutination.
  198. The correct answer is B (Contact with artificial surfaces activates factor XII and alternative complement, releasing C3a and C5a.): When blood meets artificial surfaces, factor XII and the alternative complement pathway activate, generating C3a, C5a, kallikrein and cytokines, with later contributions from ischemia reperfusion and endotoxin release. Protamine-heparin complexes activate the classical pathway after bypass, not before. Hypothermia is not the principal trigger, and reduces rather than drives inflammation.
  199. The correct answer is B (Shortened diastolic filling time raises left atrial pressure and reduces transmitral flow and cardiac output): With a fixed mitral orifice, flow depends on diastolic duration, and the tachycardia sharply shortens it, so the transmitral gradient and left atrial pressure rise while forward output and pulmonary congestion worsen. Loss of atrial kick contributes but is secondary to the rate effect. Tachycardia does not cause a rise in vascular resistance that explains this picture. Treatment is rate control or immediate cardioversion.
  200. The correct answer is A (Causation, because the breach did not produce the injury that occurred): Malpractice requires duty, breach, causation and damages. The anesthesiologist owed a duty, the late antibiotic is accepted as a breach, and reoperation with prolonged hospitalization are real damages. The expert testimony that the infection would have occurred anyway breaks the link between breach and injury, so causation is the missing element.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Early valve replacement with debridement, liposomal amphotericin B or an echinocandin, then long term azole suppression): Fungal prosthetic valve endocarditis responds poorly to drug therapy alone, so surgical debridement and replacement are recommended with a prolonged antifungal course. Liposomal amphotericin B or an echinocandin is used first, followed by suppressive azole therapy for life or years. Fluconazole alone is inadequate for a large vegetation, and antibacterial drugs do not treat a yeast.
  2. The correct answer is B (Replete potassium and magnesium before bypass, since deficits raise ventricular arrhythmia risk after cross-clamp release.): Loop diuretics waste potassium and magnesium, and deficits shorten the action potential recovery and promote ventricular ectopy and fibrillation, especially after cross-clamp removal. Cardioplegia is delivered to the myocardium during arrest and does not correct body stores. Magnesium depletion does impair potassium repletion, but both ions need correction rather than magnesium alone. Replacement should begin preoperatively with ongoing monitoring.
  3. The correct answer is C (The circumflex artery, which supplies the lateral wall segments and the obtuse marginal territory.): The left main divides into the left anterior descending and circumflex arteries. The LAD supplies the anterior wall, anterior septum and apex, which are preserved here, while the circumflex supplies the lateral and anterolateral walls via the obtuse marginal branches. Abnormal anterolateral and inferolateral segments with a spared LAD territory localize the problem to the circumflex, likely a jailed branch or side-branch compromise from the stent. The posterior descending artery supplies the inferior septum, which is not the reported abnormality.
  4. The correct answer is B (High-frequency jet ventilation with very small tidal volumes, limiting chest and cardiac motion to stabilize contact): High-frequency jet ventilation delivers small gas volumes at rates of about 100 to 150 per minute, so chest wall and diaphragmatic excursion are minimal. This reduces catheter movement relative to the left atrial wall and improves lesion durability. Larger tidal volumes increase motion, and spontaneous breathing creates variable movement that cannot be reliably synchronized with the cardiac cycle.
  5. The correct answer is B (Septal shift with respiration and a mitral E velocity fall of over 25 percent on inspiration): A rigid pericardium isolates the ventricles from intrathoracic pressure and forces them to share volume, so inspiration shifts the septum and lowers left filling, which drops the mitral E velocity. Restriction shows low e prime and atrial enlargement. Inspiratory hepatic flow reversal and high pulmonary pressure favor restriction, whereas constriction reverses on expiration.
  6. The correct answer is A (Ductus venosus blood streams across the foramen ovale into the left atrium and ascending aorta.): Oxygenated umbilical blood bypasses the liver through the ductus venosus, and the eustachian valve directs it across the foramen ovale into the left atrium. From there it enters the left ventricle and ascending aorta, supplying the coronaries and brain with saturation near 80 percent. The ductus arteriosus carries mixed, less saturated blood from the pulmonary trunk to the descending aorta, so it does not raise upper body saturation. Superior vena cava blood is poorly saturated and streams mainly into the right ventricle.
  7. The correct answer is A (Cool for at least 20 to 30 minutes toward 18 C, confirming temperature or silence first.): Slow, adequate cooling allows the brain, which has poor heat exchange, to reach a uniform low temperature, and protection depends on achieving it. Electrocerebral silence generally occurs near 15 to 20 C and not at 28 C, and rapid cooling leaves the deep tissue warmer than the nasopharyngeal probe reads.
  8. The correct answer is C (The cannula tip has slipped out of the coronary sinus, leaking cardioplegia into the right atrium): Adequate retrograde delivery needs a coronary sinus pressure of about 30 to 40 mmHg. A low value at this flow means cardioplegia is escaping, most often because the cannula has dislodged into the right atrium or the balloon has deflated. Wedging in a small vein would produce abnormally high pressure, not low. Retrograde pressures of 5 to 10 mmHg do not deliver adequate protection.
  9. The correct answer is B (5.5 Wood units, above the usual transplant threshold, so vasodilator testing is needed before listing.): Pulmonary vascular resistance equals the mean pulmonary artery pressure minus the wedge pressure, divided by cardiac output: (38 minus 16) divided by 4.0 gives 5.5 Wood units. A value above about 5 Wood units raises the risk of right ventricular failure in the donor heart, so reversibility with vasodilators such as nitroprusside is tested. Dividing the mean pressure by flow without subtracting the wedge gives the incorrect 9.5. A value of 3.0 would not be generated by these numbers.
  10. The correct answer is C (Angiosarcoma, which most often arises in the right atrium and invades locally): Angiosarcoma is the most common primary cardiac sarcoma and favors the right atrium with a hemorrhagic effusion. Myxoma is benign, usually left atrial and not invasive. Metastatic involvement is more frequent than any primary tumor, but this location and pattern fit sarcoma.
  11. The correct answer is C (Nephrogenic fibrosis risk is highest with linear agents, so a macrocyclic group II agent is acceptable.): Nephrogenic systemic fibrosis is linked to gadolinium exposure when the filtration rate is below 30, and the risk is concentrated in older linear agents. Group II macrocyclic agents carry a very low reported risk and are acceptable when the information is needed. Iodinated contrast is the agent that causes contrast nephropathy, so swapping to it worsens renal risk. The disease is not confined to patients already on dialysis.
  12. The correct answer is A (Stop nitroprusside and give hydroxocobalamin 5 g intravenously for suspected cyanide toxicity): Tachyphylaxis with metabolic acidosis, a high lactate and an elevated mixed venous saturation, because cells cannot use oxygen, point to cyanide toxicity. Nitroprusside should be stopped and cyanide bound with hydroxocobalamin, with sodium thiosulfate as another option. Methemoglobinemia lowers oxygen content and does not cause a high venous saturation. Continuing the drug would worsen the toxicity.
  13. The correct answer is B (Judging heparin adequacy from the ACT alone, because the anticoagulant already prolongs the value.): Direct thrombin inhibitors such as dabigatran prolong the baseline ACT and add to the heparin effect, so a given ACT may overstate the heparin-driven anticoagulation and risk underdosing. Interpretation should account for the elevated baseline and use confirmatory assays. Baseline values and a second test with different activator are reasonable ways to check.
  14. The correct answer is A (Upgrade to cardiac resynchronization or conduction system pacing, because high right ventricular pacing burden causes dyssynchrony.): Chronic right ventricular apical pacing produces left bundle branch block morphology and dyssynchrony, which can cause pacing induced cardiomyopathy when the pacing burden exceeds about 20% to 40%. Upgrading to biventricular or conduction system pacing restores synchrony and often improves ejection fraction. Raising the rate leaves the dyssynchrony in place, and turning pacing off is unsafe in a patient with complete heart block.
  15. The correct answer is C (Do the preanesthetic evaluation, attend induction and emergence, stay immediately available, direct four rooms or fewer): Medicare medical direction requires the physician to perform the preanesthetic evaluation, take part in the most demanding procedures including induction and emergence, and be immediately available, with a limit of four concurrent procedures. Constant presence in every room is impossible with concurrent cases and is not required. Delegating the evaluation or supervising six rooms by telephone fails the conditions and the concurrency cap.
  16. The correct answer is B (Iron deposition in cardiomyocytes impairs function, and phlebotomy or chelation can improve it when started early.): Hemochromatosis causes iron overload with intracellular deposition that produces dilated or restrictive cardiomyopathy, arrhythmias and diabetes. Removing iron by phlebotomy or chelation can reverse dysfunction if begun early. Amyloid and sarcoid have different histology and treatments, and the skin and metabolic findings with iron indices point to iron overload.
  17. The correct answer is B (Give volume and lower speed if suction occurs, since the pattern indicates underfilling): The exhibit shows low flow with a low pulsatility index and normal power, together with a low central venous pressure and ongoing bleeding. This pattern means the ventricle is underfilled. Volume restores preload, and lowering speed avoids suction. Raising speed worsens suction, and normal power argues against thrombosis.
  18. The correct answer is C (Urgent surgery, since heart failure from severe regurgitation is the strongest indication, during this admission.): Heart failure caused by severe valve dysfunction is the most common and strongest indication for early surgery in infective endocarditis, and uncontrolled infection is another. Operating during the initial hospitalization improves outcomes compared with waiting to complete antibiotics. Delaying surgery in decompensated heart failure increases the risk of death.
  19. The correct answer is C (Catheter-induced right bundle branch block could cause complete heart block, so pacing must be immediately available): The catheter tip can transiently traumatize the right bundle during passage through the right ventricle, and in a patient with existing left bundle branch block this produces complete heart block. External pacing pads, a pacing-capable catheter or other pacing readiness is therefore the precaution. Pulmonary artery rupture and tricuspid injury are real catheter complications but are not increased by left bundle branch block.
  20. The correct answer is B (Keep heart rate near 90, preserve preload, and lower afterload with a vasodilator): In acute regurgitation the ventricle is small and stiff, so a heart rate near 90 shortens diastole and limits the regurgitant volume per beat, while preload and lower afterload promote forward flow. Bradycardia lengthens diastole and allows more regurgitation into an unprepared ventricle. Raising systemic vascular resistance increases the regurgitant fraction and worsens the ventricular pressure rise.
  21. The correct answer is C (Give phenylephrine or vasopressin to restore systemic resistance and reduce right-to-left shunting.): Induction lowered systemic vascular resistance in a fixed, high pulmonary resistance circuit, so more blood crosses right to left, dropping saturation and systemic pressure. A pure vasoconstrictor restores the resistance ratio and reverses the shunt. Large volume loads can distend a pressure-loaded right ventricle, and higher PEEP raises pulmonary resistance. Dobutamine causes further systemic vasodilation.
  22. The correct answer is A (Venoconstriction converts unstressed to stressed volume, raising mean systemic filling pressure and venous return.): About 70 percent of blood volume is in the veins, and most of it is unstressed volume that does not generate pressure. Alpha stimulation constricts capacitance veins, moving unstressed volume into stressed volume and raising mean systemic filling pressure, which augments venous return. Shrinking the stressed volume would reduce return. Fluid shifts from the interstitium are slow and do not account for an immediate rise.
  23. The correct answer is B (Complete device removal, with surgical extraction considered because a vegetation over 2 cm raises embolization risk): Device infection with bacteremia requires complete removal of the system. Vegetations larger than 2 cm are associated with a high risk of pulmonary embolism with percutaneous traction, so open or hybrid surgical extraction is considered. Retaining the device with antibiotics alone has a high relapse rate.
  24. The correct answer is C (Cinefluoroscopy or cardiac CT, which image occluder motion directly despite acoustic shadowing from the prosthesis.): Mechanical prostheses cast acoustic shadows that obscure the leaflets on echocardiography, so fluoroscopy or gated CT is used to assess leaflet excursion and detect thrombus or pannus. Ultrasound contrast does not overcome shadowing from the metal. Magnetic resonance produces susceptibility artifact from the prosthesis and is not the preferred test for leaflet motion.
  25. The correct answer is C (Coronary artery bypass grafting, since high anatomic complexity favors surgery for survival and fewer repeat procedures.): Randomized trials of left main disease show that stenting is a reasonable alternative only when the anatomic complexity is low to intermediate. With a high SYNTAX score, multivessel involvement and diabetes, surgery gives better durability and fewer repeat interventions. Significant left main stenosis carries a survival benefit from revascularization regardless of ejection fraction.
  26. The correct answer is B (Endocardial plaques of myofibroblasts and matrix coat the leaflets, thickening them and fixing them semi-open.): Carcinoid heart disease results from plaque-like deposits of smooth muscle cells, myofibroblasts and extracellular matrix on the endocardial surface of valve leaflets, driven by serotonin and other vasoactive peptides. The plaques thicken and retract the leaflets, so the valve becomes fixed, producing regurgitation with variable stenosis. Myxomatous change causes prolapse with redundant tissue, and nonbacterial thrombotic vegetations are small, mobile masses on closure lines rather than diffuse leaflet encasement.
  27. The correct answer is B (Give volume to restore venous drainage, then increase flow to raise mean arterial pressure.): Line chatter with a central venous pressure near 1 mmHg indicates inadequate venous return, so more speed would only cause cavitation, hemolysis, and falling flow. Replacing volume fixes the drainage limit, after which flow can be raised. Mean arterial pressure on ECMO rises with flow, so cutting flow would lower it further.
  28. The correct answer is A (Transcatheter edge-to-edge repair of the mitral valve, as in the COAPT trial population.): COAPT enrolled patients with severe secondary mitral regurgitation persisting despite maximally tolerated medical therapy, with ventricular size and pulmonary pressure within limits like this patient, and showed lower heart failure hospitalization and death with edge-to-edge repair. MITRA-FR enrolled sicker patients with disproportionately mild regurgitation and was neutral. Surgery has not shown comparable benefit.
  29. The correct answer is B (Blockade is prolonged because hypothermia reduces clearance and metabolism, despite a larger distribution volume): Hypothermia lowers hepatic and renal clearance of nondepolarizing relaxants and also reduces sensitivity at the neuromuscular junction, so recovery is slower. Hemodilution enlarges the volume of distribution but does not outweigh the slowed elimination. Benzodiazepines show a similar delayed clearance. The idea that clearance is temperature independent is not correct for these hepatically and renally cleared drugs.
  30. The correct answer is A (Isoflurane via the oxygenator at 0.5 to 1 percent provides anesthetic depth and lowers awareness.): Volatile agent crosses the membrane oxygenator readily into the blood, so a vaporizer on the sweep gas provides anesthetic depth during bypass and treats high pressures at the same time. This reduces awareness when propofol and opioid concentrations fall from hemodilution. The circuit does not absorb enough to prevent effect, and the sweep gas does not feed the cardioplegia circuit.
  31. The correct answer is C (Shortened diastolic time reduces coronary perfusion while increasing myocardial oxygen demand.): Most left ventricular coronary flow occurs in diastole, and tachycardia shortens diastole proportionally more than systole. Perfusion falls while demand rises with heart rate, so a stenotic territory becomes ischemic. Coronary sinus oxygen content is already low in a high-extraction state and would not rise. Tachycardia does not enlarge the end-diastolic volume, and it typically reduces it.
  32. The correct answer is A (Aspirin or an NSAID combined with colchicine, with gastric protection): Fever, pleuritic pain, rub and effusion weeks after surgery with negative cultures fit postpericardiotomy syndrome. First-line therapy is aspirin or an NSAID plus colchicine, which lowers recurrence. Steroids are second-line because they increase relapse risk, and antibiotics are not indicated with negative cultures.
  33. The correct answer is C (Synchronized cardioversion with phenylephrine support, since atrial contraction is critical to filling the stiff ventricle): A stiff hypertrophied ventricle depends on the atrial kick for as much as 40 percent of filling, so atrial fibrillation with hypotension and ischemia risk requires prompt rhythm restoration. Phenylephrine maintains coronary perfusion pressure meanwhile. Diltiazem and esmolol both reduce contractility and pressure, and neither restores sinus rhythm quickly.
  34. The correct answer is C (Pacing at 160 to 200 beats/min, which shortens diastolic filling and lowers stroke volume): Rapid ventricular pacing at about 160 to 200 beats/min shortens diastolic filling so stroke volume and pulse pressure fall, giving a quiet aorta for stent graft release. Rates near 100 do not lower pressure enough. Rates far above 200 may fail to capture and are not used. Pacing is tolerated poorly with reduced ejection fraction, so function should be assessed first.
  35. The correct answer is B (Intima and media are disrupted and contained by adventitia or clot, so rupture risk is high): A true aneurysm involves dilation of all three wall layers, whereas a pseudoaneurysm results from a disruption of intima and media with containment only by adventitia, clot or surrounding tissue. Because the wall is not intact, rupture risk is high regardless of size, and repair is generally advised. The statement that a true aneurysm lacks intima and media reverses the definitions. An intact wall describes a true aneurysm, which is not what a suture line outpouching with early fever suggests.
  36. The correct answer is A (Flow becomes linearly pressure dependent because maximal vasodilation has exhausted autoregulatory reserve.): Coronary autoregulation holds flow roughly constant between mean pressures of about 60 and 140 mmHg through arteriolar tone. Below the lower limit, resistance vessels are maximally dilated and flow falls in direct proportion to perfusion pressure. A stenosis shifts the lower limit upward, so ischemia occurs even sooner. Adenosine-mediated hyperemia follows a transient occlusion and cannot raise flow when vessels are already maximally dilated.
  37. The correct answer is C (Poor collateral flow through the circle of Willis, so add left carotid cannulation for bilateral perfusion): A selective drop confined to the left hemisphere while the right is well perfused suggests that crossover flow through the anterior communicating artery is inadequate. Adding a left carotid perfusion catheter provides bilateral perfusion and restores left saturation. Hemoglobin and temperature are unchanged, and a large drop on one side is a warning, not a tolerable difference.
  38. The correct answer is C (Have external pacing available for progressive conduction disease and avoid nephrotoxic drugs because of renal involvement): Fabry disease deposits glycosphingolipid in the conduction system and kidney, so bradyarrhythmia and high-grade block can appear and nephrotoxins add risk. Tachycardia shortens diastolic filling and worsens ischemia in a hypertrophied ventricle. Ketorolac and aminoglycosides are poor choices with reduced glomerular filtration.
  39. The correct answer is A (Ask the surgeon to stop cautery and place a magnet over the generator for asynchronous pacing.): Cautery is being oversensed as intrinsic cardiac activity, inhibiting pacing in a dependent patient. Stopping cautery removes the signal, and a magnet forces asynchronous pacing for the remainder of the case. Atropine does not help infranodal block, and pads over the generator are poorly placed while the interference continues.
  40. The correct answer is A (Warfarin with an INR target of 2.5, within a range of 2.0 to 3.0): Rheumatic mitral stenosis with atrial fibrillation carries very high embolic risk and warfarin is the recommended agent with an INR of 2 to 3. In rheumatic cohorts a direct oral anticoagulant did worse than a vitamin K antagonist, so it is not recommended. A target of 3.5 to 4.5 is higher than any valve guideline target and adds bleeding.
  41. The correct answer is B (Place the drain early, delay heparin an hour, keep CSF pressure at or below 10 mmHg): A lumbar drain should be placed before surgery with normal coagulation, and heparin is delayed about an hour after placement to limit spinal hematoma risk. CSF pressure is kept at or below about 10 mmHg to maximize spinal cord perfusion pressure during and after clamping. Placement after heparinization carries a high hematoma risk. Waiting until after clamp removal misses the period of greatest ischemia, and a ceiling of 25 mmHg is too permissive.
  42. The correct answer is C (Repair is reasonable, since diameter exceeds 5.0 cm and growth reached 0.5 cm within one year): For bicuspid aortopathy, the usual surgical threshold is 5.5 cm, and repair at 5.0 cm or above is reasonable with risk factors such as rapid growth, defined as 0.5 cm in one year. This man grew 0.5 cm (5.2 minus 4.7) in one year, so repair is reasonable. Waiting for 5.5 cm ignores the growth modifier. A threshold of 6.0 cm applies to degenerative aneurysms in some older tricuspid cohorts, not to a young man with a bicuspid valve.
  43. The correct answer is C (RA mean 4 mmHg, PA 24/9 mmHg, wedge 9 mmHg, mixed venous saturation 72 percent.): Normal resting values are a right atrial mean of 2 to 6 mmHg, pulmonary artery pressure about 25/10 mmHg with a mean near 15, wedge pressure 6 to 12 mmHg, and mixed venous saturation of 65 to 75 percent. The set with elevated right atrial, pulmonary artery and wedge pressures and low saturation describes heart failure with pulmonary venous hypertension. A mixed venous saturation of 85 percent is abnormally high and suggests a shunt or sepsis physiology, so that set is not normal.
  44. The correct answer is A (Mitral valve replacement, since commissurotomy is contraindicated by left atrial thrombus and moderate regurgitation): Symptomatic severe stenosis warrants intervention, and percutaneous balloon commissurotomy is preferred when morphology is favorable. Left atrial thrombus and more than mild regurgitation are contraindications because the balloon can embolize thrombus and worsen regurgitation. Surgical replacement is therefore the correct option, and waiting for a smaller valve area has no basis.
  45. The correct answer is B (Place air-eliminating filters on every intravenous line and remove bubbles before connecting.): With right-to-left shunting, air or particles entering a vein can cross to the systemic circulation and cause stroke or myocardial infarction. Filters on every line, along with careful de-airing, reduce this risk. Air does not dissipate in the venous system, and peripheral injections carry the same risk as central ones.
  46. The correct answer is A (Clamp the arterial line, call the surgeon, and hand crank the pump to restore flow.): A stopped pump with an arrested heart means no systemic flow, so flow must be restored at once with the hand crank, after clamping the arterial line to prevent retrograde blood loss through the cannula. The surgeon must be told, and the cause is then pursued. The heat exchanger also depends on power. Decannulation is impossible with an arrested heart and no circulation.
  47. The correct answer is C (Stop lisinopril, wait 36 hours, then start sacubitril-valsartan, and add a mineralocorticoid antagonist and SGLT2 inhibitor.): Replacing an ACE inhibitor with an angiotensin receptor-neprilysin inhibitor lowers mortality and hospitalization, but a 36-hour washout is required to avoid angioedema from combined ACE and neprilysin inhibition. Mineralocorticoid antagonists and SGLT2 inhibitors each add mortality benefit and fit this potassium and renal function. Digoxin improves symptoms and admissions but does not replace beta blockade.
  48. The correct answer is C (Isoproterenol or epinephrine, which stimulate beta receptors on the denervated sinus node and myocardium directly.): A transplanted heart has no vagal or sympathetic connections, so drugs that act through the autonomic nerves, such as atropine or indirect agents like ephedrine, are ineffective or unreliable. Direct beta agonists act on receptors that remain intact and raise rate and contractility. Anticholinesterases and glycopyrrolate also depend on intact parasympathetic input.
  49. The correct answer is A (ACT above 480 seconds, because circuit contact activates coagulation and risks clotting in the pump): Exposure of blood to the foreign surfaces of the circuit triggers contact activation and thrombin generation, so a high ACT, generally above 480 seconds, is required before bypass. Some centers use lower values, but 250 seconds is far too low. A value of 600 seconds is not required, and fibrinolysis is not the concern addressed by the threshold.
  50. The correct answer is A (Compression of the right ventricle impairs filling and outflow, while mitral annular distortion reduces forward output.): Verticalizing the heart folds the right ventricle and inflow tract, raising central venous pressure while reducing output. Distortion of the mitral annulus can add regurgitation. A rising central venous pressure with falling pressure points to mechanical compromise, which responds to volume loading, Trendelenburg positioning and surgeon repositioning.
  51. The correct answer is C (Pressure half-time below 200 ms with holodiastolic flow reversal in the descending thoracic aorta.): A rapidly falling regurgitant gradient gives a short pressure half-time, and below 200 ms indicates severe aortic regurgitation. Holodiastolic flow reversal in the descending thoracic aorta, particularly with an end-diastolic velocity above 20 cm/s, also supports severity. A long half-time above 500 ms is mild. A vena contracta below 0.3 cm and a narrow jet width also indicate mild regurgitation.
  52. The correct answer is C (Switch the trigger source to the arterial pressure waveform, or use the console's atrial fibrillation mode): An irregular rhythm makes ECG R-wave timing unreliable, so the console misfires. Triggering from the arterial pressure waveform, or using a dedicated atrial fibrillation setting, keeps inflation tied to the actual cardiac cycle. Lowering frequency does not correct the timing, and paced mode requires pacing spikes that this patient does not have.
  53. The correct answer is B (Atrial fibrillation, in which loss of organized atrial contraction abolishes the a wave on the tracing): The a wave reflects atrial contraction, so an irregularly irregular ventricular response with no a waves indicates atrial fibrillation with loss of coordinated atrial systole. Junctional rhythm is regular and produces cannon a waves rather than absent ones. Mobitz type I block shows a regularly irregular, grouped pattern with preserved atrial waves.
  54. The correct answer is A (General anesthesia with positive pressure ventilation before the effusion is drained, which can precipitate collapse.): In tamponade, venous return and cardiac output depend on high sympathetic tone, a fast heart rate and spontaneous negative pressure breathing. Induction with positive pressure ventilation and sympatholytic agents removes these supports and can cause cardiovascular collapse. Local anesthesia with ketamine and volume loading preserves them while the pericardium is drained.
  55. The correct answer is B (Avoid bradycardia and beta blockade, support heart rate near 90 with inotropes, and avoid balloon counterpulsation): Acute regurgitation depends on a faster heart rate to shorten diastole and limit regurgitant volume, so beta blockade usually fails and may cause collapse. A balloon pump increases the diastolic gradient driving regurgitant flow and can extend the dissection. Inotropic support and prompt bypass are preferred.
  56. The correct answer is C (Target 300 to 400 seconds, so give more heparin now because the 60-minute value is lower.): Left atrial ablation carries a risk of thrombus and stroke, so ACT is kept between 300 and 400 seconds. The 60-minute value of 290 seconds has fallen below that range, so a supplemental heparin dose is given and the ACT repeated. A target of 200 to 250 seconds is too low, and 450 to 500 seconds raises bleeding risk.
  57. The correct answer is A (Diastolic inflation pushes more blood back across the leaking valve, raising wall stress and worsening shock.): Inflation during diastole raises aortic root pressure, so in severe aortic regurgitation it increases the regurgitant volume entering a ventricle that is already failing. That raises end-diastolic pressure and wall tension, which defeats the purpose of support. The balloon in fact raises diastolic pressure rather than lowering it, and it does not obstruct the jet or close the mitral valve.
  58. The correct answer is A (Device related thrombus, managed with oral anticoagulation and repeat imaging in two to three months): A mobile or sessile echogenic mass on the atrial face of the device is consistent with device related thrombus, which carries an increased stroke risk and is treated with anticoagulation and repeat imaging to document resolution. Normal healing shows a smooth surface without mass. A leak is demonstrated by color flow around the device, and the stem describes none.
  59. The correct answer is C (Give volatile anesthetic via the oxygenator, adding nitroprusside if pressure remains high.): High pressure at a moderate flow of 2.0 L/min/m2 reflects high systemic vascular resistance, often from light anesthesia or catecholamine release. Volatile anesthetic through the oxygenator deepens anesthesia and vasodilates, and nitroprusside or clevidipine adds control. Cutting flow lowers the pressure by sacrificing perfusion. Raising flow would raise pressure further, not lower it.
  60. The correct answer is B (Return to sinus rhythm, though atrial fibrillation can still appear during follow-up.): Bidirectional block across the cavotricuspid isthmus removes the reentrant circuit, and the patient reverts to sinus rhythm. Atrial fibrillation develops in a large fraction of patients over follow-up, so monitoring continues. A slower flutter would imply a different circuit, and the isthmus lies away from the AV node.
  61. The correct answer is A (Briefly lower pump flow, raise the venous line, and add reservoir volume to clear it.): An air lock in the venous line breaks the siphon that drives drainage, so the reservoir drops and the venous pressure in the patient rises. Reducing flow, raising the line to float the air upward, and adding volume to restore the siphon lets the air pass into the reservoir. Increasing pump speed deepens the lock and risks emptying the reservoir. Clamping the line stops all drainage and leaves the lock in place.
  62. The correct answer is B (Trusting finger cuff output during severe vasoconstriction, when signal quality and accuracy degrade.): Volume clamp finger cuff devices depend on a good digital pulsatile signal, so cold hands, vasoconstrictors and low perfusion reduce reliability. Using them in warm, stable patients is acceptable, and comparing with another method after a hemodynamic change is a sensible check.
  63. The correct answer is C (Severe tricuspid regurgitation, because retrograde systolic flow enters the dilated hepatic venous system.): Systolic reversal of hepatic vein flow reflects regurgitant volume transmitted into the hepatic veins and is a specific sign of severe tricuspid regurgitation, in line with a vena contracta above 0.7 cm. Normal respiratory variation does not cause systolic reversal, and atrial contraction against a closed valve produces a diastolic reversal wave rather than a systolic one.
  64. The correct answer is C (ACT of 180 to 220 seconds or aPTT of 60 to 80 seconds, adjusted to bleeding.): Typical ECMO anticoagulation aims for an ACT near 180 to 220 seconds or an aPTT of roughly 1.5 to 2.5 times baseline, with the exact value adjusted to bleeding and thrombosis risk. Bypass-level ACT values greatly exceed what a circuit needs. A target close to normal leaves the circuit prone to clot.
  65. The correct answer is A (Severe mitral regurgitation, because vena contracta width and orifice area both exceed accepted severe thresholds.): Severe primary mitral regurgitation is indicated by a vena contracta of at least 0.7 cm, an effective regurgitant orifice area of at least 0.4 cm2 and a regurgitant volume of at least 60 mL. All three values here meet those cutoffs. The moderate and mild descriptions quote ranges that do not match the measured values.
  66. The correct answer is A (Provide lung-protective ventilation, avoid diuretics, and notify the blood bank to investigate the donor): The timing, bilateral infiltrates, hypotension and normal filling pressure fit transfusion-related acute lung injury, which is increased permeability edema. Treatment is supportive with lung-protective ventilation, and diuretics worsen hypotension. The blood bank must be notified so the donor can be investigated and implicated products quarantined. Steroids and plasma exchange have no proven benefit.
  67. The correct answer is B (Check wire connections and the pulse generator, then try higher output or an alternate wire): Stimuli with no following QRS indicate failure to capture, usually from a loose connection, a displaced or poorly contacting wire, or a threshold rise from edema. The first steps are to inspect connections and cables, increase output, and switch to another wire pair. Lowering output reduces the chance of capture, and sensitivity adjustment addresses sensing problems rather than capture.
  68. The correct answer is B (Preserve sinus rhythm, heart rate, preload and vascular tone, treating hypotension with a vasopressor): The stiff infiltrated ventricle has a small fixed stroke volume, so output depends on rate, atrial contraction and preload, and it tolerates vasodilation and bradycardia poorly. Beta blockers can precipitate low output. Vasopressors maintain perfusion pressure without depressing contractility.
  69. The correct answer is A (Hold beta blockade and maintain a heart rate of 90 to 100 beats/min): With acute severe regurgitation the ventricle cannot dilate to compensate, so tachycardia shortens diastole and limits regurgitant volume while preserving forward output. Beta blockade slows the rate and lengthens regurgitation, risking pulmonary edema. Phenylephrine raises afterload and the regurgitant fraction, worsening ventricular distension.
  70. The correct answer is A (Lower right pump flow to slightly below left pump flow, matching pulmonary venous return): If the right pump delivers more than the left can remove, blood accumulates in the pulmonary circuit, causing pulmonary edema. Right flow should run slightly below left flow, partly because bronchial return adds to the left side. Pushing the left pump hard risks suction and mismatch, and raising right flow worsens the congestion.
  71. The correct answer is B (A mean gradient of 40 mmHg or more with peak velocity of 4.0 m/s or higher.): Severe aortic stenosis is defined by a peak velocity of 4.0 m/s or more and a mean gradient of 40 mmHg or more. The peak-to-peak gradient is lower than the peak instantaneous Doppler gradient because the two peaks occur at different times, so the mean gradient is the best comparison. Thresholds of 30 and 60 mmHg do not match the guideline definition.
  72. The correct answer is B (Neointimal hyperplasia from smooth muscle proliferation within the stent): In-stent restenosis of a bare metal stent peaks around three to twelve months and results from proliferation of smooth muscle cells and extracellular matrix. Acute thrombosis occurs in the first days to weeks and presents as infarction. Fracture and recoil are uncommon and are not the typical cause.
  73. The correct answer is C (Atrial fibrillation for 10 weeks, apixaban taken with two missed doses during the last three weeks.): Cardioversion after 48 hours of atrial fibrillation needs at least three weeks of uninterrupted therapeutic anticoagulation or exclusion of thrombus by TEE. Missed doses in the last three weeks break that requirement. Recent onset under 48 hours with immediate anticoagulation, and eight weeks of documented adherence, both allow cardioversion without TEE.
  74. The correct answer is A (Vasodilator alone triggers reflex tachycardia and faster aortic pressure rise, increasing shear stress on the flap): The force of propagation of a dissection depends on the rate of rise of aortic pressure (dP/dt) as well as the pressure itself. Vasodilators lower pressure but trigger reflex sympathetic activation that raises dP/dt, so heart rate and contractility are controlled first. Coronary steal is not the rationale, and beta blockers do not meaningfully raise vascular resistance to spare vasodilator dose.
  75. The correct answer is C (Fibrosis and calcification of left sided valves, especially the aortic valve and aortomitral curtain): Radiation causes fibrosis and calcification of the valve leaflets and the aortomitral curtain, with a predilection for the left sided valves, which are exposed to higher pressure and are nearer to the beam. Right sided valves are less commonly affected. A typical finding is thickening of the aortic root, aortic valve, and base of the mitral leaflets with sparing of the leaflet tips.
  76. The correct answer is B (Use CT or another imaging study first, because an abandoned lead adds heating risk): A non-conditional device with an abandoned or fractured lead carries a real risk of radiofrequency heating at the lead tip, and asynchronous programming does not prevent it. Higher field strength increases that risk. Alternative imaging should be used unless a formal institutional protocol with device interrogation and monitoring is followed.
  77. The correct answer is B (Implant a pacing capable ICD because sarcoid conduction disease carries substantial ventricular arrhythmia risk.): Cardiac sarcoidosis with high grade block and ventricular scar carries a substantial risk of ventricular tachycardia and sudden death, so guidelines favor an ICD with pacing function rather than a pacemaker alone. Immunosuppression treats inflammation but does not remove the need for protection against syncope and bradycardia now. A pacemaker alone leaves the arrhythmic risk untreated.
  78. The correct answer is C (RV systolic pressure approaches aortic pressure, so right coronary flow occurs mainly in diastole.): A normal RV is perfused in both systole and diastole because RV pressure is far below aortic pressure. With RV systolic pressure near 85 mmHg, systolic flow is largely lost and the RCA gradient is aortic minus RV pressure, so a fall in systemic pressure produces ischemia. Diastolic flow persists because aortic diastolic pressure exceeds RV end-diastolic pressure. Autoregulation is not abolished by hypertrophy, although its reserve is reduced.
  79. The correct answer is C (Regional tamponade from a localized clot compressing the left atrium, impairing ventricular filling.): After cardiac surgery tamponade is often localized, and TEE can show a clot compressing the left atrium or right atrium without a large circumferential effusion. Clinical clues are hypotension with rising filling pressures and falling drain output. A small ventricle with a compressed atrium contradicts right ventricular failure or an outflow gradient.
  80. The correct answer is A (Accuracy falls with irregular rhythm and significant tricuspid regurgitation, since the method needs regular beats.): The right ventricular ejection fraction catheter derives the value from the exponential decay of each thermodilution curve and needs a regular rhythm. Atrial fibrillation, frequent ectopy and significant tricuspid regurgitation distort the washout and degrade accuracy. The device is self-contained and does not need echo calibration, and it samples the right ventricle.
  81. The correct answer is B (Reduced slope of phase 0 from fast sodium channel block slows conduction velocity.): Class I antiarrhythmics bind the fast sodium channel, which lowers the upstroke velocity of phase 0 and slows conduction through His-Purkinje tissue and ventricular muscle, widening the QRS. Calcium current governs the plateau, and enhanced current would affect repolarization rather than the QRS duration. Increased phase 4 slope raises automaticity but does not widen the complex.
  82. The correct answer is A (Quadrangular resection of the prolapsing segment with a complete annuloplasty ring): The jet is directed away from the prolapsing leaflet, so an anteriorly directed jet points to posterior leaflet prolapse, and the exhibit places it in the middle scallop. The segment height of 14 mm is moderate, so resection of the segment with a ring is the standard durable repair with a high rate of success. Replacement is reserved for failed repair, and anterior leaflet work is not needed.
  83. The correct answer is C (Start transcutaneous pacing with pads away from the generator, then interrogate and raise device output): Spikes without QRS complexes show failure to capture, which in a pacemaker-dependent patient is life threatening. Causes after cardioversion include a rise in lead threshold from myocardial injury at the lead tip and electrical reset of the device. The first step is to establish reliable pacing with transcutaneous pacing as a bridge, and interrogation then reveals the cause. Atropine does not act on an inexcitable myocardium, and a second shock may worsen injury.
  84. The correct answer is B (Roughly 20 to 40 percent are affected, peaking on postoperative days two and three): Postoperative atrial fibrillation occurs in about 20 to 40 percent of patients after CABG, more after valve surgery, with peak onset on postoperative days 2 and 3 as inflammation and sympathetic activity peak. An incidence of 5 percent is far too low, and onset in the first hour is unusual.
  85. The correct answer is B (Cannulating and cross-clamping the ascending aorta at the plaque site, risking atheroembolic stroke.): Severe ascending aortic atheroma, particularly protruding plaque over 5 mm, is a major predictor of embolic stroke when cannulated or clamped. Epiaortic scanning is more sensitive than palpation or TEE for this segment. Alternative cannulation sites and no-touch techniques are accepted strategies that reduce embolic risk.
  86. The correct answer is C (Maintain sinus rhythm, a heart rate of 60 to 80 and adequate preload with modest afterload): A stiff hypertrophied ventricle depends on atrial contraction, a long diastolic filling time and adequate preload. Tachycardia shortens filling and raises ischemia risk. Aggressive preload reduction and high-dose inotropes both lower filling volume or increase oxygen demand without helping relaxation.
  87. The correct answer is A (Therapeutic heparin, either unfractionated or low molecular weight, while treating the malignancy): Nonbacterial thrombotic endocarditis consists of sterile fibrin and platelet vegetations in a hypercoagulable state, so anticoagulation with heparin is the treatment. Warfarin is less effective in cancer related hypercoagulability. Antibiotics do not treat sterile vegetations, and the negative cultures and lack of valve destruction argue against infection.
  88. The correct answer is C (Combined pre and post capillary pulmonary hypertension with an elevated resistance): Mean pulmonary artery pressure of 40 mmHg meets the criteria for pulmonary hypertension, and a wedge pressure of 24 mmHg is above 15, which places a left-sided cause. A pulmonary vascular resistance of 3.2 Wood units exceeds 2, which adds a precapillary component. The transpulmonary gradient is 16 mmHg, with resistance calculated as 16 divided by 5.
  89. The correct answer is A (Valve, root and ascending aorta replaced by a valved conduit, with coronary buttons reimplanted): The Bentall operation uses a composite valved conduit to replace the aortic valve, sinuses and ascending aorta, with the coronary ostia mobilized as buttons and reimplanted into the graft. Preserving the leaflets inside a graft describes a valve-sparing reimplantation, which is not suitable for diseased leaflets. A supracoronary tube graft would leave the aneurysmal root and regurgitant valve.
  90. The correct answer is A (About 625 dyn s/cm5, low, consistent with vasoplegia, so vasopressin is reasonable): Systemic vascular resistance equals 80 times mean arterial pressure minus central venous pressure, divided by cardiac output. The calculation is 80 x (58 - 8) / 6.4 = 4,000 / 6.4, or about 625 dyn s/cm5. The normal range is 800 to 1,200, so the value is low and a high-output, low-pressure state after bypass fits vasoplegia. A vasoconstrictor such as vasopressin is appropriate.
  91. The correct answer is C (Give protamine slowly, with pulmonary artery pressure and right ventricular function watched, and pulmonary vasodilators ready.): Protamine can cause pulmonary vasoconstriction and acute right ventricular strain, which is poorly tolerated after left device implantation. A slow infusion with continuous pressure monitoring and vasodilator therapy available reduces that risk. A rapid bolus magnifies the reaction, while skipping reversal risks surgical bleeding and does not avoid thrombosis concerns.
  92. The correct answer is B (Dual-chamber mode minimizing ventricular pacing, because burden above about 20 percent raises cardiomyopathy risk): With intact atrioventricular conduction, the goal is to preserve intrinsic ventricular activation, because chronic right ventricular pacing at a burden above roughly 20 percent can cause dyssynchrony and a fall in ejection fraction. Dual-chamber pacing with algorithms or long atrioventricular delays that allow intrinsic conduction avoids this. A short fixed delay forces ventricular pacing on every beat and defeats this purpose.
  93. The correct answer is B (Hemodynamically significant, since values of 0.80 or lower support revascularization of the lesion): Fractional flow reserve is the ratio of distal to proximal coronary pressure under maximal hyperemia, and a value of 0.80 or lower indicates ischemia-producing stenosis. Trials such as FAME showed better outcomes when intervention is guided by this cutoff, so the other thresholds are too low.
  94. The correct answer is C (Type III reaction with pulmonary vasoconstriction, treated by stopping protamine and supporting the right ventricle.): Type I protamine reaction is hypotension from rapid infusion with normal pulmonary pressures, and type II is anaphylactoid with bronchospasm and rash. Doubling of pulmonary artery pressure with right ventricular failure is the catastrophic type III reaction caused by thromboxane release. Treatment is stopping protamine, giving epinephrine and inhaled pulmonary vasodilators, and returning to bypass with heparin if needed.
  95. The correct answer is A (Mammary patency is about 90 percent at ten years, versus 50 to 60 percent for vein.): The left internal mammary artery to the left anterior descending is resistant to atherosclerosis and has patency near ninety percent at ten years, with demonstrated survival benefit. Vein grafts suffer intimal hyperplasia and accelerated atherosclerosis, with ten-year patency near half. Lumen size does not protect vein grafts.
  96. The correct answer is B (Early reoperation before severe right ventricular dysfunction and liver failure develop): Isolated tricuspid surgery after left sided valve surgery carries a high operative mortality, and risk rises with right ventricular dysfunction, hepatic congestion, and renal impairment. Operating before those develop gives better outcomes than waiting. Diuretics alone do not correct the lesion, and her preserved right ventricular function makes this the best window.
  97. The correct answer is A (A 55-year-old with infarction shock, rising lactate despite a balloon pump, and a reversible coronary lesion): The device is a bridge, so the patient needs a reversible cause or a defined exit such as recovery, transplant or a durable device. Myocardial infarction shock with a treatable lesion fits. Acute aortic dissection is a contraindication because retrograde flow risks propagation, and a patient with no exit strategy has no destination.
  98. The correct answer is A (Maintain heart rate near 70 to 80 beats/min, adequate preload, and avoid increases in afterload): Stenosis wants a slower rate for diastolic filling, while regurgitation wants a faster rate to limit regurgitant time, so a moderate rate near 70 to 80 is the compromise. Preload must remain adequate for both, and afterload should not rise because it increases regurgitant flow. A slow rate prolongs regurgitation and a fast rate worsens the stenotic gradient, so either extreme worsens one lesion.
  99. The correct answer is C (Hemodilution and fentanyl uptake by circuit surfaces lower total levels, while free propofol fraction rises.): The prime dilutes plasma drug concentration, and fentanyl is lipophilic and binds to the circuit surfaces. Dilution of albumin and alpha-1 acid glycoprotein reduces binding, so free propofol is maintained or increases even as total falls. Hepatic clearance does not rise during bypass, and hypothermia lowers rather than raises elimination, so supplemental dosing is judged clinically.
  100. The correct answer is C (Maternal risk is very high with severe symptomatic mitral stenosis, so valvotomy is considered during pregnancy): Rising blood volume, cardiac output and heart rate increase the transmitral gradient and left atrial pressure, causing pulmonary edema, so severe symptomatic mitral stenosis falls into the highest maternal risk category. Percutaneous valvotomy is considered when symptoms persist despite medical therapy. Pregnancy shortens diastole through tachycardia, and stenotic lesions are tolerated worse than regurgitant ones.
  101.
