The correct answer is A (5 Wood units, with a transpulmonary gradient of 20 mmHg suggesting a precapillary component.): Pulmonary vascular resistance equals mean pulmonary artery pressure minus wedge pressure divided by cardiac output: (38 minus 18) divided by 4.0 gives 20 over 4.0, or 5 Wood units. A value above 3 Wood units with a transpulmonary gradient above 12 mmHg suggests a precapillary contribution beyond passive venous congestion. Dividing mean pressure alone by flow ignores the downstream pressure. Using cardiac index instead of output yields a different, incorrect number.
  102. The correct answer is C (Decompress the left ventricle immediately through a vent placed via the right superior pulmonary vein): With aortic regurgitation, blood from the root re enters the left ventricle, and a fibrillating, unclamped heart cannot eject it, so the ventricle distends and the wall is injured. Venting the left ventricle, together with prompt cross clamping and cardioplegia, relieves wall tension. Higher flow and higher systemic resistance both increase the regurgitant volume and worsen distension.
  103. The correct answer is A (Reverse heparin with protamine, drain the pericardium under echo guidance, and keep the surgical team ready.): Transseptal puncture can perforate the atrial wall, and the combination of hypotension, effusion, and chamber collapse is tamponade. Treatment is urgent drainage with heparin reversal, with surgery available if bleeding continues. Waiting wastes time during hemodynamic collapse, and appendage closure does not address a transseptal or free wall puncture site.
  104. The correct answer is A (Lidocaine and chronic amiodarone can raise the threshold, so avoid lidocaine loading): Class I sodium channel blockers such as lidocaine and chronic amiodarone raise the energy required to defibrillate, which can produce misleading test results or failure at expected energies. Class III agents such as sotalol and ibutilide tend to lower the threshold. Anesthetic technique matters, because some drugs and conditions alter the threshold, so the team should anticipate and report medications to the electrophysiologist.
  105. The correct answer is C (Atrial stretch receptors increase sympathetic outflow and reduce vagal tone to the sinoatrial node.): The Bainbridge reflex describes tachycardia after rapid atrial stretch, mediated through vagal afferents that raise sympathetic and lower vagal efferent tone. It is most evident when baseline heart rate is slow. Carotid baroreceptors would respond to a pressure rise by slowing the rate, and blood pressure did not change. Hemodilution does not meaningfully stimulate peripheral chemoreceptors.
  106. The correct answer is A (Add a left common carotid cannula for bilateral perfusion, since the circle of Willis is incomplete): A large unilateral NIRS drop during unilateral perfusion suggests inadequate crossflow through the circle of Willis, which is incomplete in a substantial minority of patients. Adding a left carotid cannula for bilateral selective perfusion treats the problem directly. A fall of more than 20 percent from baseline signals inadequate perfusion even when the absolute value is not very low. Doubling right sided flow risks hyperperfusion and raised pressure in the right hemisphere without reliably reaching the left.
  107. The correct answer is A (Dual-coil ICD lead dwelling nine years, laser sheath extraction planned, prior sternotomy): Leads that have been in place for more than one year are fibrosed to the vein and myocardium, so powered sheaths are usually required and a tear of the superior vena cava or right atrium can occur. Consensus recommends a surgical team and perfusion available for such extractions. Recent leads and temporary leads can generally be removed with traction because little fibrous encapsulation has formed.
  108. The correct answer is C (Venoarterial ECMO, because venovenous support gives no direct hemodynamic assistance to a failing heart.): Venovenous ECMO supports gas exchange only and returns blood to the right atrium, so cardiac output still depends on the failing ventricles. Venoarterial ECMO provides both oxygenation and circulatory support. Oxygenation alone does not rescue contractility in profound cardiogenic shock.
  109. The correct answer is A (Its output depends on afterload, and it can flow backward when stopped unless clamped.): A centrifugal pump imparts energy to blood through a spinning cone, so its flow varies with preload and afterload and it is nonocclusive, allowing retrograde flow when stopped. Roller pumps are occlusive and positive displacement, with a risk of dangerous line pressure when the line is obstructed. The distractors describe roller pump behavior.
  110. The correct answer is C (Start intravenous diltiazem or nicardipine and limit alpha agonist vasopressors when blood pressure allows.): The radial artery has thick muscular walls and is prone to spasm, and calcium channel blockers such as diltiazem or nicardipine, often with nitroglycerin, reduce this risk. Alpha agonists and vasopressin constrict the conduit and promote spasm, so they should be minimized. Raising perfusion pressure with a constrictor does not prevent spasm and may precipitate it.
  111. The correct answer is B (Confirm tube position by bronchoscopy, raise FiO2, and add PEEP to the left lung.): Hypoxemia during one-lung ventilation is first managed by verifying tube position by bronchoscopy, increasing FiO2, and recruiting the ventilated lung with PEEP, with CPAP applied to the nonventilated lung if needed. Very large tidal volumes cause barotrauma and hemodynamic compromise, and converting to sternotomy is unnecessary when simple measures have not been tried.
  112. The correct answer is A (Vena contracta at least 0.7 cm, regurgitant volume 60 mL, EROA 0.40 cm2 or greater): Severe primary mitral regurgitation is defined by a vena contracta of at least 0.7 cm, regurgitant volume of at least 60 mL per beat, regurgitant fraction of at least 50%, and effective regurgitant orifice area of at least 0.40 cm squared. The lower set represents mild to moderate disease, and the higher set exceeds the accepted thresholds.
  113. The correct answer is A (The displayed pressure will read about 7 mmHg higher than the true pressure at the heart.): A transducer positioned below the heart adds the hydrostatic weight of the fluid column, so it reads higher than the true pressure. Each 10 cm of water equals about 7.4 mmHg (10 divided by 1.36), so a 10 cm height difference adds roughly 7 mmHg. Reading lower would occur if the transducer were above the heart, and equating centimeters of water with mmHg overstates the error.
  114. The correct answer is B (Air entering the anterior right coronary ostium, producing transient right ventricular and inferior ischemia.): The right coronary ostium lies anteriorly in the supine patient, so air ejected from the left heart preferentially enters it, causing inferior ST elevation and right ventricular dysfunction that is usually transient. Management is to raise perfusion pressure and sometimes resume bypass. A patent mammary graft and inferior leads argue against anterior ischemia, and protamine reactions do not cause inferior ST elevation.
  115. The correct answer is B (Epiaortic ultrasound to pick a clear site, with axillary cannulation if the ascending aorta is diseased.): The high-velocity cannula jet sandblasts atheroma in the aortic wall and direct clamp or cannula manipulation dislodges plaque that travels to the carotids. Epiaortic imaging is more sensitive than TEE or palpation for locating a plaque-free segment, and axillary or femoral cannulation avoids the diseased segment altogether. Hypothermia does not reduce mechanical embolization and a faster flow does not lessen the jet's force.
  116. The correct answer is A (It acts on AT1 receptors to restore tone, and thrombosis risk rises, requiring prophylaxis.): Angiotensin II acts through AT1 receptors on vascular smooth muscle and raises MAP in catecholamine refractory vasoplegia, starting near 20 ng/kg/min. Trials showed more arterial and venous thromboembolic events, so prophylaxis is recommended. It does not act on vasopressin receptors, nor does it block nitric oxide synthase.
  117. The correct answer is C (About 1.3, below the usual threshold, suggesting high risk of right ventricular failure afterward): The index is (systolic minus diastolic pulmonary artery pressure) divided by central venous pressure, so (45 minus 22) divided by 18 equals 23 divided by 18, about 1.3. Values below roughly 1.85 predict right ventricular failure after an assist device. The other values do not follow from the numbers given.
  118. The correct answer is A (Give an additional 25 to 50 mg of protamine, guided by a heparinase corrected coagulation test): Heparin rebound occurs when heparin bound to tissue and proteins is released after protamine has been cleared, and it typically appears hours later with a prolonged ACT and normal platelets and fibrinogen. A small additional protamine dose, ideally guided by a heparinase corrected test, treats it directly. Fresh frozen plasma treats factor deficiency, which is not present here. Desmopressin addresses platelet or von Willebrand dysfunction and does not neutralize heparin.
  119. The correct answer is B (Acute dilation and failure of the chronically constrained, atrophic ventricle once the pericardium is removed.): Years of constraint leave the myocardium thin and atrophic, and when the restraint is removed the ventricle can dilate and fail acutely, producing low output with high filling pressures. Management includes inotropic support and careful volume restriction. Tamponade would show collapsed chambers, not a dilating ventricle, and the sequence of events here does not fit a protamine reaction.
  120. The correct answer is C (Withhold platelets because bleeding is minimal and clot strength on thromboelastography is normal.): Counts commonly fall to 80,000 to 120,000 after bypass from hemodilution and consumption, and function matters more than the number. A normal maximum amplitude with minimal bleeding shows adequate platelet function, so transfusion adds risk without benefit. Fibrinogen of 320 mg/dL is normal, so cryoprecipitate is not needed.
  121. The correct answer is A (Acute lung injury within six hours of transfusion, with no competing ARDS risk or hydrostatic cause): Consensus criteria define transfusion-related acute lung injury as new acute lung injury during or within 6 hours of transfusion, with no other ARDS risk factor or with a stable risk factor. Delayed onset with elevated wedge pressure describes circulatory overload. Overload typically causes hypertension and responds to diuretics, while injury causes hypotension and fever.
  122. The correct answer is A (Vasopressin by bolus and infusion, together with an inhaled pulmonary vasodilator): Vasopressin raises systemic resistance with little effect on pulmonary vascular resistance, which restores right coronary perfusion. Dobutamine lowers systemic resistance and can worsen hypotension despite raising output. A large volume load distends a failing right ventricle and shifts the septum.
  123. The correct answer is B (Give antithrombin concentrate, or fresh frozen plasma if unavailable, and recheck the ACT): Prolonged heparin exposure lowers antithrombin, and here the activity is 48 percent with a platelet count that is normal, so the ACT remains below the usual 480 second target. Replacing antithrombin restores heparin responsiveness. Protamine does not fix a deficiency. Low antithrombin with a normal platelet count does not indicate heparin induced thrombocytopenia.
  124. The correct answer is A (Severe paravalvular aortic regurgitation, with an aortic regurgitation index near 7): The aortic regurgitation index equals the aortic diastolic pressure minus the end diastolic pressure, divided by the systolic pressure, times 100, or (32 minus 24) divided by 110 times 100, which is about 7. Values under 25 predict poor outcome and indicate severe regurgitation. A narrow gradient between the aorta and ventricle in diastole defines it, and mismatch would not lower diastolic pressure.
  125. The correct answer is A (It yields fresh whole blood with working platelets and factors, while earlier donation risks preoperative anemia.): With acute normovolemic hemodilution, one or two units are withdrawn after induction with crystalloid replacement and reinfused after bypass, giving fresh blood with intact platelets and factors that bypass otherwise depletes. Predonation weeks earlier lowers hematocrit before surgery and requires stored blood, with clerical error and storage lesion risks. Hemodilution does not require erythropoietin priming. The stored blood from predonation does not contain functioning platelets.
  126. The correct answer is B (Stop the inotrope, give volume and phenylephrine, and slow the rate with esmolol before reassessing): Systolic anterior motion worsens with high contractility, low preload and low afterload, so the treatment reverses each of these. More epinephrine increases the outflow gradient. An intra-aortic balloon pump lowers afterload and would aggravate the obstruction, and persistent motion would call for returning to bypass.
  127. The correct answer is A (Cross clamp the aorta at once, give cardioplegia and decompress the left ventricle with a vent): A fibrillating ventricle cannot eject, and aortic regurgitation fills it continuously from the aorta, which causes injurious wall stretch. The fastest remedy is to clamp the aorta, arrest the heart with cardioplegia, and vent the ventricle. Defibrillation may not restore effective ejection quickly and delays protection. Reducing flow compromises perfusion without eliminating regurgitation.
  128. The correct answer is B (Emergency surgery, since thrombus of 0.8 cm2 or more with NYHA III or IV favors operation.): For left-sided prosthetic valve thrombosis in a critically ill patient with a large thrombus burden, emergency surgery is recommended, with fibrinolysis reserved for small thrombus areas under 0.8 cm2 or patients who are not operative candidates. Heparin alone is unlikely to clear a large obstructive thrombus quickly in a patient with pulmonary edema.
  129. The correct answer is C (Scheduled acetaminophen, a gabapentinoid, regional blocks, and low-dose opioid reserved for breakthrough pain): Multimodal analgesia combines acetaminophen, a gabapentinoid, regional techniques and a limited opioid to cut opioid use and side effects. Scheduled nonsteroidal drugs are generally avoided after cardiac surgery because of kidney, bleeding and graft concerns. Opioid-only plans produce more sedation, delirium and respiratory depression.
  130. The correct answer is B (A mobile intimal flap with a compressed true lumen and color flow entering a false lumen): The picture is that of an iatrogenic aortic dissection from the arterial cannula, with flow entering the false lumen. TEE can show an intimal flap, a compressed true lumen and color flow into the false lumen, and bypass should be stopped for reassessment. Left atrial appendage thrombus and a persistent left superior vena cava are unrelated findings.
  131. The correct answer is B (The aortic valve, adjacent to the anterior mitral leaflet): In the conventional en face surgical orientation, the aortic valve sits at the top of the image at 12 o'clock, next to the anterior leaflet, and the left atrial appendage lies near 9 to 10 o'clock. The posterior leaflet is at the bottom, away from the aorta. The septum does not occupy the top position in this standard view.
  132. The correct answer is A (Magnesium sulfate 2 g intravenously, then correct potassium and stop QT-prolonging drugs.): Recurrent torsades is treated with intravenous magnesium, which suppresses early afterdepolarizations even when serum magnesium is normal, plus potassium repletion and removal of offending drugs. Amiodarone and procainamide prolong the QT interval and can worsen torsades.
  133. The correct answer is B (Expected surgical myocardial injury, so trend the troponin and monitor without angiography.): After bypass, some troponin release is expected from surgical handling and cardioplegia. Diagnosing infarction requires a rise above ten times the reference limit together with new Q waves, angiographic graft occlusion or imaging evidence of new loss of myocardium. Without those features, serial observation is adequate.
  134. The correct answer is B (Increased contractility from an inotrope, shown by lower end-systolic volume at unchanged end-diastolic volume.): The second loop has the same end-diastolic volume of 120 mL but a lower end-systolic volume of 35 mL and a higher peak pressure, so stroke volume rises from 70 to 85 mL. That pattern, with a steeper end-systolic pressure-volume relationship, signals greater contractility. A preload increase would shift the loop rightward with a higher end-diastolic volume. A higher afterload would raise end-systolic volume and reduce stroke volume.
  135. The correct answer is A (Flow of 200 to 500 mL/min with superior vena cava pressure at or below 25 mmHg): Retrograde cerebral perfusion delivers cold oxygenated blood backward through the cerebral veins, helping to maintain cooling and flush embolic debris, but it provides little nutritive flow. Typical flow is 200 to 500 mL/min, and pressure is limited to about 25 mmHg to avoid cerebral edema. Higher pressure and flow risk brain swelling, while very low values give no benefit.
  136. The correct answer is B (Limit invasive vascular access, use ultrasound with the smallest catheter needed, and handle tissue gently): Vascular Ehlers-Danlos syndrome causes fragile arteries and veins throughout the body, so perforation, dissection and hematoma can follow routine cannulation. Access should be minimized, ultrasound guided, and use the smallest catheters possible, with gentle handling of tissues. Fragility is not confined to the aorta, so landmark placement of a large introducer invites vessel injury. A femoral arterial sheath does not carry a lower risk because peripheral arteries are equally affected.
  137. The correct answer is A (True severe stenosis with contractile reserve, so valve replacement should improve ventricular function and survival.): A rise in stroke volume of more than 20 percent defines contractile reserve. When flow increases, the gradient rises into the severe range while the valve area remains fixed near 0.8 cm2, which confirms a truly stenotic valve. In pseudo-severe stenosis the valve area would increase above 1.0 cm2 as flow improves. Patients with contractile reserve have acceptable operative risk and gain ventricular recovery from replacement.
  138. The correct answer is A (Early surgery within days, since a large vegetation with embolism and persistent bacteremia favors prompt operation): Guidelines support early surgery for a vegetation over 10 mm with an embolic event, or persistent bacteremia despite antibiotics. A small nonhemorrhagic infarct does not mandate a long delay, and the risk of further embolism outweighs it. Waiting for shrinkage or prolonged antibiotics exposes the patient to repeated emboli and valve destruction.
  139. The correct answer is C (Higher pulmonary vascular resistance widened the gradient, so diastolic pressure overestimates left atrial pressure.): Pulmonary artery diastolic pressure approximates left atrial pressure only when pulmonary vascular resistance is normal. In the chart diastolic pressure climbs from 24 to 42 mmHg while wedge pressure stays near 12 to 14 mmHg, so the gradient widens, a pattern seen with pulmonary vasoconstriction, tachycardia or pulmonary embolism. The flat wedge trend shows left atrial pressure is not rising.
  140. The correct answer is A (Elevated left ventricular filling pressure, with a distended central circulation not dependent on venous return.): In a normal subject, strain reduces venous return and arterial pressure falls in phase II, with an overshoot in phase IV. In congestive heart failure the central venous and pulmonary reservoirs are engorged, so the strain pressure is transmitted to the aorta and pressure remains flat, the square-wave response, with no overshoot. A fall during strain would indicate low filling pressure. Autonomic failure produces a progressive decline without overshoot.
  141. The correct answer is B (Continue cooling for 20 to 30 minutes in total so deep brain structures cool uniformly): Nasopharyngeal temperature reflects superficial brain and responds fast, so it can read the target before deep structures have cooled. Cooling for about 20 to 30 minutes with a gradient limited to roughly 10 degrees allows homogeneous cooling, and arterial or core temperatures should follow. Bladder temperature lags and is a poor guide to brain temperature.
  142. The correct answer is B (Transfusing below 7.5 g/dL was noninferior to a liberal threshold of 9.5 g/dL): TRICS III randomized cardiac surgery patients to a restrictive threshold of 7.5 g/dL or a liberal threshold of 9.5 g/dL and found the restrictive strategy noninferior for the composite of death, myocardial infarction, stroke and kidney injury. It did not show an advantage for higher thresholds, and it did not test a threshold of 6 g/dL.
  143. The correct answer is C (120 mg, from a residual heparin of 12,000 units in the circulating volume.): Residual heparin equals concentration times volume, which is 2.4 units/mL x 5,000 mL = 12,000 units. At 1 mg per 100 units this requires 120 mg of protamine. Dosing from the whole 38,000 units ignores heparin metabolized and redistributed during bypass, and gives 380 mg, which risks excess protamine. Halving the ratio would leave residual heparin active.
  144. The correct answer is B (Stop heparin, obtain urgent noncontrast head CT, maintain adequate ECMO flow, and involve neurosurgery at once): A new pupillary abnormality on anticoagulated ECMO is intracranial hemorrhage until proven otherwise. Heparin is held and imaging is obtained urgently, while circuit flow is maintained so systemic perfusion continues. Waiting delays diagnosis, and abrupt hypocapnia on ECMO is itself associated with neurologic injury and does not replace imaging.
  145. The correct answer is C (Irrigation delivers 2 to 3 L of saline, so diuresis and volume tracking are needed.): Irrigated radiofrequency catheters deliver large volumes of normal saline, often 2 to 3 L in long cases, which can overload a patient with a poor ejection fraction. Anesthesia should account for irrigant volume, limit maintenance fluid, and give a loop diuretic. The irrigant is isotonic saline, not free water, and the volume is far above 200 mL.
  146. The correct answer is A (Support the right ventricle, lower right atrial pressure, and plan PFO closure if shunting persists.): Elevated right atrial pressure after RV infarction can open a patent foramen ovale and create a right-to-left shunt, which explains hypoxemia that does not respond to oxygen with a clear lung field. Treatment is to improve RV function and unload the right atrium, using inotropes, reperfusion and pulmonary vasodilation, with percutaneous closure if shunting persists. More PEEP raises right-sided pressure and worsens the shunt. With a right atrial pressure already at 22 mmHg, further volume overdistends the RV.
  147. Left-sided valves are therefore spared unless there is a right-to-left shunt, a bronchial carcinoid or very high levels. Oxygen content and immune complexes do not explain the distribution.
  148. Procainamide and ibutilide slow accessory pathway conduction and are acceptable when cardioversion is not performed first.
  149.   150. The correct answer is B (Dorsal columns receive posterior spinal artery supply, so anterior spinal artery ischemia can go undetected): Somatosensory evoked potentials test the dorsal columns, which receive blood from the paired posterior spinal arteries. The motor tracts and anterior horn lie in the territory of the single anterior spinal artery, which is the usual site of injury after aortic clamping. Motor evoked potentials are therefore added, and a preserved sensory signal does not exclude motor injury.
  151. The correct answer is A (Two CentriMag pumps, one from right atrium to pulmonary artery, one from left atrium to aorta): Biventricular failure after bypass needs a pump on each side. CentriMag supports both a right-sided circuit and a left-sided circuit, allowing flows of several liters per minute with short-term durability. Left-sided support alone fails when the right ventricle cannot fill it, and a balloon pump adds only modest augmentation, which is inadequate for both ventricles.
  152. The correct answer is A (Increase sweep gas flow to the oxygenator, leaving FiO2 unchanged, to raise carbon dioxide clearance.): Carbon dioxide diffuses readily across the membrane and its removal depends on sweep gas flow, which is the ventilation of the oxygenator. Oxygenation depends on FiO2 and blood flow, and a PaO2 of 380 mmHg already shows ample oxygen transfer. Raising FiO2 does not change CO2 removal. Lowering sweep flow would worsen the hypercapnia.
  153. The correct answer is B (Melanin and extracranial blood absorb light and contaminate the signal, so trends outperform absolute values.): Near infrared spectroscopy samples a mixed venous-weighted tissue volume and includes scalp and skull contribution. Dark pigmentation and extracranial hematoma absorb light, so absolute baselines are less reliable and the percentage change from baseline is used instead. The signal does not depend on pulsatility, which is why it works during nonpulsatile bypass.
  154. The correct answer is A (It has limited efficacy and often causes significant regurgitation, so surgery is usually preferred): Balloon valvuloplasty of the tricuspid valve produces modest gradient reduction and commonly worsens regurgitation, which is poorly tolerated by the right ventricle. It also is limited when regurgitation already exists. Surgical repair or replacement, often at the time of left sided surgery, is therefore the usual approach.
  155. The correct answer is A (Nasopharyngeal temperature of 20.1 to 28 degrees C before systemic flow is stopped): The consensus classification of hypothermic circulatory arrest defines profound as 14 degrees C or below, deep as 14.1 to 20 degrees C, moderate as 20.1 to 28 degrees C and mild as 28.1 to 34 degrees C. Moderate hypothermia is commonly paired with antegrade selective cerebral perfusion. The 14.1 to 20 degree range describes deep hypothermic arrest used for arrest without perfusion. The 28.1 to 34 degree range is mild hypothermia.

Ultrasound confirmation of the needle in the vein on reattempt reduces repeat arterial puncture. Dilating the artery with a large introducer converts a minor injury into a major one that needs vascular repair. Advanced imaging and delay are not needed for a needle-only puncture.
  157. An alpha agonist or norepinephrine restores vascular tone and perfusion pressure. Transfusion at a hematocrit of 26 percent adds viscosity and exposure risk without addressing resistance. Raising flow to 3.5 L/min/m2 treats a problem that is not present and adds shear stress and aortic jet exposure.
  158. The correct answer is A (Heparinize, institute bypass, then turn the pump off and clamp the outflow graft before device removal.): When a continuous-flow pump is stopped, blood flows backward through the outflow graft into the ventricle unless the graft is clamped. Bypass with full heparinization protects the circulation during pump removal and ventricular closure. Without bypass, the patient would be exposed to regurgitant flow and have no support while the device is taken out.
  159. The correct answer is C (Aortic valve surgery is recommended because the ejection fraction has fallen to 55% or less): In asymptomatic severe regurgitation, surgery is a class 1 recommendation once the ejection fraction is 55% or less, because ventricular dysfunction can become irreversible. Waiting for symptoms or an extreme dimension delays operation past the safe window. Vasodilators are used for hypertension or inoperable patients, not to postpone indicated surgery.
  160. The correct answer is B (Abrupt sympathectomy lowers vascular resistance and venous return, causing hypotension and coronary hypoperfusion despite fixed obstruction): A fixed outflow obstruction prevents a compensatory rise in stroke volume when vascular resistance falls, so a spinal's rapid sympathectomy can drop coronary perfusion pressure and precipitate arrest. A high block tends to produce bradycardia, not tachycardia, from loss of cardiac sympathetic tone. Intrathecal opioids do not cause meaningful pulmonary vasoconstriction.
  161. The correct answer is A (Raise proximal mean pressure above 90 mmHg and restore distal aortic perfusion, then recheck evoked potentials): Loss of motor evoked potentials after clamping signals spinal cord ischemia. Raising proximal pressure and restoring distal perfusion improve spinal cord perfusion, with intercostal reattachment, hemoglobin optimization and CSF drainage as further steps. Volatile agents suppress motor evoked potentials and worsen monitoring, and more paralytic would abolish the signals falsely.
  162. The correct answer is C (Give stress-dose hydrocortisone, 100 mg intravenously at induction, followed by about 200 mg over 24 hours.): Chronic prednisone at 5 mg or more daily for over three weeks suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, and cardiac surgery is a major stressor. Hydrocortisone 100 mg at induction with about 200 mg over the next 24 hours replaces the expected cortisol response, then tapers back to baseline. Waiting for hypotension risks adrenal crisis. Stopping steroids would remove the baseline replacement entirely.

Collaterals would not produce ST depression on stress testing. No opioid use is described, and opioids do not explain the cardiac autonomic findings.
  164. The correct answer is A (Resume the beta blocker early postoperatively, and consider amiodarone prophylaxis in higher-risk patients): Early resumption of the beta blocker is the best evidence-based measure, and amiodarone adds benefit when risk is higher. Digoxin does not prevent postoperative atrial fibrillation and may even promote it. Magnesium trials show inconsistent results, so it is not a preferred prophylactic agent.
  165. Control of the rate or restoration of sinus rhythm usually improves ejection fraction substantially. Revascularization has no role when there is no obstructive disease.

The table also shows low magnesium, which further promotes ventricular ectopy. Calcium in the table is normal, so hypercalcemia is not present. Renal impairment does raise digoxin levels, but potassium and magnesium repletion remain essential to prevent arrhythmias before bypass.

Hypovolemia and suction produce low flow and low power, not rising power. A driveline infection does not cause hemolysis or an upward power trend of this kind.

Tranexamic acid is roughly ten times more potent, so doses differ greatly, with aminocaproic acid given in gram amounts. Direct serine protease inhibition describes aprotinin, not aminocaproic acid.

Heavier anticoagulation increases bleeding risk without restoring flow, and a larger cannula occupies even more of the vessel lumen.
  170. The correct answer is C (Monopolar bursts under 5 seconds, with the return pad on the thigh away from the chest.): Interference scales with proximity of the current path to the generator and with burst length. Short bursts of under 5 seconds and a return pad that keeps the circuit away from the chest minimize oversensing, and bipolar cautery would be better still. A pad on the arm or back puts the generator in the current path, and prolonged bursts increase the chance of inhibition.
  171. The correct answer is A (Reduced diastolic aortic pressure lowers coronary perfusion to a hypertrophied ventricle with high wall tension): A hypertrophied ventricle with high wall tension needs high coronary perfusion pressure, which falls when vasodilation lowers diastolic pressure, producing subendocardial ischemia and further decline in output. The rate here is 62, so tachycardia is not the cause. Propofol does not cause coronary vasospasm. Treatment is phenylephrine to restore perfusion pressure.
  172. The correct answer is A (Heart rate 70 to 90 beats/min, adequate preload and perfusion pressure, and avoidance of marked vasodilation): With mixed disease, stenosis requires a preserved perfusion pressure and adequate preload, while regurgitation requires avoiding bradycardia and excessive afterload, so a normal to slightly high rate and maintained diastolic pressure is the compromise. A slow rate with vasodilation drops coronary perfusion, and marked tachycardia shortens coronary filling time in a thickened ventricle.
  173. The correct answer is C (Viable hibernating myocardium that may improve in function after revascularization, supporting CABG.): Contractile reserve with low dose dobutamine indicates viable, hibernating myocardium with chronically reduced flow, which tends to recover function after revascularization and improves outcome in patients with severe dysfunction. Scar does not augment under dobutamine. Stunning follows an acute event with restored flow, whereas chronic stable multivessel disease points to hibernation that needs restored perfusion.
  174. The correct answer is B (Low-dose aspirin plus warfarin titrated to an INR of 2 to 3.): Giant coronary aneurysms, defined as 8 mm or larger, carry a high risk of thrombosis and are treated with low-dose aspirin plus an anticoagulant, typically warfarin to an INR of 2 to 3 or low-molecular-weight heparin. Aspirin alone is reserved for smaller aneurysms. Dual antiplatelet therapy does not address stasis within the aneurysm.

The result is a small left ventricle and low pump flow. Hypovolemia would give a low central venous pressure and a small right ventricle, and cannula malposition does not dilate the right heart.

Reopening and repositioning the graft restores flow. Suction would show a small ventricle, and thrombosis typically presents with rising power and hemolysis rather than an abrupt change at closure.

Because similar findings appear across the class and relate to workload and clerical burden, organizational change is the recommended response, with individual support as an addition. Treating it only as individual depression or only with mindfulness modules misses the system drivers.
  178. The correct answer is A (Place a percutaneous ventricular assist device before induction to maintain perfusion during unstable tachycardia): Patients with very low ejection fraction cannot tolerate sustained ventricular tachycardia, and mechanical circulatory support such as a transvalvular pump allows activation mapping while the rhythm persists. Vasopressors alone increase afterload and myocardial oxygen demand while cardioverting shortens mapping time. An intra-aortic balloon pump depends on regular ECG or pressure triggering and does not provide reliable support at rapid ventricular rates.
  179. The correct answer is C (Avoid balloon counterpulsation; use inotropes, preserve diastolic pressure and expedite aortic valve surgery): Diastolic inflation of an intra-aortic balloon increases the pressure gradient driving regurgitant flow into the ventricle, so it is avoided with significant aortic regurgitation. Peripheral ECMO raises aortic pressure and afterload, which increases regurgitation and left ventricular distension. In this hypotensive patient inotropic support with maintained diastolic pressure and early valve surgery is the sound approach. Vasodilators are reserved for when blood pressure allows, and any support device is chosen cautiously.

Disclosure and recusal, or declining the payment, protects patient and institutional interests. Fair market value does not remove influence, and donating the honorarium does not remove the conflict because the relationship and the benefit to the vendor persist.

The competent neoaortic valve and absent lipid history argue against the other two explanations. Serial observation delays treatment of a threatened territory. Fibrinolysis is contraindicated in a patient just out of a sternotomy because of the bleeding risk.
  183. The correct answer is B (Rewarming was too fast and overshot 37 C, so slow warming and cool slightly.): The rewarming slope is about 0.6 C per minute, above the common limit near 0.5 C per minute, and the final temperature exceeds 37 C. Rapid warming risks gas bubble emboli as dissolved gas comes out of solution, and brain temperatures above 37 C worsen neurologic injury. The plateau at 28 C was the intended cooling target, so it does not signal inadequate cooling.
  184. The correct answer is B (Prior infarction with structural heart disease, which makes ventricular tachycardia the most likely cause): In a patient with prior myocardial infarction and reduced ejection fraction, a regular wide complex tachycardia is ventricular tachycardia in the great majority of cases, and it should be treated as such. Rate does not separate the two mechanisms because supraventricular tachycardia commonly reaches 170 beats per minute. Regularity also fails because both are regular. Other helpful features include AV dissociation and fusion beats.
  185. The correct answer is A (It reduces omitted steps under stress when a designated member reads it, especially after rehearsal): Cognitive aids help teams recall infrequent but critical steps during stressful events, especially when a designated reader prompts the team, and they work best when practiced in simulation so that the team knows how to use them. They supplement and do not replace training. Documentation of times is a secondary benefit, and clinicians can act on an aid in real time.
  186. The correct answer is C (Deep hypothermia is about 14 to 20 C, and EEG silence or oximetry guides cooling.): By consensus classification, deep hypothermia falls between about 14 and 20 C, and 16 C is within this range. Cerebral metabolic suppression varies between patients, so many centers confirm electrocerebral silence or monitor cerebral oximetry before arrest. Temperatures of 24 to 28 C are moderate by this scheme, and below 10 C is not used in clinical practice.
  187. The correct answer is B (Maintain sinus rhythm, a normal-to-slow heart rate, preload and resistance, treating hypotension promptly with phenylephrine.): A hypertrophied ventricle behind a fixed obstruction needs a long diastolic time, atrial contraction, adequate preload and coronary perfusion pressure. Hypotension reduces coronary flow and rapidly leads to ischemia, so vasoconstrictors such as phenylephrine are preferred. Vasodilators drop coronary perfusion pressure, and tachycardia shortens diastole and raises oxygen demand.
  188. The correct answer is C (Conservative medical therapy with a beta blocker and inpatient monitoring): Stable patients with spontaneous dissection and preserved flow do best with conservative therapy, since most dissections heal on their own. Stenting is technically difficult and risks propagating the intramural hematoma. Fibrinolysis can expand the hematoma and worsen the lumen compromise.
  189. The correct answer is B (Continue oral therapy throughout, switching to inhaled or intravenous agents if enteral intake is interrupted.): Abrupt interruption of pulmonary vasodilators can precipitate rebound pulmonary hypertension and a fall in saturation from increased right-to-left shunting. Therapy is therefore continued, with inhaled iloprost or epoprostenol, or intravenous sildenafil, bridging any period without oral intake. Holding drugs removes protection of the pulmonary circulation, and calcium channel blockers are harmful in Eisenmenger physiology because they lower systemic resistance and depress contractility.
  190. The correct answer is C (Right axillary artery through an 8 mm graft, allowing antegrade flow and later selective cerebral perfusion): Right axillary cannulation gives antegrade flow, lowers retrograde embolization risk, and permits selective cerebral perfusion during arrest. Femoral cannulation sends flow retrograde, which may pressurize the false lumen and cause malperfusion or atheroembolism. Cannulating dissected ascending aorta risks misplacement into the false lumen and extension of the dissection.
  191. The correct answer is A (Hold the morning dose to limit vasoplegia, and restart once hemodynamics stabilize after the operation.): Angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors and receptor blockers taken within 24 hours of surgery increase the incidence of vasoplegia and hypotension on bypass, so holding the morning dose is favored. Withholding a single dose does not cause rebound heart failure, and the drug can be resumed after stabilization. Intravenous enalaprilat preserves the same blockade and the same hypotensive risk, so the substitution defeats the purpose.
  192. The correct answer is C (Shear stress in the pump unfolds von Willebrand factor, which ADAMTS13 cleaves, producing acquired deficiency.): High shear within continuous-flow pumps unfolds large von Willebrand multimers, which are then cleaved by ADAMTS13, leading to acquired von Willebrand syndrome. This combines with angiodysplasia and anticoagulation to cause gastrointestinal bleeding. The platelet count is normal, so heparin-induced thrombocytopenia is not the cause.
  193. The correct answer is C (The smaller lumen that expands in systole with brisk antegrade systolic color flow): The true lumen is usually smaller, round, and expands in systole with fast antegrade color flow because it is continuous with the normal aortic channel. The false lumen is typically larger and crescentic, narrows or collapses in systole, and shows delayed, reduced or reversed flow, spontaneous echo contrast or thrombus. The larger lumen with sluggish flow and the lumen that narrows in systole are both false lumen features. Identifying the true lumen guides cannulation and wire placement.
  194. The correct answer is A (Withhold volume, start an inotrope with an inhaled pulmonary vasodilator, then recheck septal position on TEE): CVP does not predict volume responsiveness in a failing, distended right ventricle. At 18 mmHg with leftward septal shift, further volume worsens ventricular interdependence and reduces left-sided filling. Inotropic support and lowering pulmonary vascular resistance unload the right ventricle, and TEE confirms improvement in septal geometry. Phenylephrine raises pulmonary resistance and does not treat right ventricular dysfunction.
  195. The correct answer is C (Atrioventricular groove disruption, from debridement or prosthesis seating in friable calcified annulus): Severe annular calcification makes the tissue friable, and aggressive debridement or forceful seating of a prosthesis can tear the atrioventricular groove, which presents as sudden hemorrhage and hemodynamic collapse. It carries high mortality. Appendage avulsion and sinus thrombosis do not match the timing or the site of bleeding.
  196. The correct answer is A (Deliver up to three sequential defibrillation shocks immediately before starting external chest compressions): The CALS protocol for cardiac surgery patients calls for up to three immediate stacked shocks because ventricular fibrillation is the usual rhythm and defibrillation is faster and more effective than compressions. Epinephrine is avoided, since boluses risk hypertension and bleeding after return of circulation. Resternotomy follows within 5 minutes if shocks fail, not before them.
  197. The correct answer is C (Prepare for peripheral cannulation, place defibrillator pads, and have cross-matched blood immediately available): Re-entry can lacerate the right ventricle, aorta or patent graft, so femoral access for bypass, pads and blood must be ready first. Mammary grafts and adhesions make re-entry hazardous, not simple. TEE is useful, and late blood preparation is unsafe.
  198. The correct answer is B (A 58-year-old with sinus node dysfunction, sinus pauses, intact AV conduction, and a narrow QRS.): A ventricular leadless device cannot pace the atrium and does not give AV synchrony, so sinus node dysfunction with intact conduction is better treated with atrial-based pacing to avoid pacing-induced problems. Permanent atrial fibrillation with slow ventricular response is the ideal indication, and venous occlusion or pocket infection further favors a leadless approach.
  199. The correct answer is B (Ongoing mediastinal bleeding needing repeated transfusion and a likely return to the operating room): Fast-track extubation suits normothermic, awake, stable patients with controlled bleeding. Bleeding that needs repeated transfusion and probable reexploration argues for keeping the patient sedated and ventilated, since an emergency return to the operating room is safer with a secured airway. Well-controlled pain and a low-dose vasopressor with a normal cardiac index do not preclude early extubation.
  200. The correct answer is A (Maintain heart rate near 80 to 90 beats/min and avoid increases in systemic vascular resistance): Bradycardia lengthens diastole, which increases regurgitant volume and ventricular distension, so a heart rate of 80 to 90 is preferred. Increased afterload raises the gradient driving regurgitant flow and worsens forward output. Phenylephrine and a slow rate are therefore harmful here, even though they would be favorable in aortic stenosis.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (A long run of points below the center line signals special cause variation within the limits): Control chart rules flag special cause not only for points beyond limits but also for a run of about eight or more consecutive points on one side of the center line. Weeks 10 through 20 all fall below the center line, indicating a real shift. Concluding stability from the limits alone misses this rule, and reacting to every fluctuation is tampering that increases variation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Catheter-induced right bundle branch block could cause complete heart block, so pacing capability must be ready.): Calcium from a stenotic aortic valve can extend into the septum and damage the left bundle, so a new left bundle branch block signals a vulnerable conduction system. Passing a pulmonary artery catheter through the right ventricle can injure the right bundle, and a new right bundle block on top of a left block produces complete heart block. Pacing must therefore be immediately available, and a pacing catheter or external pads are reasonable. Avoiding the catheter is not required, and epicardial wires are routinely placed after valve surgery and usually capture.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop all heparin including flushes, send PF4 antibody testing, and start argatroban or bivalirudin): A 4T score of 6 means high probability, so all heparin exposure including flushes is stopped, antibody testing is sent, and a nonheparin anticoagulant such as argatroban or bivalirudin is started. Continuing heparin risks thrombosis. Low molecular weight heparin still cross-reacts and is contraindicated.
  4. The correct answer is B (Marked QRS prolongation above 180 ms, reflecting right ventricular dilation and slowed conduction.): QRS duration above 180 ms is the best established electrocardiographic marker of ventricular arrhythmia and sudden death after tetralogy repair, because it follows right ventricular dilation and slowed conduction through scar. Right bundle branch block is nearly universal after repair and carries little predictive value. Regurgitant severity drives dilation but is not itself the strongest arrhythmia predictor.
  5. The correct answer is B (Gradient at or below 10 C between inflow and venous return, inflow at most 37 C): Gas solubility falls as blood warms, so a large gradient between inflow and venous return risks gas bubble formation and microemboli. The accepted limit is about 10 C, and arterial inflow should stay at or below 37 C to avoid cerebral hyperthermia and injury. Higher inflow temperatures raise brain temperature above safe levels and worsen neurologic outcomes.
  6. The correct answer is C (The AV nodal artery arises from the circumflex, so pace and restore circumflex flow quickly.): In a left dominant system the circumflex gives rise to the posterior descending artery and the AV nodal artery, so a proximal occlusion causes inferior ischemia and nodal ischemia with high-grade block. The plan is to pace, restore flow with a bypass graft or shunt, and watch for hemodynamic collapse. Septal perforators supply the bundle branches rather than the node. Retractor-related vagal effects would not produce simultaneous inferior ST elevation.
  7. The correct answer is B (Flow of about 10 mL/kg/min with right radial pressure of about 50 to 70 mmHg): Common practice delivers about 10 mL/kg/min with a perfusion pressure of 50 to 70 mmHg, measured in the right radial artery, to maintain cerebral perfusion during arrest of the lower body. Flows and pressures much higher risk cerebral edema, while very low values risk ischemia. Cerebral oximetry is used to guide adjustment.
  8. The correct answer is B (Suppress shivering with meperidine or dexmedetomidine and continue gentle rewarming to reduce oxygen consumption): Shivering can raise oxygen consumption by 100 to 400 percent, which lowers SvO2 when cardiac output cannot compensate. Treating shivering with meperidine or dexmedetomidine while continuing to rewarm restores balance. Recooling is counterproductive and increases shivering risk on rewarming again. Raising FiO2 does not address increased consumption.
  9. The correct answer is A (Stop inotropes, give volume, start phenylephrine, and slow the heart rate with esmolol): The obstruction worsens with hyperdynamic contraction, low preload, and low afterload. Treatment reverses those factors with volume, a pure vasoconstrictor, beta blockade, and withdrawal of inotropes. Dobutamine, milrinone, and vasodilators all increase the gradient by raising contractility or reducing filling and afterload.
  10. The correct answer is C (Choose the device from the largest ostial diameter across multiplane views, here 22 mm): Device sizing for appendage occlusion is based on the maximal ostial diameter measured across the multiplane views, typically at zero, 45, 90 and 135 degrees, with an appropriate degree of compression of the device in the appendage. The smallest measurement underestimates the ostium and would produce an undersized device with leak or embolization risk. Depth confirms that the device fits but does not set the diameter.
  11. The correct answer is A (Use hemofiltration to remove drug, and rely on surgical hemostasis plus blood product support): Bivalirudin is cleared mainly by plasma proteolysis with about 20 percent renal elimination, so its half life of roughly 25 minutes can extend toward 3 hours in dialysis dependent patients. No specific reversal agent exists, and hemofiltration or ultrafiltration can remove the drug. Protamine does not bind direct thrombin inhibitors. Recombinant factor VIIa is a prothrombotic rescue agent and does not reverse the drug.
  12. The correct answer is C (Occlude the site, lower the head with left lateral positioning, and aspirate air through the catheter.): The presentation fits venous air embolism. Management is to prevent further entry, place the patient in left lateral decubitus with head down so air collects in the right ventricular apex away from the outflow tract, and aspirate through the catheter. Raising the head worsens air entry, and thrombolytics do not treat air.
  13. The correct answer is B (Target normocapnia with head elevation, since hypercapnia raises SVC pressure and hypocapnia cuts Glenn flow.): In a Glenn circulation the SVC drains directly to the pulmonary arteries, so cerebral blood flow determines pulmonary flow. Here the SVC pressure is already 14 mmHg and saturation fell with PaCO2 55 mmHg, so hypercapnia is raising pulmonary resistance and SVC pressure more than it helps flow. Hypocapnia lowers cerebral and pulmonary flow and saturation. Normocapnia with head elevation lowers SVC and cerebral venous pressure.
  14. The correct answer is C (Systolic anterior motion of the anterior leaflet, worsened by inotropy and hypovolemia.): A hyperdynamic, underfilled ventricle after repair of a redundant leaflet predisposes to systolic anterior motion, in which the leaflet is drawn into the outflow tract, creating a gradient and posteriorly directed regurgitation. Treatment is volume, phenylephrine and stopping the inotrope. Ring dehiscence or circumflex injury would not produce an outflow gradient.
  15. The correct answer is A (Mismatch from marked hypertrophy, high wall stress and abnormal intramural arterioles with elevated diastolic pressure): In this disease oxygen demand rises with wall thickness and outflow obstruction, while thickened small vessels and high diastolic pressure limit supply to the subendocardium. Septal collateral steal is not a described mechanism. Mural thrombus embolization would produce an obstruction visible at angiography.
  16. The correct answer is C (Stop pumping and remove the catheter promptly, since blood inside the balloon can clot): Blood in the helium line means the balloon membrane has ruptured and blood is entering the balloon. Blood that stays there clots, and the balloon can become entrapped in the artery, so counterpulsation is stopped and the catheter is removed or exchanged without delay. Continuing at a lower frequency or flushing the lumen leaves blood in the balloon, and repositioning does nothing for a torn membrane.
  17. The correct answer is A (It overestimates contractility because ejection into the low-pressure left atrium lowers afterload and masks dysfunction): In severe primary regurgitation the ventricle unloads into the left atrium, so ejection fraction stays pseudonormal even as myocardial function declines. An ejection fraction of 60% or less, or end-systolic dimension of 40 mm or more, already signals ventricular dysfunction and supports surgery. Ejection fraction is strongly load dependent.
  18. The correct answer is B (Escalate to a temporary ventricular assist device such as Impella 5.5 or VA ECMO): A cardiac index of 1.6 with rising lactate, low SvO2 and oliguria despite two inotropes and an intra-aortic balloon pump shows refractory cardiogenic shock. The next step is temporary mechanical circulatory support such as Impella or VA ECMO. More catecholamine raises oxygen demand and arrhythmia risk, and reducing balloon support removes the little help it gives.
  19. The correct answer is C (Place transcutaneous pacing pads and a pacing catheter, since added left bundle injury causes block): The right bundle is the more vulnerable fascicle and is already blocked, so even transient trauma to the left bundle during passage through the right ventricle can produce complete heart block. Pacing pads and a pacing-capable catheter allow immediate rescue. Right bundle block alone is not a contraindication, and an inflated balloon is what carries the catheter forward with flow and limits wall irritation.
  20. The correct answer is C (Reduced right ventricular systolic function, because both values fall below accepted lower reference limits.): A TAPSE below about 17 mm and a fractional area change below about 35 percent both indicate right ventricular systolic dysfunction. Both values here are well below those limits, so right ventricular dysfunction is present and may explain the hypotension. A small excursion reflects reduced, not vigorous, longitudinal shortening.
  21. The correct answer is B (Place a temporary right ventricular assist device, since refractory right failure limits left pump filling.): Persistent right ventricular failure on maximal medical therapy, with high venous pressure and low pulmonary artery pulsatility index, calls for mechanical right-sided support. Increasing speed would worsen septal shift and suction. Further delay risks hepatic and renal injury and bleeding from congestion.
  22. The correct answer is B (Hold the morning dose on the day of surgery and resume after renal function stabilizes): Metformin impairs hepatic lactate clearance, and bypass with hypoperfusion, hypothermia, and possible kidney injury raises the risk of metformin-associated lactic acidosis. It is held on the morning of surgery and restarted when renal function is stable. Continuing it makes postoperative lactate harder to interpret and increases the risk of severe acidosis.
  23. The correct answer is A (Stop the colleague from starting care, notify the chair or wellness program, and arrange coverage): Suspected impairment before a high-risk case is an immediate patient safety issue, and the duty is to stop the colleague from providing care and notify the responsible leader, while arranging a replacement. Waiting until after the case or monitoring informally exposes the patient to avoidable risk. Reporting also directs the colleague toward assessment and support.
  24. The correct answer is A (Increase pump flow and perfusion pressure to restore oxygen delivery, and consider raising the hematocrit.): A falling mixed venous saturation, lactate near 7 mmol/L and a base deficit show anaerobic metabolism from inadequate oxygen delivery, as flow is modest and hematocrit is 21 percent. Delivery rises with higher flow, pressure or hematocrit. Bicarbonate treats the number without correcting the cause, and lowering flow would worsen the deficit.
  25. The correct answer is C (Diffuse subendocardial ischemia from critical left main or proximal multivessel coronary stenosis): ST elevation in aVR with widespread ST depression reflects global subendocardial ischemia and is a recognized marker of left main or severe three-vessel disease. Pericarditis causes diffuse ST elevation with PR depression, and aVR shows the reciprocal ST depression with PR elevation, the opposite of this tracing. An isolated right coronary occlusion would produce inferior ST elevation, not inferior ST depression.
  26. The correct answer is B (Give insulin with dextrose and calcium, and remove potassium by circuit hemofiltration before weaning.): A potassium of 6.4 mEq/L with peaked T waves and wide QRS threatens rhythm and contractility, and cardioplegia delivery with acidosis explains it. Calcium stabilizes the membrane, insulin with glucose shifts potassium into cells, and ultrafiltration removes it on bypass. Weaning with this potassium risks arrhythmia. Polystyrene sulfonate acts over hours, not minutes.
  27. The correct answer is C (Slow upstroke with delayed peak and narrow pulse pressure, typical of fixed outflow obstruction): The tracing shows a slowly rising, late-peaking waveform with a pulse pressure of about 44 mmHg, the pulsus parvus et tardus pattern of severe aortic stenosis. Aortic regurgitation produces a brisk upstroke, wide pulse pressure and low diastolic pressure. Pulsus alternans alternates amplitude between beats, which this tracing does not show.
  28. The correct answer is A (A bioprosthetic valve, accepting a later transcatheter valve-in-valve, to avoid warfarin exposure during pregnancy.): Mechanical valves in the low-pressure pulmonic position have a high rate of thrombosis and need warfarin, which is a problem in pregnancy and a teratogenic exposure risk. A bioprosthesis avoids anticoagulation, and a catheter-delivered valve-in-valve can be used when degeneration occurs. A homograft does not need anticoagulation and calcifies early in young recipients, so that option is wrong on both counts.
  29. The correct answer is C (Add left carotid selective perfusion for bilateral delivery, since the left side lacks adequate crossflow.): A stable right signal with a left signal falling more than 20 percent from baseline shows the left hemisphere is relying on crossflow through an incomplete circle of Willis. Right radial pressure reflects the perfused right side only and does not guarantee left perfusion. Adding left carotid cannulation for bilateral perfusion treats the real deficit.
  30. The correct answer is B (A sternotomy tray in the room, a perfusionist with a primed circuit, and blood products available.): Emergent conversion after annular rupture, coronary obstruction or ventricular perforation requires bypass within minutes, so the surgical team, an open or immediately available sternotomy tray, a primed circuit and blood products should be in the room before deployment. Waiting for a complication before calling for help delays bypass and increases mortality.
  31. The correct answer is C (Obtain urgent transesophageal echocardiography and return to the operating room for evacuation of posterior clot.): After cardiac surgery, localized clot can compress a single chamber, typically the left atrium or right atrium, producing tamponade without the classic circumferential effusion. Transthoracic echocardiography often misses it, so transesophageal imaging is used. Clotted blood does not drain percutaneously, and delay allows progressive shock.
  32. The correct answer is A (Low potassium raises digoxin binding to the sodium-potassium ATPase, enhancing intracellular calcium loading.): Digoxin inhibits the sodium-potassium ATPase, raising intracellular sodium and reducing calcium extrusion through the sodium-calcium exchanger, which raises calcium stores and force. Potassium competes with digoxin at the same binding site, so hypokalemia increases digoxin binding and the toxic effect even at a normal serum level. Clearance is not the mechanism. The exchanger is not blocked by low potassium.
  33. The correct answer is B (Collaterals from the enlarged right coronary reverse into the pulmonary artery, causing coronary steal and ischemia.): With an anomalous left coronary artery from the pulmonary artery, adult survivors have a well-developed right coronary system. Collaterals carry flow retrograde into the left system and then into the low-pressure pulmonary artery, which steals perfusion from the myocardium and causes ischemia and mitral regurgitation from papillary muscle dysfunction. Interarterial compression describes a different anomaly, and antegrade perfusion is not present.
  34. The correct answer is B (A target of 140 to 180 mg/dL, since tighter control raises hypoglycemia without benefit): Large trials such as NICE-SUGAR showed higher mortality with intensive control, and guidelines recommend 140 to 180 mg/dL in critically ill cardiac surgery patients. Tighter targets raise hypoglycemia risk without clear benefit. Sustained values above 180 mg/dL increase infection and sternal wound complications.
  35. The correct answer is A (Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate, because andexanet binds heparin and can cause heparin resistance during bypass.): Andexanet alfa traps factor Xa inhibitors but also binds heparin-antithrombin complexes, producing refractory heparin resistance that makes bypass anticoagulation difficult. For urgent cardiac surgery, prothrombin complex concentrate is preferred to restore thrombin generation, and heparin dosing is monitored with ACT. Idarucizumab reverses dabigatran only and has no effect on factor Xa inhibitors.
  36. The correct answer is A (TEVAR covering the primary tear to re-expand the true lumen, reserving fenestration for persistent ischemia): Visceral malperfusion makes this a complicated dissection that needs intervention. Dynamic obstruction from a collapsed true lumen is best treated by covering the primary entry tear with a stent graft, which depressurizes the false lumen and re-expands the true lumen. Open or endovascular fenestration is reserved for persistent branch obstruction after TEVAR. Medical therapy alone allows bowel ischemia to progress, and fenestration first is more invasive than necessary when a proximal entry tear is accessible.
  37. The correct answer is B (No air in the ventricular apex or pulmonary vein ostia, and adequate biventricular contraction.): Residual intracardiac air collects at the apex, the right superior pulmonary vein and right coronary cusp and can embolize to the coronaries and brain, so de-airing is verified by TEE. Biventricular function, especially the right ventricle, guides inotrope and wean decisions. Diastolic inflow patterns and baseline effusion or appendage findings do not decide readiness.
  38. The correct answer is C (Add a right internal jugular return cannula, converting to a veno-arterio-venous configuration): The recovering left ventricle ejects poorly oxygenated pulmonary venous blood to the upper body, which causes differential hypoxemia. Sending part of the oxygenated return into the right atrium through a jugular cannula improves the oxygen content of what the lungs and left ventricle deliver. The oxygenator is already at maximum FiO2, and reducing flow gives a larger share of the circulation to the injured lungs.
  39. The correct answer is C (Unfractionated heparin titrated to the ACT, because it is rapidly and reliably reversible with protamine): Unfractionated heparin allows ACT-guided titration and rapid reversal with protamine, which suits procedures with bleeding risk at the access site. Direct oral anticoagulants are contraindicated in mechanical valves because of excess thromboembolism. Low molecular weight heparin is only partly reversed by protamine and is difficult to monitor acutely.
  40. The correct answer is A (Continue high-intensity statin therapy through the perioperative period, including the morning of surgery and early recovery.): Statin withdrawal after an acute coronary syndrome is associated with more ischemic events, and statins have pleiotropic anti-inflammatory and plaque-stabilizing effects. Guidelines recommend continuing statins perioperatively in patients having cardiac surgery. The incidence of myopathy or hepatic injury from a short perioperative course is very low. Ezetimibe lowers cholesterol modestly and lacks these protective acute effects.
  41. The correct answer is A (Give additional heparin and recheck, since bypass requires an ACT above 400 to 480 seconds): The usual minimum ACT to initiate full bypass is above 400 seconds, and many centers use 480 seconds. A value of 340 seconds means anticoagulation is inadequate, so more heparin is needed. Heparin resistance is diagnosed only after a larger total dose, such as 500 units/kg, fails to reach the target, and plasma is not the first step.
  42. The correct answer is C (Arm vasodilation and shunting lower radial pressure relative to central pressure, narrowing over an hour.): After bypass, radial mean pressure often underestimates aortic or femoral pressure by 10 to 30 mmHg due to peripheral vasodilation and shunting, and the gap closes over roughly an hour as vascular tone recovers. The converging lines in the chart argue against a fixed anatomic stenosis, and a damping artifact would not narrow in this way.
  43. The correct answer is C (Cerebral hypoperfusion during arrest or low-flow perfusion, affecting the watershed zones most vulnerable to reduced pressure): Bilateral watershed infarcts reflect global hypoperfusion rather than a single occluded vessel, since border zones lie at the far end of two arterial supplies. Embolic stroke from atheroma usually produces territory-limited cortical infarcts. Air emboli produce scattered small lesions along the superior surface, and are managed with prompt hyperbaric consideration.
  44. The correct answer is A (Early surgery this admission lowers the composite of death and embolic events versus conventional therapy): A randomized trial of patients with severe left-sided valve disease and vegetations over 10 mm found that surgery within 48 hours reduced the composite of in-hospital death and embolic events compared with conventional management. The benefit came mainly from fewer embolic events. Guidelines support early surgery during the same hospitalization for large vegetations with embolic events despite appropriate antibiotics. Waiting for heart failure or vegetation shrinkage keeps the patient exposed to the highest embolic risk.
  45. The correct answer is A (Excess bleeding from residual platelet inhibition, so give standard heparin and protamine and keep platelets ready): A PRU of 58 shows strong residual P2Y12 inhibition, which raises the risk of surgical bleeding. Heparin dosing is unchanged because platelet inhibitors do not alter heparin response. Protamine does not neutralize a P2Y12 antagonist, and excess protamine itself worsens coagulopathy.
  46. The correct answer is B (Disable tachyarrhythmia detection with a programmer or magnet before incision, and keep external defibrillation pads applied.): Electrocautery near the generator causes electromagnetic interference that the ICD can read as ventricular fibrillation, producing inappropriate shocks. Suspending tachyarrhythmia therapy with a magnet or programmer prevents this, and external pads with a defibrillator provide rescue while therapies are off. Short cautery bursts reduce but do not eliminate oversensing. Asynchronous pacing is reserved for pacemaker dependent patients and does nothing to stop shocks while therapies remain enabled.
  47. The correct answer is B (Restrict free water and optimize decongestion, correcting no faster than 8 mmol/L in 24 hours): Hypervolemic hyponatremia in heart failure reflects excess water retention, so water restriction and improved cardiac output are the foundation. Correction beyond 8 mmol/L in a day risks osmotic demyelination. Saline loading worsens congestion in a patient with edema.
  48. The correct answer is A (Calcification restricts the annulus and leaflet bases while tips stay mobile, with no commissural fusion.): Mitral annular calcification causes stenosis by stiffening the annulus and base of the leaflets and reducing annular dilation in diastole, while the leaflet tips stay thin and mobile. Rheumatic disease fuses the commissures, thickens the tips and produces the classic hockey-stick or doming motion of the anterior leaflet and a fish-mouth orifice. Findings of tip thickening with commissural calcification describe rheumatic involvement.
  49. The correct answer is B (Serratus anterior plane or intercostal blocks with local infiltration along the tunnel, plus light monitored sedation.): The intermuscular pocket lies between the serratus anterior and latissimus dorsi, supplied by lateral cutaneous branches of roughly T3 to T9 intercostal nerves, so a fascial plane or intercostal block with local infiltration covers it. A thoracic epidural adds sympathectomy, hypotension and neuraxial risk that the short, superficial case does not justify. A paravertebral block at T10 sits too low to cover the lateral chest wall and sternal tunnel.
  50. The correct answer is A (Monitored sedation with ultrasound-guided femoral venous access, with echocardiography or fluoroscopy ready to identify pericardial effusion): The leadless pacemaker is delivered through a large sheath in the femoral vein, and monitored sedation is often adequate in a cooperative patient, which avoids airway and ventilatory risk in severe COPD. Ultrasound-guided access reduces bleeding risk during anticoagulation, and imaging is needed to detect perforation. Delivery from the neck or axillary route is not used for this device.
  51. The correct answer is B (Name the behavior calmly, redirect the team, check on the trainee afterward, and report it): An upstander acts in real time in a way that is brief and safe, such as stating that the comment is inappropriate and refocusing the team, then supports the affected person and reports the event. Silence leaves the trainee unsupported, and a delayed private comment loses the chance to counter the behavior. Pausing the operation for an extended confrontation endangers the patient.
  52. The correct answer is B (Slow the infusion, give phenylephrine, and prepare epicardial pacing at about 80 per minute): Intravenous amiodarone slows sinus and atrioventricular nodal function and causes vasodilation, so bradycardia with hypotension is a known infusion complication. Slowing the infusion, supporting pressure with a vasopressor and pacing through the epicardial wires address both problems. Diltiazem would add further negative chronotropy and vasodilation. Magnesium has no meaningful chronotropic effect.
  53. The correct answer is C (Ask the surgeon to close the entry site while the perfusionist briefly lowers pump flow.): Air entering through the open atrium or around a caval cannula is drawn into the venous line, where it breaks the siphon and can pass to the reservoir. The source must be sealed, and lowering flow protects the patient while the line is cleared. More vacuum draws in additional air, and retrograde cerebral perfusion is unrelated to venous entrainment.
  54. The correct answer is C (Elevated pulmonary vascular resistance that prevents passive pulmonary flow, which an assist device cannot overcome.): A systemic ventricular assist device unloads the single ventricle but cannot generate flow through a passive pulmonary circuit. If pulmonary resistance is high, venous pressure stays elevated and flow stays limited, so support fails. Repairable valve disease is not a barrier, and a low filling pressure is favorable. Resistance is the decisive limitation.
  55. The correct answer is A (Give calcium chloride 1 g centrally, since low ionized calcium impairs contractility and vascular tone.): Hemodilution, citrate in transfused blood and binding to albumin lower ionized calcium, and a value near 0.7 mmol/L depresses contractility and vascular tone at separation. Calcium chloride corrects it and often improves pressure. Magnesium does not correct ionized calcium, and calcium is appropriate once the clamp is off. A low ionized calcium is not a normal recovery finding.
  56. The correct answer is B (The effective orifice area is too small for body size, leaving a persistently high gradient.): Patient-prosthesis mismatch means a normally functioning prosthesis has an effective orifice area that is too small for the patient's body size. An indexed area of 0.66 to 0.85 cm2/m2 is moderate and 0.65 cm2/m2 or less is severe, so the value of 0.55 here is severe. The result is a fixed residual gradient, poorer regression of left ventricular hypertrophy and worse long-term outcomes. Leaflet motion is normal, which excludes thrombosis, and pannus develops over months to years rather than appearing on the early postoperative study.
  57. The correct answer is C (Isolated tricuspid valve surgery is reasonable before irreversible right ventricular dysfunction and hepatic fibrosis develop): Guidelines consider isolated tricuspid surgery reasonable for symptomatic severe regurgitation with right-sided heart failure despite diuretics, because late referral after right ventricular failure and liver damage carries high mortality. Waiting for marked dysfunction worsens outcomes. Medical therapy does not correct the volume overload from valve incompetence.
  58. The correct answer is B (A transvenous dual-chamber ICD, because the bradycardia needs permanent pacing that an S-ICD cannot provide.): The tracing shows complete AV dissociation with a slow ventricular escape, an indication for permanent pacing. An S-ICD offers only brief transthoracic backup pacing after a shock and no chronic pacing. A wearable defibrillator also offers no pacing and leaves symptomatic bradycardia untreated.
  59. The correct answer is B (Low-dose dexmedetomidine with analgesia, aiming for a calm, cooperative patient who breathes spontaneously and joins therapy.): Awake ECMO aims to preserve muscle strength, spontaneous breathing, and mobility, so sedation is minimized and analgesia is prioritized. Dexmedetomidine provides anxiolysis with little respiratory depression. Deep sedation and paralysis defeat the purpose, and benzodiazepine boluses cause delirium and deconditioning.
  60. The correct answer is C (Bivalirudin for bypass anticoagulation, accepting no reversal agent and the risk of clotting in stagnant blood): When surgery cannot wait and the patient has active antibodies, a direct thrombin inhibitor is the best studied option, and bivalirudin is most used in this setting. Its drawbacks include absence of a reversal agent, dependence on renal clearance and the need to avoid blood stasis. Plasmapheresis requires delay and adjunct protocols with cangrelor or iloprost are limited to experienced centers.
  61. The correct answer is B (Keep sinus pressure under 40 mmHg and expect poor right ventricular and posterior septal protection): Coronary sinus pressures above about 40 to 50 mmHg risk rupture of the sinus and hemorrhagic edema. Retrograde delivery perfuses the left ventricle well but often fails to reach the right ventricle and posterior septum, because the right ventricular veins drain directly into the right atrium and the catheter balloon may sit past tributaries. Supplemental antegrade or direct ostial doses address those areas.
  62. The correct answer is B (Postpericardiotomy syndrome, an immune-mediated pericardial and pleural inflammation): Fever, pleuritic pain, a rub and a modest effusion weeks after surgery with negative cultures fit postpericardiotomy syndrome, treated with anti-inflammatory therapy. Mediastinitis would show wound instability, drainage and positive cultures. A rub with effusion is not a feature of pulmonary embolism.
  63. The correct answer is C (Prep and drape first, induce with small ketamine or etomidate doses, and keep spontaneous ventilation): Tamponade depends on a high heart rate, preload and vascular tone, which induction agents and positive pressure ventilation remove. The patient is prepped and draped so the surgeon can open the chest immediately, and hemodynamically gentle agents are used. Needle drainage of hemopericardium from dissection may precipitate rebleeding and worsen aortic extension.
  64. The correct answer is C (Preoperative erythropoietin and iron, tranexamic acid, continuous cell salvage and small-volume blood sampling): A blood conservation plan boosts red cell mass before surgery, reduces losses and recycles shed blood within a continuous circuit. Transfusing against his stated refusal violates autonomy. Blood stored apart from the circuit is not continuous, so it is generally unacceptable.
  65. The correct answer is B (Hemodynamically significant stenosis, since exercise gradient over 15 mmHg and pressure over 60 mmHg favor intervention): Exercise raises heart rate and shortens diastole, so the gradient of a stenotic valve rises, and values over 15 mmHg with a pulmonary pressure over 60 mmHg indicate significant obstruction. These values correlate with symptoms and support intervention when symptoms are disproportionate. A modest rise is expected, but this magnitude is abnormal.
  66. The correct answer is C (Stroke volume index below 35 mL/m2 despite preserved ejection fraction, from a small restrictive ventricular cavity): Paradoxical low-flow, low-gradient stenosis combines a valve area at or below 1.0 cm2, mean gradient under 40 mmHg and preserved ejection fraction, with a stroke volume index below 35 mL/m2 as the confirmatory low-flow criterion.
