The small hypertrophied cavity limits filling and output. A high stroke volume or reduced ejection fraction describes different states.
  67. The correct answer is B (Qualitative platelet dysfunction after bypass; transfuse one apheresis platelet unit now): Bypass activates and damages platelets, causing qualitative dysfunction, and a low maximum amplitude with normal fibrinogen indicates a platelet defect. Platelet transfusion treats it. A normal ACT argues against residual heparin, and a fibrinogen of 320 mg/dL does not require cryoprecipitate.
  68. The correct answer is C (Withhold alteplase because surgery within 14 days carries prohibitive bleeding risk, and consider thrombectomy): Major surgery within the previous 14 days is a contraindication to intravenous thrombolysis because bleeding at the sternotomy and mediastinum is hard to control. Mechanical thrombectomy for a large vessel occlusion avoids that risk. The ACT does not reflect surgical wound bleeding risk, and reduced dosing has no supporting evidence in this setting.
  69. The correct answer is B (Without delay, since hemorrhage is absent, neurologic injury is mild, and heart failure demands valve surgery): Guidelines support proceeding with early surgery after an embolic stroke when there is no intracranial hemorrhage and the neurologic injury is not severe, particularly when heart failure or uncontrolled infection is the indication. A mandatory four-week wait applies to large or hemorrhagic infarcts with major deficits, not this case. Waiting for infarct resolution risks further emboli and decompensation.
  70. The correct answer is B (A mitral valve area of 4.5 cm2 with a mean diastolic gradient of 2 mmHg): The clip creates a double orifice and lowers the valve area, so a baseline area above 4 cm2 and a gradient below 5 mmHg leave room to avoid iatrogenic stenosis. A smaller area with a significant gradient predicts a stenotic result. A wide flail gap, heavy calcification, and a short posterior leaflet all reduce the chance of adequate grasping.
  71. The correct answer is A (Use a qualified interpreter, involve social work, simplify the regimen, and arrange medication access and follow-up): Missed visits and inconsistent medication use in a patient with housing instability and a language barrier reflect structural obstacles. A qualified interpreter, social work, case management and a simplified regimen address them. Indefinite delay of surgery for severe symptomatic stenosis risks harm, and ad hoc family interpreting and English-only instructions compromise accuracy and understanding.
  72. The correct answer is B (Mucosal laceration of the hypopharynx or gastroesophageal junction from probe trauma, unmasked by heparin and coagulopathy): Upper gastrointestinal bleeding after TEE is usually from mucosal trauma caused by difficult insertion or prolonged manipulation, and anticoagulation and bypass-related platelet dysfunction allow it to declare itself. An aortoesophageal fistula is a late complication that does not appear on the first postoperative day. Mesenteric ischemia typically presents with abdominal pain, acidosis and lactate elevation rather than isolated dark gastric drainage with stable vital signs.
  73. The correct answer is B (Loss of glossopharyngeal nerve afferents from the carotid sinus, removing buffering of acute pressure changes.): Carotid sinus baroreceptors send afferent signals through the glossopharyngeal nerve to the nucleus tractus solitarius, whereas aortic arch afferents travel in the vagus. Bilateral loss of the carotid input removes buffering, producing labile hypertension and hypotension. Attributing the carotid input to the vagus misidentifies the pathway. Efferent sympathetic injury would not cause large pressure surges.
  74. The correct answer is A (Between pectoralis major and the intercostal muscles, at two levels lateral to the sternal border): The parasternal intercostal plane block places local anesthetic in the fascial plane between pectoralis major and the external intercostal muscle, usually at two levels, to reach the anterior cutaneous branches. The plane deep to the internal intercostal muscle and superficial to transversus thoracis is the transversus thoracis plane block. An erector spinae injection reaches posterior structures and is not the parasternal technique.
  75. The correct answer is A (High Fontan pressure causes protein loss and hepatic congestion, so expect bleeding and altered drug binding.): The Fontan pressure is elevated and the transpulmonary gradient is small, so the problem is high systemic venous pressure rather than pulmonary vascular disease. That pressure drives enteric protein loss and hepatic congestion with reduced synthesis of clotting factors, explaining low albumin and a prolonged INR. Hypoalbuminemia alters drug binding. Saturation is near normal, so hyperviscosity is not the cause.
  76. The correct answer is C (About 270 mL/min/m2, at the commonly cited critical delivery threshold): Arterial oxygen content is 1.34 x 8.0 x 1.0 plus 0.003 x 200, which is 10.7 plus 0.6, or about 11.3 mL/dL. Multiplying by cardiac index of 2.4 L/min/m2 and by 10 to convert dL to L gives about 271 mL/min/m2. This sits at the commonly cited critical threshold of about 272 mL/min/m2 for avoiding kidney injury. The other values do not follow from these inputs.
  77. The correct answer is C (Just distal to the left subclavian artery at the ligamentum arteriosum, from deceleration shear): The aortic isthmus lies between the mobile arch and the descending aorta fixed by the ligamentum arteriosum, so rapid deceleration concentrates shear there. About 90 percent of survivors of blunt aortic injury have a tear at this site. Ascending and diaphragmatic injuries occur far less often, and the ascending type is usually fatal at the scene. Management involves impulse control and usually TEVAR, timed after control of other life threatening injuries.
  78. The correct answer is A (Microvascular obstruction from distal embolization and edema, treated with intracoronary adenosine, nicardipine, or nitroprusside.): No reflow is a failure of myocardial perfusion despite an open epicardial artery, caused by distal embolization, microvascular spasm and edema. Intracoronary vasodilators such as adenosine, nicardipine or nitroprusside can restore flow. Underexpansion or dissection would be visible on angiography, and beta blockade does not treat microvascular dysfunction.
  79. The correct answer is A (Desmopressin 0.3 mcg/kg, vitamin K 10 mg slowly, and calcium chloride 500 to 1,000 mg.): Desmopressin is dosed at 0.3 mcg/kg infused over 15 to 20 minutes, vitamin K at 10 mg given slowly intravenously to limit anaphylactoid reactions, and calcium chloride at 500 to 1,000 mg for an adult. A tenfold higher desmopressin dose adds hyponatremia and thrombosis risk without benefit. A rapid push of vitamin K or 5 g of calcium chloride is unsafe.
  80. The correct answer is B (On the x-ray tube side of the table, particularly at low height near the patient's torso.): Most scatter arises where the beam enters the patient, so exposure is highest on the tube side and at low height close to the torso. The detector side is much lower because the patient and detector attenuate the beam. The primary beam is blocked by the detector, so the head of the table behind the housing receives little dose.
  81. The correct answer is B (Urgent echocardiography, since consistent capture excludes pacing failure and tamponade must be ruled out): Each pacing spike on the tracing is followed by a ventricular complex, so capture is intact and failure to capture does not explain the hypotension. With rising central venous pressure, tamponade is the leading concern and needs echocardiography. Sedation wean should raise, not lower, pressure, and fluid alone delays diagnosis. Raising output addresses a problem the tracing does not show.
  82. The correct answer is C (Extensive dehiscence involving a large portion of the sewing ring circumference, requiring consideration of reoperation): A prosthesis that rocks with each cardiac cycle indicates detachment from the annulus over a large part of its circumference, with an eccentric paravalvular jet and hemolysis, and usually needs reoperation or transcatheter closure. Normal washing jets are small and central, with a stable valve. Leaflet thrombosis restricts leaflet motion and raises the gradient, but here leaflets move normally.
  83. The correct answer is B (Pancuronium, because its vagolytic effect raises heart rate and shortens diastolic filling across the stenotic valve): Tachycardia shortens diastole and raises the transmitral gradient and left atrial pressure, so pancuronium's vagolytic and sympathomimetic effects are undesirable in stenosis. Vecuronium is hemodynamically neutral and does not cause vagal bradycardia. Cisatracurium releases minimal histamine and is a suitable choice.
  84. The correct answer is B (Osmotic demyelination from correcting sodium faster than about 8 mEq/L over 24 hours): Chronic hyponatremia lets brain cells adapt by losing solutes, so fast correction by hypertonic fluids, dialysis, or bypass prime shifts can cause osmotic demyelination. Correction should stay near 8 mEq/L per 24 hours, and elective surgery is usually postponed until sodium is higher. Hepatic encephalopathy and Wernicke encephalopathy are unrelated to this mechanism.
  85. The correct answer is A (Hypereosinophilic syndrome with eosinophil-mediated endocardial damage leading to thrombus and fibrosis): Eosinophil granule proteins injure the endocardium, leading to thrombus and fibrosis that stiffen the ventricles, the mechanism of Loeffler endocarditis. Amyloidosis and hemochromatosis are other causes of restriction but are not linked to marked eosinophilia.
  86. The correct answer is B (Approximately 62 mmHg, adding estimated right atrial pressure to the simplified Bernoulli gradient): The simplified Bernoulli gradient is 4 x 3.6 squared, which is 4 x 12.96, or about 52 mmHg. Adding the estimated right atrial pressure of 10 mmHg gives about 62 mmHg for pulmonary artery systolic pressure. Omitting right atrial pressure underestimates the value, and the factor of 2 is not part of the equation. Severe tricuspid regurgitation with equalized pressures can also lower the jet velocity and underestimate true pressure.
  87. The correct answer is B (Venodilation lowers preload and wall tension, and epicardial and collateral vessel dilation increases subendocardial flow.): At low to moderate doses nitroglycerin is predominantly a venodilator, which reduces preload, ventricular wall tension and oxygen demand. It also dilates large epicardial vessels and collaterals, improving subendocardial perfusion. Coronary steal is a feature of arteriolar vasodilators such as dipyridamole and adenosine, not nitroglycerin. Nitroglycerin has no direct inotropic action.
  88. The correct answer is A (A prolonged TEG kaolin R time that corrects on the heparinase cup): Heparinase degrades heparin in the test cup, so a prolonged kaolin R time that normalizes with heparinase isolates heparin as the cause. A prolonged prothrombin time with low fibrinogen suggests factor depletion and dilution. Reduced maximum amplitude on both cups indicates a platelet or fibrinogen problem that heparinase would not correct.
  89. The correct answer is A (A dilated hypokinetic right ventricle, since the anterior right ventricle lies directly behind the sternum.): The thin right ventricle sits against the sternum and absorbs the force of blunt chest impact, so contusion typically produces right ventricular dilation and free wall hypokinesis, with conduction abnormalities such as right bundle branch block. Valve rupture occurs but is much less frequent. Septal thickening is not a traumatic finding.
  90. The correct answer is B (Prime hemodilution lowered plasma protein and oncotic pressure, favoring interstitial filtration with inflammatory capillary leak.): Crystalloid priming and the volume given on bypass dilute albumin and other proteins, so oncotic pressure falls and Starling forces move fluid out of capillaries. The inflammatory response to the circuit increases capillary permeability and amplifies the loss. Added albumin would raise oncotic pressure, which the measurement contradicts, and the measured fall in oncotic pressure is the primary finding rather than a hydrostatic change.
  91. The correct answer is A (Surgical ablation restores sinus rhythm in many patients, and appendage exclusion lowers thromboembolism risk.): Concomitant surgical ablation during mitral surgery is reasonable in patients with atrial fibrillation and restores sinus rhythm in a substantial proportion. The left atrial appendage is the source of most atrial thrombi, so exclusion reduces the risk of stroke. Anticoagulation is still needed early after valve surgery and while atrial rhythm recovers.
  92. The correct answer is A (Aortic velocity time integral of 10 cm or more with lateral annular S' above 6 cm/s): Weaning criteria include an outflow tract velocity time integral of 10 cm or more and a lateral annular tissue velocity above 6 cm/s at low flow, showing the left ventricle can generate its own output. A dilating right ventricle with septal flattening signals that the right ventricle will fail once flow is removed. Pressure maintained only by escalating inotropes is not true readiness.
  93. The correct answer is C (Severe stenosis, with an estimated right ventricular systolic pressure near 100 mmHg.): The peak gradient is 4 x 4.3 squared, which is about 74 mmHg, and a peak velocity above 4 m/s indicates severe stenosis. Right ventricular systolic pressure is the gradient plus the pulmonary artery pressure, so 74 plus 25 gives about 99 mmHg. Using the gradient alone omits the downstream pressure.
  94. The correct answer is C (Antagonism of inhibitory glycine and GABA A receptors; give a benzodiazepine and lower later dosing): High dose tranexamic acid crosses into the brain and blocks inhibitory glycine and GABA A receptors, producing cortical disinhibition and seizures. Acute treatment is a benzodiazepine, with dose reduction for any remaining use, especially in renal impairment. A thrombotic mechanism is not supported, and heparin would add bleeding risk. NMDA blockade would not produce excitation, and continuing the same infusion would perpetuate the toxicity.
  95. The correct answer is A (Myocardial supply and demand mismatch from hypotension, anemia and tachycardia, without acute coronary plaque rupture.): Type 2 myocardial injury after noncardiac surgery typically results from sustained hypotension, anemia or tachycardia in a patient with fixed coronary stenoses. Plaque rupture causes type 1 infarction but is less common and usually produces symptoms or ECG change. Volatile agents do not cause necrosis at clinical concentrations.
  96. The correct answer is C (Verapamil, which binds amyloid fibrils and causes profound negative inotropy and worsening conduction block.): Non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers bind amyloid deposits and cause marked negative inotropy and conduction depression, so they are avoided, as is digoxin. Phenylephrine is actually a favored vasopressor because stroke volume is fixed and cardiac output depends on heart rate and vascular tone. A single induction dose of etomidate has no clinically important effect on vasopressor responsiveness.
  97. The correct answer is B (An ACE inhibitor and a beta blocker, with a loop diuretic to relieve congestion): Peripartum cardiomyopathy with severe systolic dysfunction in a woman who is not breastfeeding is treated with standard heart failure therapy, so an ACE inhibitor, beta blocker and diuretic are all appropriate. Diltiazem is a negative inotrope that worsens outcomes in reduced ejection fraction. Empiric steroids have no proven benefit without a tissue diagnosis of an inflammatory process.
  98. The correct answer is B (Phenylephrine, a pure alpha agonist that raises afterload without increasing contractility or heart rate.): In dynamic outflow obstruction, hypotension and tachycardia worsen the gradient, so the treatment is a pure vasoconstrictor that raises afterload and fills the outflow tract without stimulating contractility. Phenylephrine fits, with volume and avoidance of inotropes. Epinephrine and dopamine increase contractility and rate, which deepen the obstruction and the systolic anterior motion.
  99. The correct answer is C (The tip lies too proximal and obstructs the left subclavian origin, so withdraw about 2 cm): The ideal tip position is in the descending thoracic aorta 1 to 2 cm distal to the origin of the left subclavian artery. A tip at or above that point compromises left arm flow and causes a damped left-sided pressure trace. A low tip would not affect the left arm, and normal placement would not explain the unilateral finding.
  100. The correct answer is B (A coronary sinus pressure of 30 to 40 mmHg during the infusion): A coronary sinus pressure of 30 to 40 mmHg delivers solution in a retrograde manner to the left ventricle, and pressures above about 50 mmHg risk coronary sinus rupture. Very low pressures suggest the balloon has slipped out or the sinus tributaries are leaking, giving poor delivery. The right ventricle is incompletely protected by retrograde delivery regardless of pressure, so higher pressures do not solve that.
  101. The correct answer is C (A single 20 mL/kg dose containing lidocaine and magnesium can provide arrest for about 90 minutes): Del Nido solution is a low volume, calcium free, depolarizing solution containing lidocaine and magnesium, which limit sodium and calcium entry, along with potassium, mannitol and bicarbonate. It is mixed with blood at a ratio of about 1 to 4 and a single dose provides roughly 90 minutes of arrest. Frequent repeat dosing and a four hour arrest do not describe this solution.
  102. The correct answer is A (Place a percutaneous ventricular assist device before induction, so mapping can proceed during hemodynamically poor tachycardia.): In a patient with severe ventricular dysfunction and prior hemodynamic intolerance, prophylactic percutaneous support such as an Impella allows prolonged mapping of tachycardia. Waiting for arrest exposes the patient to end-organ injury, and an intra-aortic balloon pump relies on ECG triggering and a pulsatile rhythm, so it performs poorly in fast ventricular tachycardia.
  103. The correct answer is C (Peripheral venoarterial extracorporeal membrane oxygenation with a plan for left ventricular unloading): A mechanical aortic valve is a contraindication to crossing the valve with a transvalvular pump. Venoarterial support gives full circulatory support and can be paired with a vent. Balloon pumping adds only modest flow and is unlikely to rescue this degree of shock.
  104. The correct answer is A (Alpha stat, because it preserves cerebral autoregulation and enzyme function in adults at moderate hypothermia): Alpha stat leaves the uncorrected values measured at 37 C, which keeps the imidazole ionization of proteins constant and preserves autoregulation and enzyme function. Adult cardiac surgery data support it for moderate hypothermia. pH stat adds carbon dioxide, increases cerebral blood flow, and uncouples flow from metabolism, which can increase embolic delivery to the brain.
  105. The correct answer is B (Mobile vegetations over 10 mm on anterior mitral leaflet carry high embolic risk, supporting early surgery.): Large mobile vegetations, particularly more than 10 mm and especially on the mitral valve with Staphylococcus aureus, have a high rate of embolization, and the risk is greatest in the first weeks of therapy. Guidelines support early surgery to prevent embolic events in this setting. Waiting for 30 mm or for completion of antibiotics exposes the patient to avoidable stroke risk.
  106. The correct answer is A (Comparable vasodilation at far lower cost without methemoglobin, but it needs a nebulizer and affects platelets.): Inhaled epoprostenol is a prostacyclin analog with pulmonary vasodilating effects similar to nitric oxide in most series, at a fraction of the cost, and it does not produce methemoglobin or nitrogen dioxide. It requires a continuous nebulizer or pump and has platelet inhibitory properties. Hemoglobin inactivation and cylinder delivery describe nitric oxide.
  107. The correct answer is C (Confirm a pulse oximetry or arterial waveform deflection following each paced complex at the paced rate): A wide QRS after each spike shows electrical capture, but contraction and cardiac output can still be absent, so an independent perfusion signal is needed. A pulse oximeter plethysmograph or arterial line tracing at the paced rate provides that. Palpating the carotid is unreliable because pacing causes chest wall and neck muscle contraction that mimics a pulse. The monitor appearance alone cannot separate electrical from mechanical capture.
  108. The correct answer is A (Check cannula tip position and carotid flow, then raise flow and pressure while correcting hemoglobin): A unilateral fall of about 25 percent or more from baseline suggests regional hypoperfusion, such as cannula malposition affecting one carotid or an arch vessel. Checking cannula position, then raising flow, pressure, PaCO2 and hemoglobin as needed, restores oxygenation. Lowering flow worsens perfusion, and an extreme pressure without examining the cause is not a rescue plan.
  109. The correct answer is C (Approximately 0.8 cm2, consistent with severe aortic stenosis by area criteria): The outflow tract area is pi times the radius squared, or 3.14 times 1.0 squared, which equals 3.14 cm2. The area equals 3.14 times 20 divided by 80, which gives 0.785 cm2, about 0.8 cm2. Using the diameter instead of the radius gives 3.1 cm2, and inverting the ratio of integrals gives values that are far too large or too small.
  110. The correct answer is A (Parallel addition of sarcomeres lowers wall stress but impairs relaxation and raises subendocardial oxygen demand): Pressure overload drives parallel sarcomere replication and concentric hypertrophy, which normalizes wall stress by the law of Laplace. The thick muscle is stiff and relaxes slowly, so filling pressures rise, and the greater muscle mass and wall tension increase oxygen demand while compressed subendocardial vessels limit supply. Series addition describes volume overload, and perfusion in hypertrophy is worse, not better.
  111. The correct answer is B (None has shown better neurologic outcome in randomized trials, and hypothermia remains the main protective strategy): Randomized and observational data have not shown that barbiturates, corticosteroids or magnesium improve neurologic outcome after arch surgery with circulatory arrest. Hypothermia, with or without selective cerebral perfusion, is the only strategy with consistent supporting evidence. Thiopental also adds hypotension and delayed emergence without proven benefit, so it does not justify warmer arrest temperatures. Steroids carry hyperglycemia and infection concerns and are not standard therapy for stroke prevention in this setting.
  112. The correct answer is C (Aspirin 75 to 100 mg daily plus warfarin, INR 2.5, for three to six months.): For a bioprosthetic aortic valve in a low bleeding risk patient, guidelines support aspirin for lifelong use and a vitamin K antagonist with an INR of 2.5 for the first three to six months, when thrombotic risk from the sewing ring is highest. Dual antiplatelet therapy and intense lifelong anticoagulation increase bleeding without proven benefit.
  113. The correct answer is B (Maintain adequate preload, avoid hypoxia and hypercapnia, and support right ventricular contractility): A failing right ventricle needs adequate filling, low pulmonary resistance, and preserved contractility. Hypoxia, hypercapnia, and acidosis raise pulmonary vascular resistance and increase afterload. Aggressive diuresis lowers preload and a very slow rate reduces output in a ventricle with a fixed stroke volume.
  114. The correct answer is A (Circumflex disease threatens inferior wall and AV node supply, so ischemia and heart block are expected): In a left dominant circulation the circumflex artery gives rise to the posterior descending artery and usually supplies the AV node, so a circumflex lesion jeopardizes the inferior wall and conduction. The AV node follows the dominant artery rather than always the right coronary. The anterior descending artery does not supply the inferior wall in this anatomy, so circumflex grafting matters.
  115. The correct answer is B (Cerebral vasodilation raises blood flow and uncouples it from metabolism, improving cooling but increasing embolic delivery.): In pH-stat management, CO2 is added so that pH measured at the patient's temperature is held at 7.40, which leaves PaCO2 high and dilates cerebral vessels. The extra flow cools the brain more evenly but is no longer matched to metabolic demand and carries more emboli. CO2 reactivity persists in hypothermia, which is why pH-stat changes flow.
  116. The correct answer is B (Isoproterenol, which acts directly on beta receptors of the sinoatrial node without autonomic nerve input.): A transplanted heart has no vagal or sympathetic innervation, so drugs that act through the autonomic nerves, such as atropine, glycopyrrolate and reflex responses to phenylephrine, have little effect. Direct-acting beta agonists such as isoproterenol, epinephrine or dobutamine stimulate the sinoatrial node receptors and raise the heart rate. Anticholinergics lose effect because no vagal tone is present to block.
  117. The correct answer is C (A bioprosthetic valve, since age over 65 and high bleeding risk favor avoiding lifelong warfarin.): Guidelines favor a bioprosthesis in patients over 65 and those with a high bleeding risk or limited life expectancy because a mechanical valve requires lifelong warfarin. Although dialysis accelerates bioprosthetic calcification, the expected survival is short. Platelet dysfunction does not protect a mechanical valve from thrombosis, so aspirin alone is not adequate.
  118. The correct answer is B (About 10 mL/kg/min, roughly 700 mL/min, titrated to right radial pressure of 50 to 70 mmHg): Antegrade cerebral perfusion is usually run at about 10 mL/kg/min, adjusted to a right radial pressure of roughly 50 to 70 mmHg, which for 70 kg is about 700 mL/min. Flow of 2 mL/kg/min gives inadequate perfusion. Flows near 25 mL/kg/min risk cerebral edema and hyperperfusion injury, and also push blood out through the open arch.
  119. The correct answer is B (Increase pump flow to lower proximal pressure and raise distal mean pressure above 60 mmHg): Left heart bypass diverts oxygenated blood from the left atrium to the femoral artery, so more pump flow unloads the proximal aorta and raises distal perfusion pressure. Raising flow therefore treats both abnormal pressures, with distal mean pressure above about 60 mmHg a common goal. Reducing flow to 0.5 L/min worsens both problems. Nitroprusside lowers proximal pressure but also drops distal pressure further because distal flow depends on pump output, not on proximal pressure.
  120. The correct answer is A (Percutaneous Impella RP, which drains the inferior vena cava and delivers blood into the pulmonary artery.): The Impella RP is a percutaneous right-sided pump that moves blood from the venous system into the pulmonary artery, bypassing the failing right ventricle. A left-sided Impella does not address an isolated right failure. A balloon pump mainly assists the left ventricle and has little effect on right ventricular output.
  121. The correct answer is B (No a wave because of atrial fibrillation, with a prominent v wave and slow y descent.): In atrial fibrillation there is no organized atrial contraction, so the wedge tracing lacks an a wave. In mitral stenosis the slow emptying of the left atrium produces a delayed y descent and a modestly raised v wave. A tall a wave would require sinus rhythm, and a giant v wave in regurgitation would show a rapid y descent.
  122. The correct answer is C (Lower heart rate below 80 and systolic pressure to 100 to 120 mmHg with beta blockade.): Reducing shear stress on the injured aortic wall with heart rate and pressure control lowers the risk of rupture while repair is arranged. Right radial monitoring reflects pressure proximal to the injury. Hypertension raises wall tension, and excessive volume loading worsens right ventricular dysfunction from contusion.
  123. The correct answer is C (Suspect coronary embolization, go on bypass emergently, and replace the valve with grafting of the vessel.): A disappearing vegetation with a new regional wall motion abnormality indicates coronary embolization of infected material. The patient needs immediate bypass, valve replacement to remove the source, and bypass grafting or embolectomy. Fibrinolysis is contraindicated in endocarditis because of hemorrhagic and mycotic aneurysm risk, and delaying surgery for catheterization allows continued embolization.
  124. The correct answer is A (It reduced end systolic volume but did not improve survival or hospitalization compared with CABG alone.): The STICH surgical ventricular reconstruction hypothesis found that adding the procedure reduced left ventricular volume modestly but did not improve survival or reduce cardiac hospitalization compared with CABG alone. It therefore is not routinely recommended for patients with ischemic cardiomyopathy. The trial did not show a survival benefit, and ejection fraction rose only a few points.
  125. The correct answer is C (Loss of positive pressure raises preload and afterload, so diurese and unload before repeating the trial): Withdrawing positive pressure increases venous return and left ventricular afterload, so the failing ventricle develops pulmonary edema, shown by the doubled occlusion pressure. Optimizing diuresis and afterload reduction before another trial treats the cause. Myopathy and oversedation do not produce a rapid wedge pressure rise.
  126. The correct answer is C (Carcinoid heart disease, from serotonin-mediated plaque deposition on right-sided valve surfaces): Flushing, diarrhea and fixed, retracted right-sided leaflets are the signature of carcinoid syndrome, where plaque deposition on the tricuspid and pulmonic valves produces combined regurgitation and stenosis. Rheumatic disease almost always involves the mitral valve first. Ebstein anomaly shows apical displacement of the septal leaflet, which is not described.
  127. The correct answer is B (Venoarterial ECMO to support the failing right ventricle, followed by catheter-directed or surgical embolectomy.): Venoarterial ECMO bypasses the obstructed pulmonary circulation and supports systemic perfusion until the clot is removed mechanically. Recent neurosurgery is a major contraindication to lytics. Venovenous support does not provide circulatory assistance and leaves the failing right ventricle facing the obstruction.
  128. The correct answer is A (Hypothermia and hemodilution prolong clotting apart from heparin, and aprotinin further prolongs the celite value.): Cold temperature slows enzymatic clotting reactions and dilution lowers clotting factors and platelets, so the clotting time lengthens even without more heparin. Aprotinin binds kallikrein and prolongs the celite ACT specifically, so a kaolin-based test or a heparin concentration assay is preferred. Hypothermia prolongs, not shortens, clotting times, and hemodilution lowers antithrombin.
  129. The correct answer is A (Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate dosed by weight and INR, with a repeat INR afterward.): The prolonged INR with normal fibrinogen and acceptable platelets reflects deficiency of vitamin K dependent factors II, VII, IX and X, which prothrombin complex concentrate replaces quickly in a small volume. Platelets do not contain meaningful amounts of these factors, and a normal ACT argues against residual heparin, so more protamine adds risk without benefit.
  130. The correct answer is C (Abnormal ventricular activation causes secondary repolarization changes that mimic or mask ischemic ST deviation.): In paced or bundle branch block rhythms the QRS is wide and depolarization is abnormal, so repolarization is secondarily altered and ST segments no longer reflect only myocardial supply and demand. This makes ST trending nonspecific, and echocardiographic wall motion becomes the more useful ischemia monitor. Modern monitors filter pacing spikes, so the signal is not lost, and the rate limit of ST algorithms is not the issue.
  131. The correct answer is C (Limit cardiac manipulation before bypass because dislodged thrombus can embolize to the cerebral or systemic circulation.): Mural thrombus within a dyskinetic aneurysm can embolize when the heart is lifted, squeezed or transesophageal probe movement is vigorous, causing stroke or peripheral emboli. Careful handling and early institution of bypass minimize this. Inotropes do not shrink an aneurysm and raise wall stress. Chronic aneurysm wall is fibrous scar and rarely ruptures at induction.
  132. The correct answer is B (The inferior and posterior fossa ovalis, aligning the delivery sheath with the appendage axis.): The left atrial appendage points anteriorly and superiorly, so a low and posterior puncture gives the sheath a coaxial approach and room to deploy the device. A superior or anterior puncture makes the sheath point toward the roof and prevents coaxial delivery. Mid-fossa puncture suits pulmonary vein isolation but leaves too little height for appendage work.
  133. The correct answer is C (Flow drag and Venturi forces pull the anterior leaflet toward the septum in an underfilled heart): After relief of stenosis the hypertrophied septum is exposed to a high velocity outflow jet, and low pressure and drag forces draw the anterior mitral leaflet into the outflow tract. Catecholamines, hypovolemia, and low afterload all worsen it. Rupture and annular dilation produce different echo findings.
  134. The correct answer is B (Spinal cord ischemia risk persists, so maintain perfusion pressure and monitor for delayed paraplegia): The frozen elephant trunk covers a segment of descending aorta and its intercostal arteries, so collateral spinal cord supply is stressed after the procedure. Delayed paraplegia can appear hours to days later with hypotension or drain malfunction. Maintaining mean arterial pressure and watching neurologic function remains important during the interval. Risk does not end with arrest, and interstage rupture of the remaining aneurysm is a recognized cause of death that makes pressure control relevant throughout.
  135. The correct answer is A (Pulmonary artery pulsatility index near 1.1, below the threshold near 1.85 linked to postimplantation right failure.): The pulmonary artery pulsatility index equals pulmonary pulse pressure divided by central venous pressure, here (42 minus 22) divided by 18, or about 1.1. Values below roughly 1.85 indicate poor right ventricular reserve and predict failure. Pulmonary vascular resistance of 2.0 Wood units is normal to low, so it does not predict failure. A wedge pressure of 20 mmHg is elevated and does not show adequate decompression.
  136. The correct answer is B (Temperature 36 C, stable rhythm, inflated lungs, normal pH, potassium below 5.5 mEq/L, adequate hematocrit.): Weaning requires adequate rewarming, a stable rate and rhythm, ventilated lungs, corrected acid-base status and electrolytes, and sufficient hemoglobin for oxygen delivery. Cold patients and collapsed lungs impair separation, and acidosis with hyperkalemia raises arrhythmia risk. A paced rate of 40 beats/min, a potassium of 3.0 mEq/L and a hematocrit of 12 percent are each inadequate.
  137. The correct answer is A (Request real-time input from a colleague with advanced echocardiography expertise and discuss findings with the proceduralist): Recognizing the limit of one's expertise and seeking help promptly protects the patient. A colleague experienced in advanced echocardiography can help interpret the new finding while the team discusses next steps. Certification does not guarantee familiarity with every new device, and a vendor representative is not a clinician responsible for the interpretation.
  138. The correct answer is B (Substantial residual P2Y12 inhibition, so platelets should be available and surgical timing weighed): Platelet mapping isolates the ADP receptor pathway, where high percent inhibition with a low MA ADP shows ongoing P2Y12 blockade. At 78 percent inhibition, bleeding risk is considerable, so platelet transfusion after protamine should be anticipated and delay considered if the patient is stable. Aspirin is measured in the arachidonic acid channel, not the ADP channel.
  139. The correct answer is A (Systolic septal flattening, a dilated right ventricle, and tricuspid annular plane systolic excursion of 9 mm): Correcting regurgitation removes the low resistance escape for the right ventricle, which may unmask underlying dysfunction. Tricuspid annular plane systolic excursion under 17 mm, a dilated ventricle, and septal flattening indicate poor longitudinal function and pressure overload. A velocity above 12 cm/s and a fractional area change of 45 percent are normal values.
  140. The correct answer is C (Raise hematocrit above 21 to 24 percent, since lower values are linked to kidney injury): Observational data link nadir hematocrit below about 21 to 24 percent during bypass with acute kidney injury, stroke and death, and current guidance supports transfusion or avoiding hemodilution below that range. Moderate hypothermia lowers demand but does not make a value of 12 percent safe. A target above 35 percent is not needed and increases viscosity and transfusion exposure.
  141. The correct answer is A (It lowers ischemic stroke risk even when oral anticoagulation is continued): In a large randomized trial of patients with atrial fibrillation undergoing cardiac surgery, occlusion of the appendage added a further reduction in stroke or systemic embolism on top of continued anticoagulation. It does not replace anticoagulation, because stroke has other sources. Transesophageal echo confirms complete closure without a residual stump.
  142. The correct answer is B (Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction diverts blood from the collapsed lung toward ventilated alveoli.): Alveolar hypoxia in the collapsed lung triggers local pulmonary arteriolar constriction, which redirects blood flow to the ventilated lung and limits shunt to roughly 20 to 25 percent of cardiac output. Gas flow is not reversed by bronchoconstriction, and the main problem is blood flow, not ventilation. Nitric oxide is a vasodilator, so it would increase rather than decrease perfusion of the nonventilated lung.
  143. The correct answer is C (Low cardiac output syndrome, with index below 2.2 and hypoperfusion despite adequate preload): Low cardiac output syndrome is a cardiac index below 2.2 L/min/m2 with SvO2 under about 60%, rising lactate and oliguria despite adequate preload and inotropic support. Stunning is the usual cause but still requires treatment when perfusion is inadequate. Vasoplegia has a high cardiac index and a high SvO2, the opposite of this picture.
  144. The correct answer is C (About 693 dyn s cm-5, which is low, so start vasopressin 0.03 units/min.): Systemic vascular resistance equals (mean arterial pressure minus central venous pressure) divided by cardiac output times 80, so (60 minus 8) divided by 6.0 times 80 gives about 693 dyn s cm-5. This is below the normal 800 to 1,200 range with a preserved cardiac output and warm extremities, a vasoplegic picture best treated with a vasoconstrictor such as vasopressin. Omitting central venous pressure gives 800. Using cardiac index instead of output gives an inflated 1,387 and a wrong treatment direction.
  145. The correct answer is A (Perioperative myocardial injury, not infarction, because no supporting ischemic or angiographic evidence accompanies the elevation): Type 5 infarction after CABG requires troponin above 10 times the limit plus new Q waves, angiographic graft or native vessel occlusion, or imaging evidence of new lost myocardium. Without any of these, troponin release is expected myocardial injury from surgical handling and cardioplegia. Type 2 infarction needs clinical evidence of ischemia from supply demand mismatch, which is also absent.
  146. The correct answer is A (Titrate rate control with a beta blocker or amiodarone, avoid tachycardia, and maintain preload without hypercarbia.): Tachycardia shortens diastolic filling across a stenotic mitral valve, raising left atrial pressure and risking pulmonary edema, so rate control is the priority. Preload should be maintained but not expanded excessively, and hypercarbia or hypoxemia raise pulmonary vascular resistance and strain the right ventricle. Ketamine causes tachycardia and a rise in pulmonary pressure, which is undesirable here.
  147. The correct answer is B (pH stat management during cooling, with hematocrit maintained near 25 to 30 percent): Adding carbon dioxide with pH stat management during cooling causes cerebral vasodilation, which increases flow and cools the brain more evenly before arrest. Hematocrit near 25 to 30 percent balances oxygen capacity and viscosity at low temperature. Alpha stat does not give that vasodilation, and a hematocrit of 18 percent reduces oxygen carrying capacity too far. A value of 40 percent increases viscosity and microcirculatory resistance.
  148. The correct answer is A (A bioprosthesis avoids warfarin embryopathy but degenerates faster, so reoperation is probable): Mechanical valves are durable but require warfarin, which crosses the placenta and causes embryopathy and fetal loss, and alternative regimens bring valve thrombosis risk. Bioprostheses avoid long term anticoagulation, but young age and pregnancy are associated with faster structural deterioration and a likely second operation. The decision is made jointly after counseling about both risks.
  149. The correct answer is B (Impaired angiotensin II and adrenergic compensation lower vascular tone, so vasopressin should be available early): ACE inhibitors block angiotensin II formation and amiodarone blunts adrenergic responses, so vascular tone depends on vasopressin and is poorly restored after bypass. Early vasopressin is the targeted therapy. Bradykinin accumulation causes vasodilation and angioedema, not pulmonary edema. These drugs do not enhance nitric oxide synthase, and prophylactic norepinephrine does not address the deficit.
  150. The correct answer is C (Give four-factor PCC 25 units/kg, accepting thrombotic risk, since fibrinogen and platelets are adequate.): With an INR of 1.8 from warfarin effect and other causes corrected, four-factor PCC replaces vitamin K dependent factors quickly, and it is generally preferred to recombinant factor VIIa. Both drugs raise the risk of arterial and venous thrombosis, including graft occlusion and stroke, so adequate fibrinogen and platelets are needed first. Intravenous vitamin K works over hours, not minutes.
  151. The correct answer is C (Right ventricular puncture with hemopericardium that may require pericardial drainage and possible autotransfusion): The subxiphoid needle passes close to the anterior right ventricle, so inadvertent ventricular puncture with bleeding into the pericardium is a recognized complication that is specific to this access. Anesthesia should have blood available and be prepared for tamponade. Pulmonary vein stenosis and left atrial air embolism are complications of endocardial left atrial procedures and transseptal access, not of subxiphoid pericardial entry.
  152. The correct answer is B (Hemoglobin carries oxygen to the arrested heart between doses and adds buffering and antioxidant capacity.): Crystalloid carries only dissolved oxygen, which is limited especially when cold, while hemoglobin in blood cardioplegia delivers oxygen to the hypertrophied, vulnerable myocardium and buffers acid. Both solutions use similar potassium concentrations for arrest. Even with blood, the arrested heart keeps a small oxygen demand, so repeat doses are still given to wash out metabolites.
  153. The correct answer is C (Therapeutic anticoagulation for at least four weeks, then long-term therapy decided by stroke risk score): Atrial mechanical function remains depressed for weeks after cardioversion, a state called stunning, so thromboembolic risk persists even with a negative echocardiogram and anticoagulation must be continued for at least four weeks. Long-term therapy then depends on the CHA2DS2-VASc score, which here supports continued treatment. Aspirin is not an adequate substitute.
  154. The correct answer is C (Give a fluid bolus and phenylephrine, then confirm ventricular filling with echocardiography.): A low pulsatility index, falling flow, and hypotension after induction suggest reduced preload and vasodilation. Volume and a vasopressor restore filling and afterload, and echocardiography confirms the diagnosis. Raising speed in an underfilled ventricle causes suction, and vasodilation worsens the hypotension.
  155. The correct answer is A (Impaired angiotensin II vasoconstriction with relative vasopressin deficiency, treated with vasopressin and possibly angiotensin II.): Vasoplegia after bypass in a patient taking an angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor reflects loss of angiotensin II-mediated vasoconstriction and a relative deficiency of vasopressin. Vasopressin infusion is the first-line rescue, and angiotensin II or methylene blue can be added if needed. Protamine reactions are accompanied by raised pulmonary pressures and a low cardiac index, which are not present here.
  156. The correct answer is B (Expect sequestration of lipophilic drugs such as fentanyl and midazolam, and titrate to clinical effect): Lipophilic drugs bind to the plastic circuit and oxygenator, and ECMO also enlarges the volume of distribution, so higher doses are often needed. Titration to effect with EEG guidance is the practical approach. Cutting doses on the grounds of hemodilution runs against the sequestration data, and hydrophilic agents are the ones least sequestered, not most.
  157. The correct answer is C (Replace the ascending aorta during the valve operation, because its diameter already exceeds 4.5 cm.): Guidelines support concomitant ascending aortic replacement when a patient with a bicuspid valve undergoes valve surgery and the ascending aorta exceeds 4.5 cm. Standalone repair thresholds are 5.5 cm, or 5.0 cm with risk factors, but the lower threshold applies when the chest is already open. Waiting for 6.0 cm accepts an unacceptable dissection risk.
  158. The correct answer is C (Monitor impedance respiration sensing is read by the pacemaker as increased ventilation, raising paced rate): Minute ventilation sensors measure transthoracic impedance, so current injected by respiratory monitoring or ventilator-related impedance changes can be misread as exercise, increasing the paced rate. Blood pressure stays stable because the rise is a programmed sensor response, not a catecholamine surge. Retrograde conduction produces a different pattern, and light anesthesia would be expected to raise pressure as well.
  159. The correct answer is B (Use warm blood cardioplegia and keep temperature above the thermal amplitude, accepting less hypothermic protection.): Agglutination occurs when blood falls below the thermal amplitude, here 30 C, so any cold cardioplegia or cooling below it risks vessel occlusion and hemolysis. Warm blood cardioplegia keeps the blood above that threshold and accepts a weaker hypothermic effect. A warm flush helps wash out agglutinins but systemic cooling to 28 C remains below 30 C. Cold crystalloid cardioplegia is the worst option.
  160. The correct answer is B (Prophylactic recombinant factor VIIa before any bleeding, because of its arterial thrombosis signal.): Recombinant factor VIIa carries a documented increase in arterial thromboembolic events, which is dangerous with a recent stent and prior graft thrombosis, so it is reserved for refractory bleeding after conventional therapy. Lower-dose tranexamic acid and goal-directed product replacement are accepted, since they treat demonstrated deficits.
  161. The correct answer is C (Stop warfarin about 5 days before surgery without heparin bridging, confirming INR below 1.5 beforehand): A bileaflet mechanical aortic valve without atrial fibrillation, prior thromboembolism, or reduced ejection fraction has low thrombotic risk, so bridging is not needed during a short interruption. Warfarin is stopped about 5 days before surgery and the INR is checked to confirm it is below 1.5. Continuing warfarin or relying on vitamin K leaves a variable INR and bleeding risk.
  162. The correct answer is A (Overall incidence is 1 to 3 percent, highest with combined valve and CABG surgery or atheroma): Stroke occurs in about 1 to 3 percent of cardiac surgery patients, with higher rates after combined valve and CABG surgery and in patients with aortic atheroma, prior stroke or carotid disease. A rate of 10 to 15 percent is far too high. Off-pump surgery reduces aortic manipulation and does not carry the highest risk.
  163. The correct answer is A (Late inflation reduces diastolic augmentation and coronary perfusion; move inflation earlier, to the dicrotic notch.): The exhibit shows inflation starting well after the dicrotic notch, so the diastolic augmentation is smaller and later than it could be. Inflation should begin at the notch to maximize coronary perfusion. Early inflation would show augmentation before the notch, and late deflation would show a widened assisted systolic pressure.
  164. The correct answer is C (Increase pump flow and consider raising hematocrit, since oxygen delivery is lagging behind consumption.): A steady downward drift to the low 60s in a normothermic patient shows that extraction is rising because delivery, the product of flow and oxygen content, is not meeting demand. Higher flow or a higher hematocrit raises delivery. A threshold of 50 percent is too low for normothermia, and cutting flow would worsen the imbalance.
  165. The correct answer is B (It preserves antegrade flow to distal myocardium during the anastomosis and limits ischemia and instability.): An intracoronary shunt carries blood from the proximal vessel past the arteriotomy to the distal bed, so the territory keeps perfusion while the anastomosis is sewn. This reduces regional ischemia, arrhythmias and hemodynamic collapse during off-pump grafting. It does not create a bloodless field by occluding flow, and it does not route blood to the right atrium.
  166. The correct answer is C (Rapid outflow ejection produces Venturi and drag forces that pull the leaflet toward the septum.): In obstructive HCM the septal bulge narrows the outflow tract and shifts the mitral apparatus anteriorly, so high velocity ejection flow drags the leaflet toward the septum through Venturi and drag forces. Obstruction therefore worsens with low preload, low afterload and high contractility. Chordal rupture produces a flail leaflet with a different jet and no septal contact. Left atrial pressure acts in diastole, when the leaflet is open and no outflow gradient exists.
  167. The correct answer is B (Hyperfibrinolysis from tissue plasminogen activator release, treated with tranexamic acid.): An elevated LY30 means the clot is dissolving abnormally fast, as occurs with excess plasmin activity after prolonged bypass from tissue plasminogen activator release. Antifibrinolytics such as tranexamic acid or aminocaproic acid treat it. Residual heparin or platelet dysfunction does not elevate LY30.
  168. The correct answer is B (Preoperative erythropoietin with intravenous iron to raise hemoglobin, plus continuous-circuit cell salvage during surgery): Raising red cell mass beforehand with erythropoietin and iron builds reserve for operative loss, and cell salvage in a circuit continuous with the patient is generally acceptable. Stored autologous blood is usually refused because it leaves the body and is held outside circulation. Minimizing phlebotomy and tight hemostasis also help.
  169. The correct answer is A (Keep sedation light to preserve inducibility, avoid sympatholytic agents, and keep adenosine available): AVNRT relies on a reentrant circuit that can be suppressed by deep anesthesia and by drugs that slow nodal conduction, so light sedation keeps the arrhythmia inducible, and isoproterenol may be added by the electrophysiologist. Adenosine terminates a sustained episode. Dexmedetomidine depresses sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodal function and makes induction harder, and deep volatile anesthesia also suppresses inducibility.
  170. The correct answer is B (Abrupt hemodilution lowers viscosity and washes out catecholamines, causing transient vasodilation that resolves in minutes.): Hemodilution at the start of bypass abruptly lowers blood viscosity and circulating catecholamines, so systemic vascular resistance falls and MAP often dips even at full flow. A steady, unremarkable line pressure with achieved flow and no root change argues against cannula kinking and against dissection, which usually causes a rising line pressure, poor flow and a visible hematoma. The dip usually resolves within minutes, and a small vasopressor dose can bridge it.
  171. The correct answer is C (A target of 3.0, because atrial fibrillation is a thromboembolic risk factor excluding the reduced-intensity protocol): The reduced INR range of 1.5 to 2.0 with low-dose aspirin after the first three months applies only to On-X aortic recipients without thromboembolic risk factors. Atrial fibrillation, prior thromboembolism, left ventricular dysfunction or hypercoagulability call for an INR target of 3.0 with an aortic mechanical valve. Valve design does not offset atrial fibrillation risk.
  172. The correct answer is B (Transgastric midpapillary short axis, which displays regions supplied by all three major coronary arteries.): The transgastric midpapillary short axis view cuts the left ventricle in cross section, showing the anterior and septal walls (left anterior descending), the lateral and posterior walls (circumflex) and the inferior wall (right coronary), so one view reflects all three territories. The four chamber and two chamber views show only some walls.
  173. The correct answer is B (Dexamethasone 4 mg intravenously, which does not prolong the QT interval): A QTc above 500 ms with hypokalemia carries a high risk of torsades de pointes. Ondansetron and droperidol both prolong the QT interval, even at antiemetic doses, and droperidol carries a boxed warning. Dexamethasone has no repolarization effect, and potassium and magnesium should be replaced while methadone is avoided.
  174. The correct answer is C (Transfusion related acute lung injury, with noncardiogenic edema, low filling pressures and hypotension.): Hypoxemia with bilateral infiltrates within six hours of plasma, plus fever and hypotension, with normal filling pressures and a normal BNP and ejection fraction, indicates TRALI. Overload would show raised CVP and wedge pressure and a high BNP, with hypertension and response to diuresis. Stunned myocardium would show reduced ejection fraction and a raised wedge pressure.
  175. The correct answer is C (The unconditioned donor right ventricle faces acutely elevated resistance from the recipient's chronic pulmonary hypertension): A donor right ventricle has not adapted to high pulmonary resistance, so a recipient with pulmonary hypertension can overload it immediately after reperfusion. Hyperacute rejection would impair the left ventricle as well, and this left ventricle contracts well. Denervation lowers resting vagal influence and produces a fast resting rate, not bradycardia.
  176. The correct answer is B (Transfuse apheresis platelets and give desmopressin 0.3 mcg/kg, since clopidogrel's active metabolite clears within hours.): The platelet count is normal but function is blocked, since clopidogrel binds P2Y12 irreversibly on circulating platelets. Its active metabolite has a short half-life, so transfused platelets work. Desmopressin adds von Willebrand factor mediated adhesion. Fibrinogen is already adequate, and protamine does not reverse antiplatelet drugs and can itself impair platelet function.
  177. The correct answer is A (Increased permeability pulmonary edema with preserved preload, not simple volume overload from excess fluid): A raised extravascular lung water index shows pulmonary edema, but the global end-diastolic volume index is within the normal range, so cardiac filling is not excessive. This pattern points to increased capillary permeability, as in post-bypass inflammatory lung injury. Hydrostatic edema would show a raised global end-diastolic volume, and hypovolemia would show a low one.
  178. The correct answer is A (Place right and left radial lines, since right arm pressure can read falsely high.): With axillary inflow the right radial artery sits next to the cannula, so its pressure can exceed true systemic pressure and may also reflect cerebral perfusion pressure during unilateral perfusion. A second site, left radial or femoral, shows systemic pressure and detects right arm malperfusion. A single right line misleads on systemic pressure, while a single left line loses the cerebral perfusion reading.
  179. The correct answer is B (Platelet transfusion is limited by circulating ticagrelor that inhibits donor platelets, so use targeted coagulation therapy.): Ticagrelor binds the P2Y12 receptor reversibly, and its plasma half-life is about seven to nine hours, so substantial drug remains eighteen hours after the last dose. Transfused platelets are exposed to this drug and are partly inhibited, which blunts the benefit. Fibrinogen, prothrombin complex, desmopressin, and antifibrinolytics address other bleeding contributors. Protamine reverses heparin only and has no effect on P2Y12 inhibition.
  180. The correct answer is C (Profound hypothermia near 18 C with adequate cooling time, which markedly reduces cerebral metabolic rate.): Cooling lowers cerebral oxygen consumption by roughly 5 to 7 percent per degree and is the one intervention with consistent evidence in circulatory arrest. Barbiturates add no benefit once the EEG is already silent from cold, and trials of corticosteroids in cardiac surgery have not shown stroke reduction.
  181. The correct answer is B (Hemodilution and uptake into tubing and oxygenator lower total lipophilic drug levels, though free fractions rise.): Bypass adds a large crystalloid volume and a synthetic surface; lipophilic drugs such as fentanyl and propofol bind to the circuit, and total plasma concentration drops. Hemodilution also lowers binding protein concentration and can raise the free fraction. Cooling lowers hepatic clearance rather than raising it, and heparin does not cause fentanyl renal excretion.
  182. The correct answer is B (Isoproterenol infusion, a direct beta-agonist that acts without relying on neural input to the donor heart.): The transplanted heart has no vagal or sympathetic innervation, so atropine and indirect agents such as ephedrine have little or no effect. Direct-acting agents such as isoproterenol, epinephrine, or dobutamine work on receptors. Isoproterenol also lowers pulmonary vascular resistance, helping the donor right ventricle.
  183. The correct answer is A (Surgical vegetation debridement with valve repair, given the size over 2 cm and recurrent emboli): Tricuspid vegetations larger than 2 cm with recurrent pulmonary emboli despite therapy are an indication for surgery. Repair or vegetation removal is favored over a prosthesis in people who inject drugs to limit reinfection of foreign material. Continuing drugs alone ignores the recurrent embolic failure of therapy, and a mechanical valve adds anticoagulation burden in a high risk group.
  184. The correct answer is C (Preoperative education and prehabilitation, opioid-sparing multimodal analgesia, early extubation, and early mobilization with structured nutrition): Enhanced recovery combines patient preparation, multimodal opioid-sparing analgesia, early extubation, early mobilization and nutrition to speed recovery. Prolonged fasting, heavy opioid dosing and delayed mobilization run counter to the pathway. Prolonged ventilation and fixed fluid volumes are also opposed to individualized, goal-directed care.
  185. The correct answer is B (The gap partly reflects where patients receive care, a structural factor that referral changes can address): Race is a social category, and persistent outcome differences linked to treatment at low-volume centers point to structural drivers such as access, referral and hospital quality. These can be addressed with system-level action. Attributing the gap to biology is unsupported by the data, and a persistent gap after adjustment cannot by itself prove intraoperative bias because unmeasured care-setting factors remain.
  186. The correct answer is C (Keep superior vena cava pressure at or below about 25 mmHg while following cerebral oximetry trends.): Retrograde perfusion is limited to a superior vena cava pressure of about 25 mmHg to avoid cerebral edema, with flow commonly 200 to 400 mL/min. Cerebral oximetry offers a trend to judge adequacy. Pressures near 50 mmHg risk edema and hemorrhage, and pressures near 10 mmHg may not drive meaningful flow.
  187. The correct answer is A (12,000 to 16,000 units, or 150 to 200 units/kg, targeting an ACT of about 300 seconds): Off-pump surgery needs partial anticoagulation without a circuit, commonly 150 to 200 units/kg, which for 80 kg is 12,000 to 16,000 units, aiming for an ACT near 300 seconds. The full bypass dose of 300 to 400 units/kg gives 24,000 to 32,000 units, which is unnecessary here. A dose of 4,000 to 6,000 units is too low to protect grafts and target vessels.
  188. The correct answer is C (Cancel cardioversion, continue therapeutic anticoagulation for at least three weeks, and repeat TEE for thrombus resolution.): Thrombus in the appendage is a contraindication to cardioversion because restoration of atrial contraction can embolize it. Guidelines advise at least three weeks of therapeutic anticoagulation, then repeat imaging before attempting cardioversion. Amiodarone can also convert rhythm pharmacologically, so it carries the same embolic risk.
  189. The correct answer is B (Preserve sinus rhythm, keep a normal heart rate, and maintain right-sided preload with euvolemia.): Tricuspid stenosis restricts right ventricular filling, so cardiac output depends on adequate preload, adequate diastolic filling time and preserved atrial contraction. A normal heart rate allows time for filling, and loss of sinus rhythm or marked tachycardia raises the gradient and reduces output. Diuresis and junctional rhythm both lower preload or atrial kick, which compromises right-sided output.
  190. The correct answer is B (Inhaled epoprostenol or nitric oxide, lowering pulmonary vascular resistance without causing systemic vasodilation.): Inhaled pulmonary vasodilators act on ventilated alveoli, reduce pulmonary vascular resistance and right ventricular afterload, and are rapidly inactivated so they do not lower systemic pressure. Intravenous vasodilators such as nitroprusside lower systemic pressure and impair gas exchange, which is dangerous with a pressure of 85/50. Phenylephrine tends to raise pulmonary vascular resistance.
  191. The correct answer is C (Anticoagulate and perform transesophageal echocardiography to exclude atrial thrombus before cardioversion.): Fontan atria have sluggish flow and a high incidence of thrombus, and arrhythmia lasting several days without anticoagulation carries a high embolic risk. Imaging for thrombus and anticoagulation before cardioversion are standard. Calcium channel blockers rarely terminate reentry and may precipitate hypotension in a failing single ventricle.
  192. The correct answer is A (A large randomized trial showed about one-third fewer ischemic strokes with occlusion added to anticoagulation): The LAAOS III trial randomized patients with atrial fibrillation and elevated stroke risk undergoing cardiac surgery to appendage occlusion or none, with anticoagulation continued in both arms. Occlusion reduced stroke or systemic embolism by roughly one-third. Anticoagulation was not stopped in that trial, so it does not support discontinuation, and postoperative atrial fibrillation rates did not differ between groups.
  193. The correct answer is C (Replace it with short, stiff tubing and remove the extra stopcocks): Natural frequency rises with shorter, wider and stiffer tubing and with fewer compliant components. Four sustained oscillations show an underdamped system with a low natural frequency, so shortening the line and removing stopcocks helps. Compliant tubing and air bubbles both lower the natural frequency, although a bubble also adds damping.
  194. The correct answer is C (Bypass grafting with an internal mammary graft to the left anterior descending, given lower mortality.): The FREEDOM trial and related analyses showed that in diabetic patients with multivessel disease, bypass surgery reduced death and myocardial infarction compared with drug-eluting stents, although it had a higher stroke rate. Guidelines favor surgery for such patients with acceptable risk. Medical therapy alone does not match the survival benefit of surgical revascularization.
  195. The correct answer is A (About 3.8 Wood units, the transpulmonary gradient divided by cardiac output.): Pulmonary vascular resistance equals mean pulmonary artery pressure minus wedge pressure, divided by cardiac output: (29 - 14) / 4.0 = 3.75 Wood units. Dividing the mean pressure by flow without subtracting the wedge gives 7.25. Using the diastolic pressure minus wedge, (22 - 14) / 4.0, gives 2.0 and is the diastolic pulmonary gradient, not resistance.
  196. The correct answer is B (Impella 5.5 placed through a surgically sewn graft on the right axillary artery): The larger Impella 5.5 is designed for axillary delivery through a graft, avoiding diseased iliofemoral vessels and allowing ambulation and longer support. The femoral Impella CP needs a 14 French sheath that these small, calcified arteries cannot accommodate. An intra-aortic balloon pump augments flow by only a few hundred milliliters per minute.
  197. The correct answer is C (Warm substrate enriched cardioplegia restores depleted ATP through aerobic metabolism in energy starved myocardium): In acute ischemia the myocardium has depleted energy stores and needs oxygen and substrate to restore them. Warm blood cardioplegia with aspartate and glutamate allows aerobic resuscitation, and a terminal warm reperfusion dose supports recovery before unclamping. Cold solution, not warm, lowers oxygen consumption, and warm cardioplegia does not promote contractility during clamping.
  198. The correct answer is C (Back pain persisting despite pressure and heart rate control, with ulcer enlargement on repeat imaging): Penetrating ulcers are managed medically when asymptomatic, but persistent pain, rapid growth, expanding hematoma, saccular aneurysm formation or rupture signs indicate repair, usually by TEVAR. A stable shallow incidental ulcer is followed with serial imaging. A thin incidental hematoma without symptoms or progression also does not meet the repair criteria.
  199. The correct answer is A (Large v waves also occur with septal rupture or a stiff atrium, so regurgitation is unconfirmed): A tall v wave signals rapid left atrial filling during ventricular systole, which occurs with mitral regurgitation but also with a ventricular septal defect and with a noncompliant left atrium. Echocardiography is required to separate the causes. Atrial contraction generates the a wave, not the v wave.
  200. The correct answer is C (Right radial artery, because the left radial will be harvested and bilateral pressures match closely): The planned left radial conduit should not be cannulated because catheter injury to the intima can compromise the graft. Matching bilateral pressures mean the right radial gives reliable values. Brachial cannulation carries a higher risk of nerve injury and distal ischemia and is unnecessary when an uninvolved radial artery is available.
